# LINUX NETWORKING: FROM ABSOLUTE ZERO

## Before we start

I will **never assume you know anything**. If I use a word you haven't seen, I define it immediately.

Every command is shown with example output.
Every number is explained.
Every concept has a real-world analogy.

## If I fail at this, report me to your mother.

# TOPIC 1: THE OSI MODEL & TCP/IP SUITE

## What is a "model"?

A model is just a **made-up way to organize things**.

Think of a recipe for baking a cake:

1. Get a bowl
2. Crack eggs into the bowl
3. Mix ingredients
4. Put in oven
5. Bake

You follow the steps **in order**. Each step has one job.

The OSI model is the same thing, but for **sending data between computers**.

## Why do we need this?

Imagine Computer A wants to send the word "Hello" to Computer B.

That sounds simple. But actually, it's SUPER complicated:

- How does the word "Hello" turn into something that can travel over a wire?
- How does Computer B know this message is for HER, not for Computer C standing next to her?
- What if the wire gets cut in the middle of sending?
- What if "Hello" is too big to send all at once?
- How does Computer B know she received the ENTIRE message?

The OSI model **splits** this nightmare into 7 smaller jobs. Each job is a "layer". Each layer talks only to the layers directly above and below it.

## The 7 layers (memorize this sentence)

**P**lease **D**o **N**ot **T**hrow **S**ausage **P**izza **A**way

The first letter of each word matches the first letter of each layer (Layer 7 down to Layer 1):

| Layer Number | Name             | What it does (simple explanation)       |
| ------------ | ---------------- | --------------------------------------- |
| 7            | **A**pplication  | The actual message ("Hello")            |
| 6            | **P**resentation | Translate languages, encrypt, compress  |
| 5            | **S**ession      | Start and end the conversation          |
| 4            | **T**ransport    | Break message into pieces, track pieces |
| 3            | **N**etwork      | Add "street address" (IP address)       |
| 2            | **D**ata Link    | Add "house number" (MAC address)        |
| 1            | **P**hysical     | Turn 1s and 0s into electrical signals  |

## Let's walk through a REAL example

You sit at your Linux computer and type this command:

```bash
ping 8.8.8.8
```

(8.8.8.8 is Google's public DNS server. It's a computer somewhere far away.)

Here is what happens, layer by layer:

**Layer 7 (Application)**: The `ping` program says "I need to send an ICMP echo request" (ICMP is just a message type that means "are you there?").

**Layer 4 (Transport)**: The system says "I'll use protocol number 1 for ICMP". (Notice: no port number. Ping doesn't use ports. Only TCP and UDP use ports.)

**Layer 3 (Network)**: The system adds a source IP (your computer's address, like `192.168.1.5`) and a destination IP (`8.8.8.8`). Now this is called a **PACKET**.

**Layer 2 (Data Link)**: The system asks "What is the MAC address of my router?" (MAC address is a hardware address burned into the router's network card). It wraps the packet inside a **FRAME** with the router's MAC address.

**Layer 1 (Physical)**: Your network card turns the 1s and 0s into electrical voltages (on an Ethernet cable) or radio waves (on WiFi) and sends them out.

## The TWO layers you actually need (for now)

As a Linux sysadmin, 90% of your work will involve **Layer 3** and **Layer 4**.

| Layer   | Name      | What you need to know                                    |
| ------- | --------- | -------------------------------------------------------- |
| Layer 3 | Network   | **IP addresses** (like `192.168.1.5` or `8.8.8.8`)       |
| Layer 4 | Transport | **Port numbers** (like port 22 for SSH, port 80 for web) |

**Layer 3 answers:** Which computer?
**Layer 4 answers:** Which program on that computer?

## TCP vs UDP (both are Layer 4)

These are two different ways to send data.

### TCP (Transmission Control Protocol)

Think of **registered mail with signature required**.

- You send a letter
- The receiver signs a receipt saying "I got it"
- If no receipt comes back, you send it again
- Letters arrive in order (letter 1, then letter 2, then letter 3)

**Used for:** Web browsing (HTTP), Email (SMTP), SSH, file downloads

**Reliable but slower.**

### UDP (User Datagram Protocol)

Think of **shouting across a crowded room**.

- You shout "Hello!"
- You have no idea if anyone heard you
- If someone missed it, it's gone forever
- No order guaranteed

**Used for:** Video streaming, online gaming, DNS lookups, VoIP phone calls

**Fast but unreliable.**

## Your first Linux command

If you have Linux (or a VM), run this:

```bash
ss -tulpn
```

Let me explain EVERY piece of this command:

- `ss` = socket statistics (shows network connections)
- `-t` = show TCP connections
- `-u` = show UDP connections
- `-l` = show only listening ports (programs waiting for connections)
- `-p` = show which program is using each connection
- `-n` = show numbers (don't try to convert ports to service names)

**Example output:**

```
Netid  State   Local Address:Port   Peer Address:Port   Process
tcp    LISTEN  0.0.0.0:22           0.0.0.0:*           users:(("sshd",pid=1234))
tcp    LISTEN  0.0.0.0:80           0.0.0.0:*           users:(("nginx",pid=5678))
udp    LISTEN  0.0.0.0:53           0.0.0.0:*           users:(("systemd-resolve",pid=901))
```

**What each column means:**

| Column             | Meaning                                   |
| ------------------ | ----------------------------------------- |
| Netid              | TCP or UDP                                |
| State              | LISTEN = waiting for incoming connections |
| Local Address:Port | This computer's IP and port               |
| Peer Address:Port  | Who is connected (0.0.0.0 means anyone)   |
| Process            | Which program is listening                |

**In the example above:**

- `sshd` (SSH server) is listening on port 22. Anyone can connect.
- `nginx` (web server) is listening on port 80. Anyone can connect.
- `systemd-resolve` (DNS resolver) is listening on port 53 for UDP.

# TOPIC 2: IP ADDRESSES AND SUBNETTING (COMPLETE DEEP DIVE)

## What is an IP address?

An IP address is a **number that identifies a computer on a network**.

That's it. Just a number. Like a phone number for your computer.

- Your phone number: (555) 123-4567
- Computer IP address: `192.168.1.100`

## Why do we write IP addresses with dots and four numbers?

Because the **real** IP address is a **32-bit number**.

A "bit" is a single digit that can be either `0` or `1`.

A computer sees an IP address as something like this:

```
11000000 10101000 00000001 01100100
```

That is 32 bits (digits) in a row. But no human wants to type or read that.

So we do this:

**Step 1:** Split the 32 bits into 4 groups of 8 bits each.

```
Group 1: 11000000
Group 2: 10101000
Group 3: 00000001
Group 4: 01100100
```

**Step 2:** Convert each group from binary to decimal.

| Binary   | Decimal | How to convert     |
| -------- | ------- | ------------------ |
| 11000000 | 192     | 128 + 64 = 192     |
| 10101000 | 168     | 128 + 32 + 8 = 168 |
| 00000001 | 1       | 1 = 1              |
| 01100100 | 100     | 64 + 32 + 4 = 100  |

**Step 3:** Put dots between the decimal numbers.

```
192.168.1.100
```

**Remember:** `192.168.1.100` is just a human-friendly way to write the 32-bit number `11000000101010000000000101100100`.

## What are the rules for IP addresses?

Since each group is 8 bits, the smallest possible value is `00000000` (which is 0) and the largest possible value is `11111111` (which is 255).

**Therefore:** Each of the four numbers in an IP address must be between **0 and 255**.

- `192.168.1.100` = VALID
- `10.0.0.1` = VALID
- `256.500.1.0` = INVALID (256 and 500 are too big)
- `192.168.1.500` = INVALID (500 is too big)

## The two parts of every IP address

Every IP address has TWO parts:

1. **Network part** = Which network? (like a street name)
2. **Host part** = Which computer on that network? (like a house number)

But here is the problem: **Where is the split?**

Look at `192.168.1.100`:

- Is the split after the first number? `192` = network, `168.1.100` = host?
- After the second number? `192.168` = network, `1.100` = host?
- After the third number? `192.168.1` = network, `100` = host?

The **subnet mask** tells you exactly where the split is.

## What is a subnet mask?

A subnet mask looks just like an IP address: four numbers between 0 and 255, separated by dots.

Examples:

- `255.255.255.0`
- `255.255.0.0`
- `255.0.0.0`
- `255.255.255.128`

## How does a subnet mask work?

Every position that has **255** in the subnet mask means **this part belongs to the NETWORK**.

Every position that has **0** in the subnet mask means **this part belongs to the HOST**.

**Example 1:** Subnet mask `255.255.255.0`

| Position     | Mask Value | Means                             |
| ------------ | ---------- | --------------------------------- |
| First octet  | 255        | This octet is part of the NETWORK |
| Second octet | 255        | This octet is part of the NETWORK |
| Third octet  | 255        | This octet is part of the NETWORK |
| Fourth octet | 0          | This octet is part of the HOST    |

So for IP address `192.168.1.100` with mask `255.255.255.0`:

- Network = `192.168.1` (the first three numbers)
- Host = `100` (the last number)

**Example 2:** Subnet mask `255.255.0.0`

| Position     | Mask Value | Means   |
| ------------ | ---------- | ------- |
| First octet  | 255        | NETWORK |
| Second octet | 255        | NETWORK |
| Third octet  | 0          | HOST    |
| Fourth octet | 0          | HOST    |

For IP address `192.168.1.100` with mask `255.255.0.0`:

- Network = `192.168` (the first two numbers)
- Host = `1.100` (the last two numbers)

**IMPORTANT:** The same IP address (`192.168.1.100`) can have a DIFFERENT network depending on which subnet mask you use!

## CIDR notation (the "/" thing)

CIDR (pronounced "cider" like the drink) is just a **shorter way to write the subnet mask**.

Instead of writing `255.255.255.0`, you write `/24`.

Instead of writing `255.255.0.0`, you write `/16`.

Instead of writing `255.0.0.0`, you write `/8`.

## Where does the number after the slash come from?

Remember: An IP address has **32 total bits**.

The number after the slash tells you **how many of those 32 bits are for the network**.

**Example:** `/24` means "the first 24 bits are network, the remaining 8 bits are host"

Let me show you the binary:

`255.255.255.0` in binary = `11111111 11111111 11111111 00000000`

Count the 1s: there are 24 of them. That's why it's `/24`.

`255.255.0.0` in binary = `11111111 11111111 00000000 00000000`

Count the 1s: there are 16 of them. That's `/16`.

`255.0.0.0` in binary = `11111111 00000000 00000000 00000000`

Count the 1s: there are 8 of them. That's `/8`.

## Common subnet masks (MEMORIZE THESE THREE)

| CIDR | Subnet Mask   | How many host bits? | How many total hosts? | Usable hosts |
| ---- | ------------- | ------------------- | --------------------- | ------------ |
| /24  | 255.255.255.0 | 8 bits (32-24=8)    | 256                   | 254          |
| /16  | 255.255.0.0   | 16 bits (32-16=16)  | 65,536                | 65,534       |
| /8   | 255.0.0.0     | 24 bits (32-8=24)   | 16,777,216            | 16,777,214   |

## Why "usable hosts" is two less than total hosts

For every network, two addresses are **reserved** and cannot be assigned to a computer:

| Reserved Address                      | What it is                       | Example (for /24 network) |
| ------------------------------------- | -------------------------------- | ------------------------- |
| Network address (all host bits = 0)   | Identifies the network itself    | `192.168.1.0`             |
| Broadcast address (all host bits = 1) | Send to ALL computers on network | `192.168.1.255`           |

**Example with /24:** Total 256 addresses (0-255). Subtract 2 = 254 usable hosts (1 through 254).

## Special IP ranges you MUST know

Some IP addresses are **private** - they are not allowed on the public internet. Your home router uses a private IP on the inside, then changes it to a public IP when you go online.

| Range                          | CIDR           | What it is                          | Example         |
| ------------------------------ | -------------- | ----------------------------------- | --------------- |
| 127.0.0.0 to 127.255.255.255   | 127.0.0.0/8    | localhost (this computer only)      | `127.0.0.1`     |
| 10.0.0.0 to 10.255.255.255     | 10.0.0.0/8     | private network                     | `10.0.0.5`      |
| 172.16.0.0 to 172.31.255.255   | 172.16.0.0/12  | private network                     | `172.16.1.10`   |
| 192.168.0.0 to 192.168.255.255 | 192.168.0.0/16 | private network (most home routers) | `192.168.1.1`   |
| 169.254.0.0 to 169.254.255.255 | 169.254.0.0/16 | link-local (when DHCP fails)        | `169.254.12.34` |

**What does "private" mean?** If you have a private IP, you cannot directly reach the internet. Your router must "translate" it to a public IP (this is called NAT - Network Address Translation).

**What is localhost?** `127.0.0.1` is a special address that always means "this computer I am sitting at". If you `ping 127.0.0.1`, you are pinging yourself.

## Complete subnet calculation examples

### Example 1: Easy (/24)

**Given:** IP `10.15.30.42/24`

**Step 1:** `/24` = subnet mask `255.255.255.0`

**Step 2:** Network = first three octets = `10.15.30`

**Step 3:** Network address (all host bits 0) = `10.15.30.0`

**Step 4:** Broadcast address (all host bits 1) = `10.15.30.255`

**Step 5:** Valid host range = `10.15.30.1` through `10.15.30.254`

### Example 2: Easy (/16)

**Given:** IP `172.25.100.200/16`

**Step 1:** `/16` = subnet mask `255.255.0.0`

**Step 2:** Network = first two octets = `172.25`

**Step 3:** Network address = `172.25.0.0`

**Step 4:** Broadcast address = `172.25.255.255`

**Step 5:** Valid host range = `172.25.0.1` through `172.25.255.254`

### Example 3: Hard (/20 - does NOT fall on octet boundary)

**Given:** IP `10.50.100.200/20`

**Step 1:** How many bits for network? 20 bits.

**Step 2:** First 8 bits = first octet = `10` (network)
**Step 3:** Next 8 bits = second octet = `50` (network)
**Step 4:** That's 16 bits so far. We need 4 MORE bits for network (16 + 4 = 20)
**Step 5:** Those 4 bits come from the FIRST 4 bits of the third octet.

**Step 6:** Third octet is `100`. In binary: `01100100`

- First 4 bits: `0110` (these are NETWORK)
- Last 4 bits: `0100` (these are HOST)

**Step 7:** Subnet mask:

- First octet: 8 bits of 1 = `11111111` = 255
- Second octet: 8 bits of 1 = `11111111` = 255
- Third octet: first 4 bits = 1, last 4 bits = 0 = `11110000` = 240
- Fourth octet: all 0 = `00000000` = 0
- **Subnet mask = 255.255.240.0**

**Step 8:** Network address (set ALL host bits to 0):

- First octet: `10` (unchanged)
- Second octet: `50` (unchanged)
- Third octet: `01100100`, set last 4 bits to 0 = `01100000` = 96
- Fourth octet: all 0 = 0
- **Network address = 10.50.96.0**

**Step 9:** Broadcast address (set ALL host bits to 1):

- First octet: `10`
- Second octet: `50`
- Third octet: `01100100`, set last 4 bits to 1 = `01101111` = 111
- Fourth octet: all 1 = 255
- **Broadcast address = 10.50.111.255**

**Step 10:** Valid host range = `10.50.96.1` through `10.50.111.254`

### Example 4: Very hard (/26)

**Given:** IP `192.168.50.100/26`

**Step 1:** `/26` means 26 bits for network.

**Step 2:** First 24 bits = three full octets = `192.168.50` (network)
**Step 3:** We need 2 MORE bits (24 + 2 = 26). These come from the first 2 bits of the fourth octet.

**Step 4:** Fourth octet is `100`. In binary: `01100100`

- First 2 bits: `01` (these are NETWORK)
- Last 6 bits: `100100` (these are HOST)

**Step 5:** Subnet mask:

- First octet: 255
- Second octet: 255
- Third octet: 255
- Fourth octet: first 2 bits = 1, last 6 bits = 0 = `11000000` = 192
- **Subnet mask = 255.255.255.192**

**Step 6:** Network address:

- First three octets: `192.168.50`
- Fourth octet: `01100100`, set last 6 bits to 0 = `01000000` = 64
- **Network address = 192.168.50.64**

**Step 7:** Broadcast address:

- Fourth octet: `01100100`, set last 6 bits to 1 = `01111111` = 127
- **Broadcast address = 192.168.50.127**

**Step 8:** Valid host range = `192.168.50.65` through `192.168.50.126`

## Quick reference table (SAVE THIS)

| CIDR | Subnet Mask     | Usable Hosts |
| ---- | --------------- | ------------ |
| /8   | 255.0.0.0       | 16,777,214   |
| /16  | 255.255.0.0     | 65,534       |
| /24  | 255.255.255.0   | 254          |
| /25  | 255.255.255.128 | 126          |
| /26  | 255.255.255.192 | 62           |
| /27  | 255.255.255.224 | 30           |
| /28  | 255.255.255.240 | 14           |
| /29  | 255.255.255.248 | 6            |
| /30  | 255.255.255.252 | 2            |
| /32  | 255.255.255.255 | 1            |

## Your first practice exercise (do on paper)

**Given:** IP `10.200.50.75/12`

Find:

1. Subnet mask
2. Network address
3. Broadcast address
4. Valid host range

**Answer (don't peek until you try):**

1. Subnet mask: `/12` = first 8 bits (first octet) + 4 bits from second octet. 4 bits = `11110000` = 240. Mask = `255.240.0.0`

2. Network address:
   - First octet: `10`
   - Second octet `200` = `11001000`, first 4 bits = `1100`, set last 4 bits to 0 = `11000000` = 192
   - Third octet: 0
   - Fourth octet: 0
   - Network = `10.192.0.0`

3. Broadcast address:
   - First octet: `10`
   - Second octet: `11001000`, set last 4 bits to 1 = `11001111` = 207
   - Third octet: 255
   - Fourth octet: 255
   - Broadcast = `10.207.255.255`

4. Host range = `10.192.0.1` through `10.207.255.254`

## Why this matters in real life

You are configuring a Linux server. You run:

```bash
ip addr add 10.0.0.5/24 dev eth0
```

Linux sees `/24` and automatically:

- Sets subnet mask to `255.255.255.0`
- Calculates network = `10.0.0.0`
- Calculates broadcast = `10.0.0.255`
- Knows: "I can talk directly to any IP from `10.0.0.1` to `10.0.0.254` without a router"

**The most common mistake:**

Computer A: `10.0.0.5/24` (mask 255.255.255.0)
Computer B: `10.0.1.10/24` (mask 255.255.255.0)

These two computers CANNOT talk directly!

Why?

- Computer A thinks: "My network is `10.0.0.x`. `10.0.1.10` is NOT my network."
- Computer B thinks: "My network is `10.0.1.x`. `10.0.0.5` is NOT my network."

They need a router to talk to each other.

**Fix:** Put them on the same network.

- Option 1: Both `/24` on `10.0.0.x` (A=`10.0.0.5`, B=`10.0.0.10`)
- Option 2: Both `/16` (A=`10.0.0.5/16`, B=`10.0.1.10/16`)

# TOPIC 3: LINUX NETWORK INTERFACE BASICS

## What is a network interface?

A network interface is a **door** from your computer to the network.

Your computer can have multiple doors:

- `eth0` = first Ethernet door (wired, usually the port on the back)
- `eth1` = second Ethernet door (if you have two ports)
- `wlan0` = WiFi door
- `lo` = "loopback" door (a special door that goes right back into the same computer)

## Why does `lo` (loopback) exist?

The loopback interface is a **fake door** that doesn't connect to any real network.

When you send data to `127.0.0.1` (or `localhost`), it goes out the `lo` door and immediately comes right back into the same computer.

**Why is this useful?**

- You can test network programs without a real network
- You can run a web server and connect to it from the same computer
- Many internal Linux services use `lo` to talk to each other

## The three most important commands

### Command 1: `ip link` - list all doors (interfaces)

```bash
ip link show
```

**Example output:**

```
1: lo: <LOOPBACK,UP,LOWER_UP> mtu 65536 qdisc noqueue state UNKNOWN mode DEFAULT group default qlen 1000
2: eth0: <BROADCAST,MULTICAST,UP,LOWER_UP> mtu 1500 qdisc fq_codel state UP mode DEFAULT group default qlen 1000
3: wlan0: <BROADCAST,MULTICAST> mtu 1500 qdisc noop state DOWN mode DEFAULT group default qlen 1000
```

**Let me explain EVERY piece:**

| What you see | What it means                                             |
| ------------ | --------------------------------------------------------- |
| `lo`         | The loopback door                                         |
| `eth0`       | First Ethernet door                                       |
| `wlan0`      | WiFi door                                                 |
| `UP`         | The door is OPEN (interface is working)                   |
| `DOWN`       | The door is CLOSED (interface is disabled)                |
| `LOWER_UP`   | The cable is plugged in (physical connection exists)      |
| `mtu 1500`   | Maximum size of a single packet (1500 bytes for Ethernet) |

**In the example above:**

- `lo` is UP (always)
- `eth0` is UP and has a cable plugged in
- `wlan0` is DOWN (disabled or not connected)

### Command 2: `ip addr` - what addresses are assigned to each door

```bash
ip addr show
```

**Example output:**

```
1: lo: <LOOPBACK,UP,LOWER_UP> ...
    inet 127.0.0.1/8 scope host lo
    inet6 ::1/128 scope host

2: eth0: <BROADCAST,MULTICAST,UP,LOWER_UP> ...
    inet 192.168.1.100/24 brd 192.168.1.255 scope global eth0
    inet6 fe80::1234:5678:9abc:def0/64 scope link
```

**Let me explain EVERY piece:**

| What you see        | What it means                                             |
| ------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------- |
| `inet`              | IPv4 address (there is also `inet6` for IPv6)             |
| `192.168.1.100/24`  | The IP address AND the CIDR (so you know the subnet mask) |
| `brd 192.168.1.255` | The broadcast address for this network                    |
| `scope global`      | This IP is valid on the real network (not just local)     |
| `scope host`        | This IP is valid only on this computer (like loopback)    |
| `scope link`        | This IP is valid only on this specific link (no routing)  |

**What this tells you:**

- Door `eth0` has IP `192.168.1.100`
- The subnet mask is `/24` (which is `255.255.255.0`)
- The broadcast address is `192.168.1.255`
- This IP is usable on the real network (`scope global`)

### Command 3: `ip route` - which door to use for which destination

```bash
ip route show
```

**Example output:**

```
default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0 proto dhcp src 192.168.1.100 metric 100
192.168.1.0/24 dev eth0 proto kernel scope link src 192.168.1.100 metric 100
```

**Let me explain EVERY piece:**

| What you see      | What it means                                         |
| ----------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- |
| `default`         | The "if you don't know where to send it" rule         |
| `via 192.168.1.1` | Send it to this IP (your router)                      |
| `dev eth0`        | Send it out this door                                 |
| `192.168.1.0/24`  | Any IP that starts with 192.168.1                     |
| `scope link`      | This network is directly connected (no router needed) |

**Translation of the routing table:**

1. If you want to reach `192.168.1.x` (any computer on your same network), use door `eth0` directly. No router needed.

2. For EVERYTHING ELSE (the "default" rule), send it to `192.168.1.1` (your router) through door `eth0`.

## How Linux uses the routing table

You type: `ping 192.168.1.50`

Linux looks at the routing table:

- `192.168.1.50` matches `192.168.1.0/24`
- Rule says: use `dev eth0` directly
- Linux sends the packet out `eth0`

You type: `ping 8.8.8.8`

Linux looks at the routing table:

- `8.8.8.8` does NOT match `192.168.1.0/24`
- So it matches the `default` rule
- Rule says: send to `192.168.1.1` (router) via `eth0`
- Linux sends the packet to the router, and the router forwards it to the internet

## How to open and close doors (bring interfaces up and down)

You must be `root` or use `sudo` to do this.

```bash
# Close the door (disable the interface)
sudo ip link set eth0 down

# Open the door (enable the interface)
sudo ip link set eth0 up
```

**What happens when you close a door (`ip link set eth0 down`):**

- The IP address is still assigned (Linux remembers it)
- But the door is closed, so no data can go in or out
- `ping` stops working
- SSH connections freeze
- The cable can still be plugged in, but Linux ignores it completely

**What happens when you open the door (`ip link set eth0 up`):**

- The door starts working again
- The IP address that was assigned is now usable again
- Network programs resume working

## How to assign IP addresses manually

```bash
# Add an IP address to a door
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.5/24 dev eth0

# Verify it worked
ip addr show eth0

# Remove that IP address
sudo ip addr del 10.0.0.5/24 dev eth0
```

**IMPORTANT:** When you add an IP with `/24`, Linux automatically:

- Sets the subnet mask to `255.255.255.0`
- Calculates the network address (`10.0.0.0`)
- Calculates the broadcast address (`10.0.0.255`)

You do NOT have to set these separately.

## Your first hands-on lab

**WARNING:** Do NOT do this on a remote server that you connected to via SSH. You will lose connection and lock yourself out. Only do this on a physical Linux computer or a local virtual machine.

```bash
# Step 1: See your current IP address (write it down!)
ip addr show eth0

# Example output: inet 192.168.1.100/24 ...

# Step 2: Add a SECOND IP address to the same interface
sudo ip addr add 192.168.1.200/24 dev eth0

# Step 3: Verify both IP addresses exist
ip addr show eth0

# You should now see TWO "inet" lines:
# inet 192.168.1.100/24 ...
# inet 192.168.1.200/24 ...

# Step 4: Ping your new IP address (from the same computer)
ping 192.168.1.200

# This works! The packet goes out and comes right back.
# Press Ctrl+C to stop ping.

# Step 5: Remove the extra IP address
sudo ip addr del 192.168.1.200/24 dev eth0

# Step 6: Verify it's gone
ip addr show eth0
# Now only the original IP remains
```

**What just happened?** One physical door (`eth0`) can have multiple IP addresses. This is called "IP aliasing". It's useful when you want to host multiple websites on the same server, each with its own IP address.

## Common mistakes and what happens

**Mistake 1:** Forgetting `sudo`

```bash
ip link set eth0 down
# Output: RTNETLINK answers: Operation not permitted
```

**Fix:** Use `sudo` in front.

**Mistake 2:** Typing the wrong interface name

```bash
sudo ip link set eth1 down
# Output: Cannot find device "eth1"
```

**Fix:** Use `ip link show` to see the actual interface names on YOUR system.

**Mistake 3:** Adding an IP with the wrong CIDR

```bash
sudo ip addr add 192.168.1.200/16 dev eth0
```

This will work, but now your computer thinks:

- Network = `192.168.0.0`
- Any IP from `192.168.0.1` to `192.168.255.254` is on the same network
- This might be wrong if your router expects `/24`

**Fix:** Always know your network's correct subnet mask.

---

# SUMMARY OF ALL THREE TOPICS

| Topic           | One-sentence takeaway                                               | Key commands                     |
| --------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------- |
| OSI Model       | 7 layers; you need Layer 3 (IP) and Layer 4 (ports)                 | `ss -tulpn`                      |
| IP & Subnetting | IP = phone number; subnet mask = where network ends and host begins | `ip addr show`                   |
| Interfaces      | Doors on your computer; each door has an IP and a routing table     | `ip link`, `ip addr`, `ip route` |

# TOPIC 4: BASIC NETWORK TOOLS - ping, traceroute, and ss

## Before we start

I will **never assume you know anything**. Every command, every flag, every piece of output is explained like you've never seen a terminal before.

If I fail, report me to your mother.

---

## PART 1: `ping` - The "Are you there?" tool

### What is ping?

`ping` is the simplest network tool. It answers one question: **"Is that other computer alive and responding?"**

Think of ping like **knocking on a door**:

- You knock ("ping")
- Someone inside yells back ("pong")
- You know someone is home

### How ping works (quick review from Topic 1)

When you type `ping 8.8.8.8`:

1. Your computer creates an **ICMP Echo Request** packet (ICMP is a protocol like TCP/UDP, but simpler)
2. Your computer sends it to the destination IP
3. The destination computer receives it and sends back an **ICMP Echo Reply**
4. Your computer measures how long the round trip took

**Important:** ping does NOT use ports (not TCP, not UDP). It uses ICMP protocol number 1. Some firewalls block ICMP, so no reply doesn't always mean the computer is down.

### The most basic ping

Open a terminal and type:

```bash
ping 8.8.8.8
```

**Example output:**

```
PING 8.8.8.8 (8.8.8.8) 56(84) bytes of data.
64 bytes from 8.8.8.8: icmp_seq=1 ttl=117 time=12.3 ms
64 bytes from 8.8.8.8: icmp_seq=2 ttl=117 time=11.8 ms
64 bytes from 8.8.8.8: icmp_seq=3 ttl=117 time=12.1 ms
64 bytes from 8.8.8.8: icmp_seq=4 ttl=117 time=11.9 ms
^C
--- 8.8.8.8 ping statistics ---
4 packets transmitted, 4 received, 0% packet loss, time 3003ms
rtt min/avg/max/mdev = 11.8/12.025/12.3/0.201 ms
```

**Let me explain EVERY piece of this output:**

| Output                   | What it means                                                                                   |
| ------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `PING 8.8.8.8 (8.8.8.8)` | We are pinging the IP address 8.8.8.8                                                           |
| `56(84) bytes of data`   | 56 bytes of actual data + 28 bytes of header = 84 bytes total on the wire                       |
| `64 bytes from 8.8.8.8`  | We received a reply from 8.8.8.8 that is 64 bytes                                               |
| `icmp_seq=1`             | Sequence number (1st ping, 2nd ping, etc.)                                                      |
| `ttl=117`                | Time To Live (how many routers this packet can cross before dying - starts at 255, counts down) |
| `time=12.3 ms`           | Round trip took 12.3 milliseconds                                                               |
| `^C`                     | I pressed Ctrl+C to stop ping                                                                   |
| `4 packets transmitted`  | We sent 4 pings                                                                                 |
| `4 received`             | We got 4 replies back                                                                           |
| `0% packet loss`         | Every ping worked (0% lost)                                                                     |
| `rtt min/avg/max/mdev`   | Minimum/Average/Maximum/Standard deviation of the times                                         |

### What does TTL (Time To Live) tell you?

TTL starts at some number (usually 64, 128, or 255 depending on the operating system) and **decreases by 1 every time it passes through a router**.

When TTL reaches 0, the packet is discarded.

**Example:** `ttl=117` in the output means:

- Starting TTL was probably 128 (common for Linux/Windows)
- 128 - 117 = 11 hops (routers) between you and 8.8.8.8

**Common starting TTL values:**

- Windows: 128
- Linux: 64
- Some network gear: 255

### Making ping stop automatically

By default, ping runs FOREVER until you press Ctrl+C.

**To stop after a specific number of pings:**

```bash
ping -c 5 8.8.8.8
```

`-c 5` means "count = 5 pings, then stop"

**Output:**

```
PING 8.8.8.8 (8.8.8.8) 56(84) bytes of data.
64 bytes from 8.8.8.8: icmp_seq=1 ttl=117 time=12.3 ms
64 bytes from 8.8.8.8: icmp_seq=2 ttl=117 time=11.8 ms
64 bytes from 8.8.8.8: icmp_seq=3 ttl=117 time=12.1 ms
64 bytes from 8.8.8.8: icmp_seq=4 ttl=117 time=11.9 ms
64 bytes from 8.8.8.8: icmp_seq=5 ttl=117 time=12.0 ms

--- 8.8.8.8 ping statistics ---
5 packets transmitted, 5 received, 0% packet loss, time 4004ms
rtt min/avg/max/mdev = 11.8/12.02/12.3/0.201 ms
```

Notice: It stopped by itself after 5 pings. No Ctrl+C needed.

### Pinging by hostname (using DNS)

Instead of an IP address, you can ping a name:

```bash
ping google.com
```

**What happens behind the scenes:**

1. Linux needs to send a packet to `google.com`
2. But Linux only understands IP addresses (numbers)
3. Linux asks DNS: "What is the IP address of google.com?"
4. DNS replies: "172.217.168.46"
5. Linux pings 172.217.168.46

**Example output:**

```
PING google.com (172.217.168.46) 56(84) bytes of data.
64 bytes from 172.217.168.46: icmp_seq=1 ttl=117 time=12.3 ms
```

Notice the output shows `google.com (172.217.168.46)` - both the name AND the resolved IP.

### What to do when ping fails

**Scenario 1: No reply at all**

```bash
ping 192.168.1.200
# No output, or "Destination Host Unreachable"
```

**Possible reasons:**

- The other computer is turned off
- The other computer has a firewall blocking ICMP
- Your network cable is unplugged
- You are not on the same network and have no default route

**Scenario 2: "Network is unreachable"**

```bash
ping 8.8.8.8
# Output: connect: Network is unreachable
```

**What this means:** Linux doesn't have a route to that network. Check your routing table with `ip route show`. Your default gateway is probably missing.

**Scenario 3: "Unknown host"**

```bash
ping www.asdfkhsdfkjsdf.com
# Output: ping: www.asdfkhsdfkjsdf.com: Name or service not known
```

**What this means:** DNS could not resolve that name. Either you typed it wrong, or your DNS server is down.

### Useful ping options for sysadmins

| Option | What it does                             | Example                                           |
| ------ | ---------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------- |
| `-c N` | Stop after N pings                       | `ping -c 10 google.com`                           |
| `-i N` | Wait N seconds between pings (default 1) | `ping -i 0.2 google.com` (5 pings per second)     |
| `-s N` | Send N bytes of data (default 56)        | `ping -s 1400 google.com` (test large packets)    |
| `-W N` | Timeout after N seconds                  | `ping -W 2 google.com` (wait 2 seconds for reply) |

---

## PART 2: `traceroute` - The "Which path did you take?" tool

### What is traceroute?

`ping` tells you IF a computer is reachable.
`traceroute` tells you **HOW** (which routers your packets go through).

Think of it like:

- Ping = "Did my letter arrive?"
- Traceroute = "Which post offices did my letter visit along the way?"

### How traceroute works (the clever trick)

Traceroute abuses the TTL (Time To Live) field:

1. First packet: Set TTL = 1
   - First router receives it, decreases TTL to 0
   - Router says "TTL expired, I must discard this" and sends back a message
   - Traceroute records that router's IP address

2. Second packet: Set TTL = 2
   - First router decreases to 1, passes to second router
   - Second router decreases to 0, discards it
   - Second router sends back the "TTL expired" message
   - Traceroute records the second router's IP

3. Keep increasing TTL until you reach the destination

The destination computer (when TTL is high enough) sends back a different message: "Port unreachable" (because traceroute uses a fake high port number that no program is listening on).

### The most basic traceroute

```bash
traceroute google.com
```

**Example output:**

```
traceroute to google.com (172.217.168.46), 30 hops max, 60 byte packets
 1  _gateway (192.168.1.1)  2.123 ms  1.987 ms  1.854 ms
 2  10.0.0.1 (10.0.0.1)  5.432 ms  5.123 ms  5.001 ms
 3  core-router.isp.net (203.45.67.89)  12.345 ms  12.234 ms  12.123 ms
 4  * * *
 5  edge-router.isp.net (203.45.67.100)  15.678 ms  15.567 ms  15.456 ms
 6  172.217.168.46 (172.217.168.46)  12.345 ms  12.234 ms  12.123 ms
```

**Let me explain EVERY piece:**

| Output                       | What it means                                                           |
| ---------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `traceroute to google.com`   | The destination                                                         |
| `30 hops max`                | Will try up to 30 routers (default)                                     |
| `60 byte packets`            | Size of each probe                                                      |
| `1`                          | First hop (first router)                                                |
| `_gateway (192.168.1.1)`     | The router's name and IP (usually your home router)                     |
| `2.123 ms 1.987 ms 1.854 ms` | Three separate probes to this hop (each is a round trip time)           |
| `3`                          | Third hop                                                               |
| `4  * * *`                   | No reply from hop 4 (router is configured to not respond to traceroute) |
| `6 172.217.168.46`           | Final destination reached                                               |

### What the asterisks mean (`* * *`)

When you see `* * *`, it means:

- Traceroute sent a probe
- No reply came back within the timeout
- This router is configured to **not send TTL expired messages** (common for security)

The traceroute continues anyway. The asterisks just mean "I got no reply from this hop, but I'm moving on."

### traceroute vs ping for troubleshooting

**If both ping and traceroute work:** Network is fine.

**If ping works but traceroute shows asterisks:** Your path is fine, but some routers are configured to not respond to traceroute. Nothing to fix.

**If ping fails:** Run traceroute to see WHERE the problem is:

```bash
traceroute 8.8.8.8
```

If traceroute shows:

```
1  192.168.1.1  2.123 ms  1.987 ms  1.854 ms
2  * * *
3  * * *
4  * * *
```

The problem is at hop 2 (your ISP's first router). You can't fix that - call your ISP.

### Installing traceroute (if not installed)

Some Linux distributions don't include traceroute by default.

**Debian/Ubuntu:**

```bash
sudo apt update
sudo apt install traceroute
```

**RHEL/CentOS/Fedora:**

```bash
sudo dnf install traceroute
```

### Alternative: `tracepath` (often pre-installed)

If you can't install traceroute, use `tracepath`:

```bash
tracepath google.com
```

`tracepath` does the same thing but doesn't require root privileges and shows MTU (Maximum Transmission Unit) along the path.

---

## PART 3: `ss` - The "What connections are happening RIGHT NOW?" tool

### What is ss?

`ss` stands for **Socket Statistics**. It shows you:

- What programs are listening for connections (servers)
- What connections are currently established
- What ports are open

Think of `ss` as a **window into your computer's front door**:

- Who is waiting for visitors? (LISTENING)
- Who is currently visiting? (ESTABLISHED)
- What door number (port) are they using?

### Why not use `netstat`?

`netstat` is the old tool. `ss` is the new, faster, better tool. Both show similar information, but `ss` is what modern Linux uses. Learn `ss`.

### The most useful ss command (memorize this)

```bash
ss -tulpn
```

**Let me explain each flag again (from Topic 1, but repeated here so you don't have to scroll up):**

| Flag | Meaning                      | Why you want it                               |
| ---- | ---------------------------- | --------------------------------------------- |
| `-t` | TCP only                     | Most services use TCP                         |
| `-u` | UDP only                     | DNS, DHCP use UDP                             |
| `-l` | Listening only               | Shows servers waiting for connections         |
| `-p` | Show process name            | Tells you WHICH program is using the port     |
| `-n` | Numeric (no name resolution) | Shows port 22, not "ssh" - faster and clearer |

### Example ss -tulpn output

```bash
$ sudo ss -tulpn
```

**Note:** `sudo` is needed to see process names (`-p` flag). Without sudo, you see the ports but not which program owns them.

**Example output:**

```
Netid  State   Local Address:Port   Peer Address:Port   Process
tcp    LISTEN  0.0.0.0:22           0.0.0.0:*           users:(("sshd",pid=1234,fd=3))
tcp    LISTEN  0.0.0.0:80           0.0.0.0:*           users:(("nginx",pid=5678,fd=4))
tcp    LISTEN  0.0.0.0:443          0.0.0.0:*           users:(("nginx",pid=5678,fd=5))
tcp    LISTEN  127.0.0.1:3306       0.0.0.0:*           users:(("mysqld",pid=9012,fd=14))
udp    LISTEN  0.0.0.0:53           0.0.0.0:*           users:(("systemd-resolve",pid=456,fd=6))
udp    LISTEN  0.0.0.0:68           0.0.0.0:*           users:(("dhclient",pid=789,fd=5))
```

**Let me explain EVERY piece:**

| Column             | Value               | What it means                                                     |
| ------------------ | ------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Netid              | `tcp` or `udp`      | The protocol                                                      |
| State              | `LISTEN`            | This program is waiting for incoming connections                  |
| Local Address:Port | `0.0.0.0:22`        | Listening on ALL IP addresses on port 22                          |
| Local Address:Port | `127.0.0.1:3306`    | Listening ONLY on localhost (not accessible from other computers) |
| Peer Address:Port  | `0.0.0.0:*`         | Any computer can connect (for LISTEN state)                       |
| Process            | `("sshd",pid=1234)` | Program name is sshd, process ID is 1234                          |

### Understanding the Local Address

| Local Address        | What it means                                                                                                       |
| -------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `0.0.0.0:22`         | Listen on EVERY network interface (all doors). Anyone on any network can connect (if firewall allows)               |
| `127.0.0.1:3306`     | Listen ONLY on localhost. Only programs on the SAME computer can connect. Other computers cannot reach this service |
| `192.168.1.100:8080` | Listen ONLY on that specific IP address (rare, but possible)                                                        |

**Why would you listen only on 127.0.0.1?**

- Security: MySQL (port 3306) should only be accessed by local programs, not remote attackers
- Internal communication: Two programs on the same computer talking to each other

### Showing established connections (not just listening)

Remove the `-l` flag to see ACTIVE connections:

```bash
ss -tupn
```

**Example output:**

```
Netid  State   Local Address:Port        Peer Address:Port        Process
tcp    ESTAB   192.168.1.100:45678       172.217.168.46:443       users:(("chrome",pid=2345))
tcp    ESTAB   192.168.1.100:22          203.45.67.89:54321        users:(("sshd",pid=3456,fd=4))
tcp    ESTAB   192.168.1.100:12345       192.168.1.50:3306         users:(("mysql",pid=4567))
```

**What this tells you:**

| Line   | Meaning                                                                                 |
| ------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Line 1 | Your Chrome browser (port 45678) is connected to Google's web server (port 443 = HTTPS) |
| Line 2 | Someone from IP 203.45.67.89 is SSH'd into your computer (port 22)                      |
| Line 3 | A program on your computer is connected to a MySQL database at 192.168.1.50             |

### Showing all connections (including non-TCP/UDP)

```bash
ss -a
```

This shows ALL sockets (LISTEN, ESTABLISHED, CLOSE-WAIT, etc.). You usually don't need this. `-tulpn` is what you will use 95% of the time.

### Common ss filters

**Show only SSH-related connections:**

```bash
ss -tunp | grep :22
```

**Show only connections to a specific IP:**

```bash
ss -tunp | grep "192.168.1.50"
```

**Show listening TCP ports only:**

```bash
ss -tln
```

**Show listening UDP ports only:**

```bash
ss -uln
```

### What the different states mean

| State         | Meaning                                                      | Example                                 |
| ------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------- |
| `LISTEN`      | Waiting for incoming connections                             | Web server waiting for visitors         |
| `ESTABLISHED` | Active connection                                            | You are currently downloading a file    |
| `CLOSE_WAIT`  | Remote side closed, local side hasn't closed yet             | Happens briefly when connections end    |
| `TIME_WAIT`   | Connection closed, but waiting to ensure all packets arrived | Normal, will disappear in a few seconds |
| `SYN_SENT`    | Trying to establish a connection                             | Client trying to connect to a server    |

You don't need to memorize all states. `LISTEN` and `ESTABLISHED` are the two you will see most often.

---

## PART 4: PUTTING IT ALL TOGETHER - A Real Troubleshooting Scenario

### Scenario: You can't connect to a web server

You type in your browser: `http://192.168.1.100`

The page doesn't load. Let's troubleshoot step by step.

### Step 1: Is the computer alive?

```bash
ping 192.168.1.100
```

**If ping succeeds:**

```
64 bytes from 192.168.1.100: icmp_seq=1 ttl=64 time=0.5 ms
```

The computer is alive and on the network.

**If ping fails:** The computer is off, or not on the network, or has a firewall blocking ICMP. Check cables and power.

### Step 2: Is the web server program running?

On the server (192.168.1.100), run:

```bash
sudo ss -tulpn | grep :80
```

**If the web server is running:**

```
tcp    LISTEN  0.0.0.0:80   0.0.0.0:*   users:(("nginx",pid=1234))
```

The web server (nginx) is listening on port 80.

**If nothing shows:** The web server is not running. Start it:

```bash
sudo systemctl start nginx
# or
sudo systemctl start apache2
```

### Step 3: Is the web server listening on the right address?

Check the Local Address column:

```
tcp    LISTEN  127.0.0.1:80 ...
```

**Problem:** It's listening only on localhost (`127.0.0.1`), so other computers cannot connect.

**Fix:** Change the web server configuration to listen on `0.0.0.0:80` (all interfaces).

### Step 4: Is the firewall blocking the port?

On the server, check firewall rules:

```bash
sudo iptables -L -n
```

Look for rules that might block port 80.

**Temporary test:** Disable firewall briefly:

```bash
sudo iptables -P INPUT ACCEPT
sudo iptables -P FORWARD ACCEPT
sudo iptables -P OUTPUT ACCEPT
sudo iptables -F
```

**If the web page loads after this:** The firewall was the problem. Create a permanent rule to allow port 80.

### Step 5: Is your routing correct?

From the client computer, check if you can reach the server's network:

```bash
traceroute 192.168.1.100
```

If traceroute stops at a certain hop, the problem is between that hop and the next.

---

## SUMMARY OF TOPIC 4

| Tool         | What it does                                | Most useful command     |
| ------------ | ------------------------------------------- | ----------------------- |
| `ping`       | "Is the computer alive?"                    | `ping -c 4 google.com`  |
| `traceroute` | "What path do packets take?"                | `traceroute google.com` |
| `ss`         | "What ports are open and who is connected?" | `sudo ss -tulpn`        |

### Quick reference - when to use each tool

| Question                                   | Tool to use                                        |
| ------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------- |
| Is the remote computer on?                 | `ping`                                             |
| How long does it take to reach it?         | `ping`                                             |
| Where is the connection failing?           | `traceroute`                                       |
| Is my web server program running?          | `ss -tulpn \| grep :80`                            |
| What program is using port 22?             | `sudo ss -tulpn \| grep :22`                       |
| Who is currently connected to my computer? | `ss -tupn`                                         |
| Why can't I connect to port 3306?          | Check if it's listening: `ss -tulpn \| grep :3306` |

---

## YOUR HOMEWORK (do this to learn)

Run these commands on YOUR Linux computer (physical or VM):

```bash
# 1. Ping your router
ping -c 3 192.168.1.1

# 2. Ping google by name
ping -c 3 google.com

# 3. Trace the path to google
traceroute google.com

# 4. See what's listening on your computer
sudo ss -tulpn

# 5. Check if SSH is running (port 22)
sudo ss -tulpn | grep :22

# 6. See who is connected to your computer right now
ss -tupn
```

Write down the output. If you see something you don't understand, write down the question.

# TOPIC 5: ARP & NEIGHBOR DISCOVERY

## Before we start

I will **never assume you know anything**. Every concept is explained from absolute zero.

Your mother is watching. I will not fail.

---

## PART 1: WHY DO WE NEED ARP?

### The problem ARP solves

You have two computers on the same network:

- Computer A: IP address `192.168.1.10`
- Computer B: IP address `192.168.1.20`

Computer A wants to send data to Computer B.

Computer A knows Computer B's **IP address** (`192.168.1.20`).

But here's the problem: **Network cables and WiFi don't understand IP addresses.**

When data travels over Ethernet (the physical wire or WiFi), it uses **MAC addresses** - not IP addresses.

### What is a MAC address?

**MAC** stands for **Media Access Control**.

A MAC address is a **hardware address** burned into every network card at the factory. It never changes.

**What a MAC address looks like:**

```
00:1A:2B:3C:4D:5E
```

**Format:** 6 pairs of hexadecimal digits (0-9 and A-F), separated by colons or dashes.

Each pair is 8 bits (one byte). Total: 48 bits.

**Examples:**

- `aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff`
- `00:11:22:33:44:55`
- `DE:AD:BE:EF:00:01`

**Analogy:**

- IP address = Your mailing address (can change if you move)
- MAC address = Your fingerprint (never changes, unique to you)

### The missing piece

Computer A wants to send data to Computer B.

Computer A knows: Computer B's IP = `192.168.1.20`

But the network hardware (Ethernet) needs: Computer B's MAC address = `??:??:??:??:??:??`

**ARP is the tool that finds the answer.**

**ARP** stands for **Address Resolution Protocol**.

Its only job: **"I have an IP address. Give me the MAC address for that IP."**

---

## PART 2: HOW ARP WORKS (STEP BY STEP)

### The scenario

Computer A (IP `192.168.1.10`, MAC `aa:aa:aa:aa:aa:aa`) wants to send data to Computer B (IP `192.168.1.20`, MAC unknown).

Computer A has never talked to Computer B before.

### Step 1: Computer A checks its ARP cache

Before asking the network, Computer A looks in its **ARP cache** (a temporary memory of "IP → MAC" pairs).

```bash
ip neigh show
```

(This is the command to see the ARP cache on Linux. We'll cover it soon.)

The cache is empty for `192.168.1.20` because they've never talked.

### Step 2: Computer A sends an ARP request (broadcast)

Computer A shouts to EVERYONE on the network:

> "Hello! I am `192.168.1.10` with MAC `aa:aa:aa:aa:aa:aa`.
> Who has IP address `192.168.1.20`?
> Please tell me your MAC address."

This message is called an **ARP request**.

**Important:** This message goes to **every computer** on the network. The destination MAC address is `ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff` - the **broadcast address** (meaning "everyone").

### Step 3: Every computer receives the request

- Computer C (IP `192.168.1.30`) receives the request, looks at it, says "That's not my IP, ignore"
- The router (IP `192.168.1.1`) receives it, says "Not me, ignore"
- The printer (IP `192.168.1.99`) receives it, says "Not me, ignore"
- Computer B (IP `192.168.1.20`) receives it and says "THAT'S ME!"

### Step 4: Computer B sends an ARP reply

Computer B sends a message directly back to Computer A:

> "Hello Computer A. I am IP `192.168.1.20` and my MAC address is `bb:bb:bb:bb:bb:bb`."

This is called an **ARP reply**.

**Important:** This message goes ONLY to Computer A (not broadcast to everyone).

### Step 5: Computer A updates its ARP cache

Computer A now knows: `192.168.1.20` = MAC `bb:bb:bb:bb:bb:bb`

It stores this in its ARP cache for future use.

### Step 6: Data can now be sent

Computer A can now wrap its data in an Ethernet frame with:

- Destination MAC = `bb:bb:bb:bb:bb:bb`
- Destination IP = `192.168.1.20`

The network hardware knows exactly where to send it.

---

## PART 3: VISUAL EXAMPLE

```
Network: 192.168.1.0/24

+----------------+     +----------------+     +----------------+
| Computer A     |     | Computer B     |     | Computer C     |
| IP: 192.168.1.10|     | IP: 192.168.1.20|     | IP: 192.168.1.30|
| MAC: aa:aa:aa  |     | MAC: bb:bb:bb  |     | MAC: cc:cc:cc  |
+----------------+     +----------------+     +----------------+
        |                       |                       |
        +-----------------------+-----------------------+
                           |
                    [Ethernet Switch]
                           |
                    +----------------+
                    | Router         |
                    | IP: 192.168.1.1|
                    | MAC: rr:rr:rr  |
                    +----------------+

STEP 1: A wants to talk to B. A doesn't know B's MAC.
STEP 2: A shouts "Who has 192.168.1.20? Tell aa:aa:aa"
        → EVERYONE hears this (A, B, C, router)
STEP 3: B replies "I have 192.168.1.20, my MAC is bb:bb:bb"
        → ONLY A hears this reply
STEP 4: A stores (192.168.1.20 = bb:bb:bb) in cache
STEP 5: A sends actual data to bb:bb:bb
```

---

## PART 4: ARP CACHE (THE "REMEMBERED" MAP)

### What is the ARP cache?

Your computer remembers IP-to-MAC mappings so it doesn't have to ask every single time.

The cache is temporary. Entries expire after a few minutes (usually 1-5 minutes).

### Viewing the ARP cache on Linux

```bash
ip neigh show
```

**`neigh` stands for "neighbors"** - other computers on the same network.

**Example output:**

```
192.168.1.1 dev eth0 lladdr 00:11:22:33:44:55 REACHABLE
192.168.1.20 dev eth0 lladdr aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff STALE
192.168.1.30 dev eth0 lladdr 11:22:33:44:55:66 DELAY
```

**Let me explain EVERY piece:**

| Column     | Example             | What it means                                |
| ---------- | ------------------- | -------------------------------------------- |
| IP address | `192.168.1.1`       | The IP address of the neighbor               |
| `dev eth0` | `dev eth0`          | Which interface this neighbor is on          |
| `lladdr`   | `00:11:22:33:44:55` | Link Layer address = MAC address             |
| State      | `REACHABLE`         | Verified working recently                    |
| State      | `STALE`             | Was working, but hasn't been used in a while |
| State      | `DELAY`             | Waiting to confirm before marking REACHABLE  |
| State      | `FAILED`            | No reply to ARP request                      |

### What the ARP states mean (detailed)

| State       | What it means                                                                                                                     | When it happens                        |
| ----------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------- |
| `REACHABLE` | Confirmed working. Computer sent something recently and got a reply.                                                              | Normal active connection               |
| `STALE`     | Entry is old. Computer hasn't talked to this IP in a while. Linux will try to use it, but if it fails, will send new ARP request. | Normal for idle connections            |
| `DELAY`     | Linux sent something and is waiting for confirmation. Temporary state.                                                            | Lasts a few seconds                    |
| `PROBE`     | Linux is actively sending ARP requests to verify this entry.                                                                      | Happens automatically                  |
| `FAILED`    | No reply to ARP requests. This IP is not reachable.                                                                               | Computer turned off or cable unplugged |
| `PERMANENT` | Manually added entry that never expires.                                                                                          | Set by administrator                   |

### Adding a permanent ARP entry (rare, but sometimes needed)

```bash
sudo ip neigh add 192.168.1.99 lladdr de:ad:be:ef:00:01 dev eth0 nud permanent
```

**Why would you do this?** Some devices (certain printers, industrial equipment) don't respond to ARP properly. You can manually tell Linux their MAC address.

### Deleting an ARP entry

```bash
sudo ip neigh del 192.168.1.20 dev eth0
```

**Why delete?** If a computer changed its network card (MAC address changed), the old ARP entry is wrong. Deleting it forces Linux to ask again.

### Flushing (clearing) the entire ARP cache

```bash
sudo ip neigh flush all
```

This removes every ARP entry. Linux will re-learn them as needed.

---

## PART 5: THE OLD WAY (`arp` command)

Before `ip neigh`, the old command was `arp`.

You will still see this in older scripts and tutorials.

| Old command                             | New command                           |
| --------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------- |
| `arp -n`                                | `ip neigh show`                       |
| `arp -d 192.168.1.20`                   | `sudo ip neigh del 192.168.1.20`      |
| `arp -s 192.168.1.99 de:ad:be:ef:00:01` | `sudo ip neigh add ... nud permanent` |

**Learn both.** Use `ip neigh` for your own work. Recognize `arp` when you see it.

---

## PART 6: ARP TROUBLESHOOTING

### Problem 1: Cannot ping a computer on the same network

You try:

```bash
ping 192.168.1.20
# Output: Destination Host Unreachable
```

Check ARP:

```bash
ip neigh show | grep 192.168.1.20
```

**If you see `FAILED`:**

```
192.168.1.20 dev eth0 lladdr (incomplete) FAILED
```

**What this means:** Linux sent an ARP request asking "Who has 192.168.1.20?" and got NO reply.

**Possible causes:**

- Computer at 192.168.1.20 is turned off
- Computer at 192.168.1.20 has a different subnet mask (different network)
- Cable is unplugged from computer at 192.168.1.20
- Firewall is blocking ARP (rare)

**What to do:**

1. Check if the computer is on: go look at it
2. Check if you are on the same network: `ip addr show` on BOTH computers
3. Check cables

**If you see `STALE` or `REACHABLE` but ping still fails:**

The ARP entry exists (so the computer responded), but ping isn't working. Problem is higher level (firewall blocking ICMP, etc.)

### Problem 2: Wrong ARP entry (ARP poisoning/spoofing)

Sometimes a malicious computer replies to ARP requests pretending to be someone else.

**Example:** Attacker says "I am 192.168.1.1 (the router)!" and gives their own MAC address.

Now all your traffic goes to the attacker instead of the real router.

**How to detect:** Check the MAC address of your router.

```bash
ip neigh show | grep 192.168.1.1
```

Compare the MAC address shown with the actual MAC address of your router (printed on the bottom of the router, or check router's admin page).

If they don't match, someone is spoofing ARP.

**How to fix (temporary):**

```bash
sudo ip neigh del 192.168.1.1 dev eth0
sudo ip neigh add 192.168.1.1 lladdr REAL_MAC_ADDRESS dev eth0 nud permanent
```

**How to fix (permanent):** Configure static ARP on your router, use switch security features (DHCP snooping, ARP inspection), or use network segmentation.

### Problem 3: ARP requests flooding the network

If a computer is trying to reach many different IP addresses that don't exist, it will send ARP requests for each one.

**Check if your computer is doing this:**

```bash
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 arp
```

If you see a constant stream of "Who has X.X.X.X?" for random IPs, something is wrong (maybe a misconfigured program, or malware).

---

## PART 7: IPv6 AND NEIGHBOR DISCOVERY PROTOCOL (NDP)

IPv6 does NOT use ARP.

IPv6 uses **Neighbor Discovery Protocol (NDP)**.

But the command on Linux is the SAME:

```bash
ip neigh show
```

For IPv6, you will see entries like:

```
fe80::1234:5678:9abc:def0 dev eth0 lladdr 00:11:22:33:44:55 REACHABLE
```

**Key differences (just so you know):**

- ARP uses broadcast (ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff) - inefficient on large networks
- NDP uses multicast (more efficient)
- NDP includes router discovery, duplicate address detection, and more

**For now:** Just know that `ip neigh` works for both IPv4 and IPv6. The concepts are the same.

---

## PART 8: PRACTICAL LABS

### Lab 1: Watch ARP in real time

**On Computer A (your Linux machine):**

First, clear the ARP cache:

```bash
sudo ip neigh flush all
```

Now, in one terminal, start watching ARP traffic:

```bash
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 arp
```

(If you don't have `tcpdump` installed: `sudo apt install tcpdump` or `sudo dnf install tcpdump`)

In another terminal, ping a computer on your network:

```bash
ping 192.168.1.1
```

**What you will see in tcpdump:**

```
ARP, Request who-has 192.168.1.1 tell 192.168.1.10, length 28
ARP, Reply 192.168.1.1 is-at 00:11:22:33:44:55, length 28
```

**You just watched ARP in action!**

### Lab 2: View your ARP cache before and after ping

**Step 1:** Check ARP cache (should be empty or have old entries):

```bash
ip neigh show
```

**Step 2:** Ping a computer on your network:

```bash
ping -c 1 192.168.1.1
```

**Step 3:** Check ARP cache again:

```bash
ip neigh show
```

You should now see an entry for `192.168.1.1` with state `REACHABLE` (or `STALE`).

### Lab 3: Delete ARP entry and watch it come back

**Step 1:** Verify you have an ARP entry for your router:

```bash
ip neigh show | grep 192.168.1.1
```

**Step 2:** Delete it:

```bash
sudo ip neigh del 192.168.1.1 dev eth0
```

**Step 3:** Verify it's gone:

```bash
ip neigh show | grep 192.168.1.1
# Should show nothing
```

**Step 4:** Ping the router again:

```bash
ping -c 1 192.168.1.1
```

**Step 5:** Check ARP cache again:

```bash
ip neigh show | grep 192.168.1.1
```

The entry is back! Linux automatically re-ARP'd when you tried to ping.

---

## SUMMARY OF TOPIC 5

| Concept         | One-sentence explanation                                  |
| --------------- | --------------------------------------------------------- |
| MAC address     | Hardware address burned into network card (never changes) |
| ARP             | Protocol that finds MAC address for a given IP address    |
| ARP request     | Broadcast message: "Who has IP X?"                        |
| ARP reply       | Direct message: "IP X is at MAC Y"                        |
| ARP cache       | Temporary memory of IP → MAC mappings                     |
| `ip neigh show` | Command to view ARP cache on Linux                        |

### Common ARP states you'll see

| State       | Meaning                               |
| ----------- | ------------------------------------- |
| `REACHABLE` | Good - recently confirmed working     |
| `STALE`     | Old entry - may or may not still work |
| `FAILED`    | Bad - no reply to ARP request         |
| `PERMANENT` | Manually added, never expires         |

### When you need ARP knowledge

| Scenario                         | What to do                                   |
| -------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------- |
| Can't ping same-network computer | Check `ip neigh` for `FAILED` state          |
| Suspicious network activity      | Check for wrong MAC addresses (ARP spoofing) |
| Troubleshooting slow network     | Flush ARP cache (`ip neigh flush all`)       |
| Static IP for critical device    | Add permanent ARP entry                      |

---

# TOPIC 6: ROUTING & DEFAULT GATEWAY

## Before we start

I will **never assume you know anything**. Every concept, every command, every number is explained from absolute zero.

Your mother is watching. I will not fail.

---

## PART 1: WHAT IS ROUTING?

### The problem routing solves

You have two computers:

- Computer A: `192.168.1.10`
- Computer B: `8.8.8.8` (Google's DNS server - far away on the internet)

Computer A wants to send data to Computer B.

But Computer A is not directly connected to Computer B. There is no cable between them.

**How does the data get there?**

**Routing** is the answer. Routing is the process of deciding:

> "Which door (interface) should I send this packet out of, and who should I give it to?"

### The routing table

Every Linux computer has a **routing table** - a set of rules that answers:

> "For any destination IP address, what should I do?"

Think of the routing table like a **set of signs** at an intersection:

| If you want to go to... | Use this exit...    |
| ----------------------- | ------------------- |
| Your own street         | Drive directly      |
| Any other street        | Go to the main road |

```bash
ip route show
```

This command shows your routing table. We'll explain every piece of the output soon.

---

## PART 2: THE THREE TYPES OF ROUTES

A routing table has three types of rules:

### Type 1: Directly connected network

**Rule:** "If the destination is on MY network, send it directly. No router needed."

**Example:** `192.168.1.0/24 dev eth0`

**Translation:** Any IP that starts with `192.168.1` is on my network. Use door `eth0` and send it directly to that IP.

**No router involved.** The destination computer is on the same physical network (same switch, same cable).

### Type 2: Default route (gateway)

**Rule:** "If the destination is NOT on any network I know about, send it to this special computer (the gateway)."

**Example:** `default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0`

**Translation:** I don't know where this destination is. Send it to `192.168.1.1` (the router) through door `eth0`. The router will figure it out.

**The gateway is your router.** It knows how to reach other networks.

### Type 3: Specific remote network

**Rule:** "For this specific network, use a specific router."

**Example:** `10.0.0.0/8 via 192.168.1.254 dev eth0`

**Translation:** Any IP that starts with `10.x.x.x` should be sent to router `192.168.1.254` (not the default gateway). Use door `eth0`.

**When do you use this?** When you have multiple routers for different networks (VPNs, company networks, etc.).

---

## PART 3: YOUR ACTUAL ROUTING TABLE (DECODED)

Run this command on your Linux computer:

```bash
ip route show
```

**Typical output for a home computer:**

```
default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0 proto dhcp src 192.168.1.100 metric 100
192.168.1.0/24 dev eth0 proto kernel scope link src 192.168.1.100 metric 100
```

**Let me explain EVERY piece of EVERY line:**

### Line 1: The directly connected network

```
192.168.1.0/24 dev eth0 proto kernel scope link src 192.168.1.100 metric 100
```

| Piece               | Value             | What it means                                                     |
| ------------------- | ----------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `192.168.1.0/24`    | Network address   | This rule applies to any IP from `192.168.1.1` to `192.168.1.254` |
| `dev eth0`          | Door              | Send packets out of interface `eth0`                              |
| `proto kernel`      | Added by kernel   | Linux automatically added this (not manually configured)          |
| `scope link`        | Direct connection | This network is directly connected (no router)                    |
| `src 192.168.1.100` | Source hint       | When sending to this network, use this source IP                  |
| `metric 100`        | Priority          | Lower number = higher priority (if multiple routes match)         |

**Translation:** If you want to reach any IP in `192.168.1.x`, use door `eth0` and send it directly to that IP. No router needed.

### Line 2: The default route (gateway)

```
default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0 proto dhcp src 192.168.1.100 metric 100
```

| Piece               | Value           | What it means                                 |
| ------------------- | --------------- | --------------------------------------------- |
| `default`           | Catch-all       | If no other rule matches, use this rule       |
| `via 192.168.1.1`   | Through this IP | Send the packet to `192.168.1.1` (the router) |
| `dev eth0`          | Door            | Use door `eth0` to reach the router           |
| `proto dhcp`        | Added by DHCP   | Your router told Linux this route (via DHCP)  |
| `src 192.168.1.100` | Source hint     | Use this source IP when sending               |
| `metric 100`        | Priority        | Priority of this route                        |

**Translation:** If you want to reach ANY IP that is NOT `192.168.1.x`, send it to `192.168.1.1` (your router) through door `eth0`. The router will forward it.

---

## PART 4: HOW LINUX USES THE ROUTING TABLE (DECISION PROCESS)

When Linux needs to send a packet to destination IP `X`, it does this:

### Step 1: Check for exact match

Linux looks at the routing table and finds ALL rules that match destination `X`.

**Which rules match?**

- `192.168.1.0/24` matches any IP between `192.168.1.1` and `192.168.1.254`
- `default` matches ANY IP (it's the catch-all)

### Step 2: Choose the most specific match

**Rule:** The route with the **longest prefix** (most specific) wins.

| Destination    | Which route matches?                              | Why?                                           |
| -------------- | ------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------- |
| `192.168.1.50` | `192.168.1.0/24` (24 bits) AND `default` (0 bits) | /24 is more specific than /0, so /24 wins      |
| `8.8.8.8`      | Only `default` matches                            | /24 doesn't match (8.8.8.8 is not 192.168.1.x) |

### Step 3: Send the packet

Once Linux chooses the route:

- If it's a **direct route** (scope link): Send directly to destination IP
- If it's a **gateway route** (via X): Send to the gateway IP instead

### Step 4: ARP (if needed)

If sending directly (same network):

1. Linux needs the MAC address of destination IP
2. If MAC not in ARP cache, Linux sends ARP request
3. Once MAC is known, Linux sends packet

If sending to gateway:

1. Linux needs the MAC address of the gateway IP
2. If MAC not in ARP cache, Linux sends ARP request for gateway
3. Once MAC is known, Linux sends packet to gateway

---

## PART 5: REAL EXAMPLES (TRACE THE DECISION)

### Example 1: Ping another computer on same network

You type: `ping 192.168.1.50`

**Routing table:**

```
192.168.1.0/24 dev eth0 scope link
default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0
```

**Linux decision process:**

1. Destination = `192.168.1.50`
2. Does `192.168.1.0/24` match? YES (192.168.1.50 is inside 192.168.1.0/24)
3. Does `default` match? YES (default matches everything)
4. Which is more specific? `/24` (24 bits) > `/0` (0 bits). `/24` wins.
5. Route says: `scope link` (direct connection, no gateway)
6. Linux sends packet directly to `192.168.1.50` (needs ARP first)

**Result:** Packet goes directly to `.50`. No router involved.

### Example 2: Ping a website on the internet

You type: `ping 8.8.8.8`

**Routing table:**

```
192.168.1.0/24 dev eth0 scope link
default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0
```

**Linux decision process:**

1. Destination = `8.8.8.8`
2. Does `192.168.1.0/24` match? NO (8.8.8.8 is not 192.168.1.x)
3. Does `default` match? YES (default matches everything)
4. Only `default` matches. It wins by default.
5. Route says: `via 192.168.1.1` (send to gateway)
6. Linux sends packet to `192.168.1.1` (needs ARP for gateway)

**Result:** Packet goes to router (192.168.1.1). Router forwards it to the internet.

### Example 3: Ping a VPN network (with specific route)

You have a VPN that uses network `10.0.0.0/8`.

**Routing table:**

```
192.168.1.0/24 dev eth0 scope link
10.0.0.0/8 via 192.168.1.254 dev eth0
default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0
```

You type: `ping 10.20.30.40`

**Linux decision process:**

1. Destination = `10.20.30.40`
2. Does `192.168.1.0/24` match? NO (10.x is not 192.168.1.x)
3. Does `10.0.0.0/8` match? YES (10.20.30.40 is inside 10.0.0.0/8)
4. Does `default` match? YES
5. Which is more specific? `/8` (8 bits) > `/0` (0 bits). `/8` wins.
6. Route says: `via 192.168.1.254` (send to VPN gateway)
7. Linux sends packet to `192.168.1.254` (VPN router)

**Result:** Packet goes to VPN gateway (192.168.1.254), NOT to the default gateway.

---

## PART 6: MANIPULATING THE ROUTING TABLE

### Viewing routes

```bash
# Basic view
ip route show

# More detailed view
ip route show table all

# Show only default route
ip route show default

# Show routes for a specific network
ip route show 192.168.1.0/24
```

### Adding a route

```bash
# Add a direct network route
sudo ip route add 192.168.2.0/24 dev eth0

# Add a route through a gateway
sudo ip route add 10.0.0.0/8 via 192.168.1.254 dev eth0

# Add a route to a single IP (host route)
sudo ip route add 192.168.100.50/32 dev eth0
# /32 means "exactly this one IP address"
```

### Adding the default gateway

```bash
# Add default gateway
sudo ip route add default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0

# Same thing, different syntax
sudo ip route add 0.0.0.0/0 via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0
# 0.0.0.0/0 means "any IP" (same as default)
```

### Deleting routes

```bash
# Delete a network route
sudo ip route del 192.168.2.0/24

# Delete default gateway
sudo ip route del default

# Delete route to a specific gateway
sudo ip route del 10.0.0.0/8 via 192.168.1.254
```

### Replacing a route (add or change)

```bash
# If route exists, change it. If not, add it.
sudo ip route replace 192.168.2.0/24 via 192.168.1.253 dev eth0
```

---

## PART 7: METRICS (WHEN MULTIPLE ROUTES MATCH)

Sometimes you have multiple routes that could work. Linux uses **metrics** (also called "distance") to decide.

### Example: Two default gateways

```bash
default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0 metric 100
default via 192.168.2.1 dev wlan0 metric 200
```

**Linux chooses the route with the LOWER metric.**

In this example:

- Ethernet gateway (metric 100) is preferred over WiFi (metric 200)
- If Ethernet fails, Linux will use WiFi

### Viewing metrics

```bash
ip route show
```

You will see `metric 100`, `metric 200`, etc. at the end of each line.

### Changing metric

```bash
# Add route with specific metric
sudo ip route add default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0 metric 50

# Change metric of existing route
sudo ip route replace default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0 metric 50
```

---

## PART 8: TROUBLESHOOTING ROUTING PROBLEMS

### Problem 1: No default gateway (can't reach internet)

**Symptom:** Can ping local computers but cannot ping `8.8.8.8`

**Check:**

```bash
ip route show | grep default
```

**If nothing shows:** You have no default gateway.

**Fix:**

```bash
sudo ip route add default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0
```

(Replace `192.168.1.1` with your router's IP)

### Problem 2: Wrong default gateway

**Symptom:** Can't reach internet, or traffic going to wrong place

**Check:**

```bash
ip route show default
```

**If the gateway IP is wrong:** Delete and re-add

```bash
sudo ip route del default
sudo ip route add default via CORRECT_GATEWAY_IP dev eth0
```

### Problem 3: Missing route to local network

**Symptom:** Can't ping other computers on same network

**Check:**

```bash
ip route show | grep 192.168.1.0
# (replace with your network)
```

**If nothing shows:** Add the local network route

```bash
sudo ip route add 192.168.1.0/24 dev eth0
```

### Problem 4: Two default gateways (conflict)

**Symptom:** Intermittent connectivity, weird routing behavior

**Check:**

```bash
ip route show | grep default
```

**If you see TWO default lines:** One might be wrong.

**Fix:** Delete the wrong one

```bash
# Delete the one with higher metric (less preferred) or wrong IP
sudo ip route del default via WRONG_IP dev eth0
```

### Problem 5: Can reach IPs but not hostnames

**Symptom:** `ping 8.8.8.8` works, `ping google.com` fails

**This is NOT a routing problem.** This is a DNS problem. We cover DNS in Topic 7.

---

## PART 9: PERSISTENT ROUTES (SURVIVING REBOOT)

### The problem

`ip route add` commands are **temporary**. They disappear when you reboot or restart networking.

### Solution 1: Distribution-specific configuration

**Ubuntu/Debian (Netplan - modern):**

Edit `/etc/netplan/00-installer-config.yaml`:

```yaml
network:
  version: 2
  renderer: networkd
  ethernets:
    eth0:
      dhcp4: yes
      routes:
        - to: 10.0.0.0/8
          via: 192.168.1.254
          metric: 100
```

Then apply:

```bash
sudo netplan apply
```

**Ubuntu/Debian (legacy /etc/network/interfaces):**

Edit `/etc/network/interfaces`:

```
auto eth0
iface eth0 inet static
    address 192.168.1.100
    netmask 255.255.255.0
    gateway 192.168.1.1
    up ip route add 10.0.0.0/8 via 192.168.1.254
```

**RHEL/CentOS/Fedora:**

Edit `/etc/sysconfig/network-scripts/route-eth0`:

```
10.0.0.0/8 via 192.168.1.254 dev eth0
```

### Solution 2: Script at boot (works everywhere)

Create a script:

```bash
sudo nano /etc/network/if-up.d/custom-routes
```

Contents:

```bash
#!/bin/bash
ip route add 10.0.0.0/8 via 192.168.1.254 dev eth0
```

Make executable:

```bash
sudo chmod +x /etc/network/if-up.d/custom-routes
```

---

## PART 10: PRACTICAL LABS

### Lab 1: View your routing table

```bash
ip route show
```

Write down:

1. Your default gateway IP
2. Your local network (the /24 or /16 line)

### Lab 2: Add and remove a test route

```bash
# Add a route to a fake network
sudo ip route add 203.0.113.0/24 dev eth0

# Verify it's there
ip route show | grep 203.0.113.0

# Delete it
sudo ip route del 203.0.113.0/24

# Verify it's gone
ip route show | grep 203.0.113.0
```

### Lab 3: Trace the route decision

Use `ip route get` to ask Linux "Which route would you use for this destination?"

```bash
# Ask for route to local computer
ip route get 192.168.1.50

# Ask for route to internet
ip route get 8.8.8.8

# Ask for route to a fake IP
ip route get 1.2.3.4
```

**Example output:**

```
192.168.1.50 via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0 src 192.168.1.100 uid 1000
    cache
```

This tells you exactly which gateway Linux would use.

### Lab 4: Break and fix your routing (WARNING: Do on local machine only)

**This will disconnect you if done over SSH! Only do on a physical machine or local VM.**

```bash
# Remove default gateway (internet will stop working)
sudo ip route del default

# Try to ping google (will fail)
ping -c 2 8.8.8.8

# Add it back
sudo ip route add default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0

# Ping works again
ping -c 2 8.8.8.8
```

---

## SUMMARY OF TOPIC 6

| Concept         | One-sentence explanation                               |
| --------------- | ------------------------------------------------------ |
| Routing         | Deciding which door (interface) to send packets out of |
| Routing table   | List of rules that tell Linux where to send packets    |
| Direct route    | Destination is on same network; send directly          |
| Default route   | Catch-all rule; send everything else to gateway        |
