```bash
option domain-name-servers 8.8.8.8, 8.8.4.4;

# Default lease time (seconds)
default-lease-time 86400;   # 24 hours

# Maximum lease time (client can request up to this)
max-lease-time 172800;      # 48 hours

# Authoritative means "I am the ONLY DHCP server on this network"
# Unauthoritative means "There may be other DHCP servers"
authoritative;

# Define the network/subnet this DHCP server manages
subnet 192.168.1.0 netmask 255.255.255.0 {
    # IP address range to hand out
    range 192.168.1.100 192.168.1.200;

    # Default gateway (router)
    option routers 192.168.1.1;

    # DNS servers
    option domain-name-servers 8.8.8.8, 8.8.4.4;

    # Broadcast address for this network
    option broadcast-address 192.168.1.255;
}
```

**Let me explain EVERY line:**

| Line                                       | Meaning                                                         |
| ------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `option domain-name`                       | Domain name for the network (appended to unqualified hostnames) |
| `option domain-name-servers`               | DNS servers to give to clients                                  |
| `default-lease-time`                       | How long a lease lasts if client doesn't ask for specific time  |
| `max-lease-time`                           | Maximum lease time allowed (client can request less)            |
| `authoritative`                            | This server is the official DHCP server for this network        |
| `subnet 192.168.1.0 netmask 255.255.255.0` | Which network this section manages                              |
| `range`                                    | Pool of IP addresses to give out (start to end)                 |
| `option routers`                           | Default gateway to give to clients                              |

### Example 2: Static IP assignments (reservations)

Sometimes you want the same device to ALWAYS get the same IP address.

```bash
# First, find the MAC address of the device
# On the device itself:
ip link show

# Example MAC: 00:11:22:33:44:55

# Then add to dhcpd.conf:
host printer {
    hardware ethernet 00:11:22:33:44:55;
    fixed-address 192.168.1.50;
    option host-name "office-printer";
}

host webserver {
    hardware ethernet aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff;
    fixed-address 192.168.1.10;
    option host-name "web-server";
}
```

**What this does:**

- The device with MAC `00:11:22:33:44:55` ALWAYS gets IP `192.168.1.50`
- The device with MAC `aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff` ALWAYS gets IP `192.168.1.10`

### Example 3: Multiple subnets (different networks)

If your Linux server has multiple network interfaces (eth0 and eth1), you can serve different networks:

```bash
# Interface eth0 (192.168.1.x network)
subnet 192.168.1.0 netmask 255.255.255.0 {
    range 192.168.1.100 192.168.1.200;
    option routers 192.168.1.1;
    option domain-name-servers 8.8.8.8;
}

# Interface eth1 (10.0.0.x network - different physical network)
subnet 10.0.0.0 netmask 255.255.255.0 {
    range 10.0.0.100 10.0.0.200;
    option routers 10.0.0.1;
    option domain-name-servers 8.8.8.8;
}
```

### Example 4: Advanced options

```bash
subnet 192.168.1.0 netmask 255.255.255.0 {
    range 192.168.1.100 192.168.1.200;
    option routers 192.168.1.1;
    option domain-name-servers 8.8.8.8, 8.8.4.4;
    option domain-name "internal.mycompany.com";

    # Send specific options to clients
    option ntp-servers 192.168.1.10;        # NTP time server
    option netbios-name-servers 192.168.1.20; # WINS server
    option netbios-node-type 8;              # Hybrid node type

    # Default lease time for this subnet only
    default-lease-time 43200;   # 12 hours
    max-lease-time 86400;       # 24 hours
}
```

### Which interface to listen on?

Edit `/etc/default/isc-dhcp-server` (Ubuntu/Debian):

```bash
sudo nano /etc/default/isc-dhcp-server
```

Find this line:

```bash
INTERFACESv4="eth0"
```

Change `eth0` to the interface you want the DHCP server to listen on.

### Start and enable DHCP server

```bash
# Start the server
sudo systemctl start isc-dhcp-server

# Enable at boot
sudo systemctl enable isc-dhcp-server

# Check status
sudo systemctl status isc-dhcp-server

# Check logs
sudo journalctl -u isc-dhcp-server -f
```

### View DHCP leases (who got which IP)

```bash
sudo cat /var/lib/dhcp/dhcpd.leases
```

**Example output:**

```
lease 192.168.1.100 {
    starts 5 2025/01/19 08:00:00;
    ends 5 2025/01/19 20:00:00;
    cltt 5 2025/01/19 08:00:00;
    binding state active;
    next binding state free;
    rewind binding state free;
    hardware ethernet aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff;
    client-hostname "my-laptop";
}
```

**What this tells you:**

- Which IP was given out
- Which MAC address got it
- When the lease started and ends
- The hostname of the client

---

## PART 6: TROUBLESHOOTING DHCP

### Problem 1: Client not getting an IP address

**On the client (Linux):**

```bash
# Check if interface is up
ip link show eth0

# Manually request DHCP
sudo dhclient -v eth0
```

**Look for errors:**

- `No DHCPOFFERS received` = No DHCP server responded
- `Network is unreachable` = Interface is down or cable unplugged

**On the DHCP server (if you control it):**

```bash
# Check if DHCP service is running
sudo systemctl status isc-dhcp-server

# Check logs
sudo journalctl -u isc-dhcp-server -n 50

# Check if there are available IPs in range
sudo cat /var/lib/dhcp/dhcpd.leases | grep "lease" | wc -l
```

### Problem 2: Client gets wrong IP (169.254.x.x)

**Symptom:** Client gets IP like `169.254.12.34`

**What `169.254.x.x` means:** This is a "link-local" address. The client tried DHCP, got no reply, and assigned itself a random IP in this range.

**Causes:**

- DHCP server is down
- Network cable unplugged
- Firewall blocking DHCP (ports 67 and 68 UDP)

**Fix:** Check network connectivity and DHCP server.

### Problem 3: DHCP server won't start

**Check configuration syntax:**

```bash
sudo dhcpd -t -cf /etc/dhcp/dhcpd.conf
```

**Common syntax errors:**

- Missing semicolon `;` at the end of a line
- Missing closing `}`
- IP address format wrong

### Problem 4: Static IP reservation not working

**Check the MAC address is correct:**

```bash
# On the client, get MAC address
ip link show eth0 | grep ether

# Example: ether 00:11:22:33:44:55
```

**In dhcpd.conf:**

```bash
host client {
    hardware ethernet 00:11:22:33:44:55;  # Must match EXACTLY
    fixed-address 192.168.1.50;
}
```

**Case matters?** MAC addresses are not case sensitive, but use lowercase for consistency.

---

## PART 7: DHCP OPTIONS REFERENCE

| Option number | Name                | What it does               | Example            |
| ------------- | ------------------- | -------------------------- | ------------------ |
| 1             | subnet-mask         | Subnet mask                | `255.255.255.0`    |
| 3             | routers             | Default gateway            | `192.168.1.1`      |
| 6             | domain-name-servers | DNS servers                | `8.8.8.8, 8.8.4.4` |
| 12            | host-name           | Client hostname            | `my-laptop`        |
| 15            | domain-name         | DNS domain                 | `mycompany.local`  |
| 42            | ntp-servers         | NTP time servers           | `192.168.1.10`     |
| 66            | tftp-server         | TFTP server (for PXE boot) | `192.168.1.10`     |
| 67            | bootfile-name       | Boot file (for PXE)        | `pxelinux.0`       |

**In dhcpd.conf, you use the NAME, not the number.**

---

## PART 8: PRACTICAL LABS

### Lab 1: See DHCP in action (as a client)

```bash
# Step 1: Release your current IP
sudo dhclient -r eth0

# Step 2: Watch network traffic (in another terminal)
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 port 67 or port 68

# Step 3: Request a new IP
sudo dhclient eth0

# You will see:
# - DHCPDISCOVER (client broadcast)
# - DHCPOFFER (server reply)
# - DHCPREQUEST (client accepts)
# - DHCPACK (server confirms)
```

### Lab 2: Set up a simple DHCP server

**WARNING:** Only do this on an ISOLATED network or VM. Running a second DHCP server on your home network will cause conflicts.

```bash
# Step 1: Install DHCP server
sudo apt update && sudo apt install isc-dhcp-server -y

# Step 2: Configure it (edit /etc/dhcp/dhcpd.conf)
sudo nano /etc/dhcp/dhcpd.conf
```

Use this minimal config:

```bash
option domain-name-servers 8.8.8.8;
default-lease-time 600;
max-lease-time 7200;
authoritative;

subnet 192.168.1.0 netmask 255.255.255.0 {
    range 192.168.1.200 192.168.1.210;
    option routers 192.168.1.1;
}
```

```bash
# Step 3: Tell DHCP server which interface to use
echo 'INTERFACESv4="eth0"' | sudo tee /etc/default/isc-dhcp-server

# Step 4: Start the server
sudo systemctl start isc-dhcp-server
sudo systemctl enable isc-dhcp-server

# Step 5: Check status
sudo systemctl status isc-dhcp-server
```

### Lab 3: Test your DHCP server

On a DIFFERENT computer on the same network:

```bash
# Release current IP
sudo dhclient -r eth0

# Get new IP from your DHCP server
sudo dhclient eth0

# Check what IP you got
ip addr show eth0

# Should be in the range 192.168.1.200-210
```

On your DHCP server, check the lease:

```bash
sudo cat /var/lib/dhcp/dhcpd.leases
```

---

## SUMMARY OF TOPIC 10

| Concept       | One-sentence explanation                                                  |
| ------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| DHCP          | Protocol that automatically assigns IP addresses and network settings     |
| DORA          | Discover, Offer, Request, Acknowledge (the 4 steps of DHCP)               |
| Lease         | Time limit on an IP address (borrowed, not owned)                         |
| DHCP client   | Software that ASKS for an IP (dhclient, systemd-networkd, NetworkManager) |
| DHCP server   | Software that GIVES out IPs (isc-dhcp-server)                             |
| Scope         | Range of IP addresses a DHCP server can give out                          |
| Reservation   | Static IP assigned to a specific MAC address                              |
| Authoritative | This DHCP server is the official one for this network                     |

### DORA process (remember the order)

1. **D**iscover - Client: "Any DHCP server?"
2. **O**ffer - Server: "Here is an IP address"
3. **R**equest - Client: "I accept that IP"
4. **A**cknowledge - Server: "Confirmed, here are your settings"

### Common DHCP client commands

| What you want                      | Command                                  |
| ---------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------- |
| Request new IP (dhclient)          | `sudo dhclient eth0`                     |
| Release IP (dhclient)              | `sudo dhclient -r eth0`                  |
| Check lease (dhclient)             | `cat /var/lib/dhcp/dhclient.eth0.leases` |
| Check DHCP status (systemd)        | `networkctl status eth0`                 |
| Check DHCP status (NetworkManager) | `nmcli dev show eth0`                    |
| Force renew (systemd)              | `sudo networkctl renew eth0`             |

### Common DHCP server commands

| What you want      | Command                                  |
| ------------------ | ---------------------------------------- |
| Test config syntax | `sudo dhcpd -t -cf /etc/dhcp/dhcpd.conf` |
| Start server       | `sudo systemctl start isc-dhcp-server`   |
| Check status       | `sudo systemctl status isc-dhcp-server`  |
| View leases given  | `sudo cat /var/lib/dhcp/dhcpd.leases`    |
| Watch logs         | `sudo journalctl -u isc-dhcp-server -f`  |

# TOPIC 11: NETWORK BONDING & VLANS

## Before we start

I will **never assume you know anything**. Every concept, every command, every file is explained from absolute zero.

Your mother is watching. I will not fail.

---

## PART 1: WHAT IS NETWORK BONDING?

### The problem bonding solves

Your server has one network cable (eth0). That cable is connected to a switch.

**Problems with one cable:**

| Problem               | What happens                              |
| --------------------- | ----------------------------------------- |
| Cable breaks          | Server is offline                         |
| Switch port fails     | Server is offline                         |
| Too much traffic      | Cable is full (100 Mbps, 1 Gbps, etc.)    |
| One cable = one speed | You are limited to that cable's max speed |

**Bonding** (also called NIC teaming, link aggregation) combines multiple network interfaces into **one logical interface**.

### Analogy: Single lane vs multiple lane highway

| One cable (no bonding)        | Two cables bonded                          |
| ----------------------------- | ------------------------------------------ |
| Single lane road              | 2-lane highway                             |
| If road closed, traffic stops | If one lane closed, other lane still works |
| Max speed: 60 mph             | Max speed: 120 mph (if both lanes used)    |

### What bonding gives you

| Benefit                 | Explanation                                            |
| ----------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Redundancy**          | If one cable/port fails, traffic continues on other(s) |
| **Increased bandwidth** | Combine speeds (1 Gbps + 1 Gbps = up to 2 Gbps)        |
| **Load balancing**      | Traffic distributed across multiple cables             |

### The physical setup

For bonding to work, you need:

1. **Two or more network cables** from your server to the switch
2. **Switch configured for link aggregation** (LACP or static aggregation)
3. **Bonding configuration on Linux**

**If you only configure Linux but not the switch,** bonding may work for failover but NOT for increased bandwidth.

---

## PART 2: TYPES OF BONDING (MODES)

Linux bonding has **7 modes** (0 through 6). You only need to know 3 of them.

### Mode 0: balance-rr (round robin)

| Property             | Value                    |
| -------------------- | ------------------------ |
| Redundancy?          | Yes                      |
| Increased bandwidth? | Yes                      |
| Switch required?     | Yes (static aggregation) |

**How it works:** Packets are sent alternately on each interface.

- Packet 1: eth0
- Packet 2: eth1
- Packet 3: eth0
- Packet 4: eth1

**Warning:** Packets may arrive out of order. Not recommended for most uses.

### Mode 1: active-backup (MOST COMMON FOR REDUNDANCY)

| Property             | Value                          |
| -------------------- | ------------------------------ |
| Redundancy?          | Yes                            |
| Increased bandwidth? | NO (only one active at a time) |
| Switch required?     | NO (any switch works)          |

**How it works:**

- One interface is ACTIVE (sends/receives all traffic)
- Other interfaces are BACKUP (standby)
- If active fails, backup takes over immediately

**This is the SAFEST mode.** No special switch configuration needed.

### Mode 4: 802.3ad (LACP - MOST COMMON FOR SPEED + REDUNDANCY)

| Property             | Value                                              |
| -------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- |
| Redundancy?          | Yes                                                |
| Increased bandwidth? | Yes                                                |
| Switch required?     | YES (must support LACP - most managed switches do) |

**How it works:**

- Switch and server negotiate the bond using LACP protocol
- Traffic is distributed based on a hash (MAC address, IP, or port)
- All interfaces are active simultaneously

**This is the PROFESSIONAL choice** for servers that need both speed and redundancy.

### Mode 6: balance-alb (adaptive load balancing)

| Property             | Value                   |
| -------------------- | ----------------------- |
| Redundancy?          | Yes                     |
| Increased bandwidth? | Yes (for transmit only) |
| Switch required?     | NO                      |

**How it works:** Linux does load balancing without switch support by rewriting MAC addresses.

**Use this if:** You want load balancing but cannot configure the switch.

### Quick reference: Which mode to choose?

| Your situation                          | Recommended mode       |
| --------------------------------------- | ---------------------- |
| You just want failover (cable breaks)   | Mode 1 (active-backup) |
| You have a managed switch with LACP     | Mode 4 (802.3ad)       |
| You cannot configure switch, want speed | Mode 6 (balance-alb)   |
| You are doing something very specific   | Look up modes 0,2,3,5  |

---

## PART 3: CONFIGURING BONDING (TEMPORARY - COMMAND LINE)

### Step 1: Load the bonding kernel module

```bash
# Load the bonding module
sudo modprobe bonding

# Verify it loaded
lsmod | grep bonding
```

**Output:**

```
bonding                16384  0
```

### Step 2: Create the bond interface

```bash
# Create bond0 interface with mode 1 (active-backup)
sudo ip link add bond0 type bond mode 1

# Or with mode 4 (LACP)
sudo ip link add bond0 type bond mode 4
```

### Step 3: Add physical interfaces to the bond

```bash
# Bring physical interfaces down first
sudo ip link set eth0 down
sudo ip link set eth1 down

# Add them to the bond
sudo ip link set eth0 master bond0
sudo ip link set eth1 master bond0

# Bring physical interfaces up
sudo ip link set eth0 up
sudo ip link set eth1 up
```

### Step 4: Configure the bond interface with an IP

```bash
# Give bond0 an IP address
sudo ip addr add 192.168.1.100/24 dev bond0

# Bring bond0 up
sudo ip link set bond0 up

# Add default gateway
sudo ip route add default via 192.168.1.1 dev bond0
```

### Step 5: Verify the bond

```bash
# Check bond status
cat /proc/net/bonding/bond0
```

**Example output (mode 1 - active-backup):**

```
Ethernet Channel Bonding Driver: v3.7.1

Bonding Mode: fault-tolerance (active-backup)
Primary Slave: None
Currently Active Slave: eth0
MII Status: up
MII Polling Interval (ms): 100
Up Delay (ms): 0
Down Delay (ms): 0

Slave Interface: eth0
MII Status: up
Speed: 1000 Mbps
Duplex: full
Link Failure Count: 0

Slave Interface: eth1
MII Status: up
Speed: 1000 Mbps
Duplex: full
Link Failure Count: 0
```

**What this tells you:**

- Mode is active-backup
- eth0 is the ACTIVE slave (currently handling all traffic)
- eth1 is the BACKUP slave (ready to take over)
- Both cables are up (MII Status: up)

### Step 6: Test failover

```bash
# In one terminal, ping continuously
ping 192.168.1.1

# In another terminal, pull the cable from eth0 (or disable it)
sudo ip link set eth0 down

# Watch the ping. You might see 1-2 packets lost, then it continues.
# The backup interface (eth1) takes over automatically.
```

---

## PART 4: CONFIGURING BONDING (PERMANENT)

### Method A: Netplan (Ubuntu 18.04+)

**File:** `/etc/netplan/01-bonding.yaml`

**Example 1: Mode 1 (active-backup) - no special switch needed**

```yaml
network:
  version: 2
  renderer: networkd
  bonds:
    bond0:
      interfaces:
        - eth0
        - eth1
      parameters:
        mode: active-backup
        primary: eth0
        mii-monitor-interval: 100
  ethernets:
    eth0: {}
    eth1: {}
  bonds:
    bond0:
      addresses:
        - 192.168.1.100/24
      gateway4: 192.168.1.1
      nameservers:
        addresses:
          - 8.8.8.8
          - 8.8.4.4
```

**Let me explain EVERY piece:**

| Line                        | Meaning                                             |
| --------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------- |
| `bonds:`                    | We are configuring a bond                           |
| `bond0:`                    | Name of the bond interface                          |
| `interfaces:`               | Which physical interfaces to use                    |
| `mode: active-backup`       | Mode 1 (one active, one backup)                     |
| `primary: eth0`             | eth0 is preferred active (if available)             |
| `mii-monitor-interval: 100` | Check link status every 100 milliseconds            |
| `ethernets:`                | Physical interfaces (empty {} means no config here) |
| `addresses:`                | IP address for the bond                             |
| `gateway4:`                 | Default gateway                                     |

**Example 2: Mode 4 (LACP) - requires switch configuration**

```yaml
network:
  version: 2
  renderer: networkd
  bonds:
    bond0:
      interfaces:
        - eth0
        - eth1
      parameters:
        mode: 802.3ad
        lacp-rate: fast
        mii-monitor-interval: 100
  ethernets:
    eth0: {}
    eth1: {}
  bonds:
    bond0:
      addresses:
        - 192.168.1.100/24
      gateway4: 192.168.1.1
      nameservers:
        addresses:
          - 8.8.8.8
```

**Apply:**

```bash
sudo netplan try
# or
sudo netplan apply
```

### Method B: ifupdown (traditional Debian/Ubuntu)

**File:** `/etc/network/interfaces`

```bash
# The bond interface
auto bond0
iface bond0 inet static
    address 192.168.1.100
    netmask 255.255.255.0
    gateway 192.168.1.1
    bond-slaves eth0 eth1
    bond-mode active-backup
    bond-miimon 100
    bond-primary eth0

# Physical interfaces (referenced by bond)
auto eth0
iface eth0 inet manual
    bond-master bond0

auto eth1
iface eth1 inet manual
    bond-master bond0
```

**Apply:**

```bash
sudo systemctl restart networking
```

### Method C: systemd-networkd

**File 1:** `/etc/systemd/network/bond0.netdev`

```ini
[NetDev]
Name=bond0
Kind=bond

[Bond]
Mode=active-backup
MIIMonitorSec=100ms
```

**File 2:** `/etc/systemd/network/bond0.network`

```ini
[Match]
Name=bond0

[Network]
Address=192.168.1.100/24
Gateway=192.168.1.1
DNS=8.8.8.8
```

**File 3:** `/etc/systemd/network/eth0.network`

```ini
[Match]
Name=eth0

[Network]
Bond=bond0
```

**File 4:** `/etc/systemd/network/eth1.network`

```ini
[Match]
Name=eth1

[Network]
Bond=bond0
```

**Apply:**

```bash
sudo systemctl restart systemd-networkd
```

### Method D: RHEL/CentOS (ifcfg files)

**File:** `/etc/sysconfig/network-scripts/ifcfg-bond0`

```bash
DEVICE=bond0
NAME=bond0
TYPE=Bond
BONDING_MASTER=yes
IPADDR=192.168.1.100
PREFIX=24
GATEWAY=192.168.1.1
ONBOOT=yes
BONDING_OPTS="mode=active-backup miimon=100"
```

**File:** `/etc/sysconfig/network-scripts/ifcfg-eth0`

```bash
DEVICE=eth0
NAME=eth0
TYPE=Ethernet
ONBOOT=yes
MASTER=bond0
SLAVE=yes
```

**File:** `/etc/sysconfig/network-scripts/ifcfg-eth1`

```bash
DEVICE=eth1
NAME=eth1
TYPE=Ethernet
ONBOOT=yes
MASTER=bond0
SLAVE=yes
```

**Apply:**

```bash
sudo systemctl restart network
```

---

## PART 5: MONITORING AND TROUBLESHOOTING BONDS

### Check bond status

```bash
# Main status file
cat /proc/net/bonding/bond0

# Quick summary
cat /proc/net/bonding/bond0 | grep -E "Bonding Mode|Currently Active Slave|Slave Interface|MII Status"
```

### Check which interfaces are in the bond

```bash
ip link show | grep master
```

**Output:**

```
2: eth0: <...> master bond0 state UP
3: eth1: <...> master bond0 state UP
```

### Force a failover (test redundancy)

```bash
# Make eth0 the active slave
sudo ip link set eth0 down

# Wait a few seconds, then check
cat /proc/net/bonding/bond0 | grep "Currently Active Slave"
# Should show eth1

# Bring eth0 back
sudo ip link set eth0 up

# Check again (may still be eth1 if primary not set)
cat /proc/net/bonding/bond0 | grep "Currently Active Slave"
```

### Change the active slave manually (mode 1 only)

```bash
# Make eth1 active
echo eth1 > /sys/class/net/bond0/bonding/active_slave

# Make eth0 active again
echo eth0 > /sys/class/net/bond0/bonding/active_slave
```

---

## PART 6: WHAT ARE VLANS?

### The problem VLANs solve

You have one physical network switch. Different types of traffic need to be separated:

| Department  | Should they see each other?         |
| ----------- | ----------------------------------- |
| Finance     | NO - must be isolated from others   |
| Engineering | NO - separate network for security  |
| Guest WiFi  | NO - cannot access internal network |
| Management  | NO - separate for security          |

Without VLANs, you would need **separate physical switches and cables** for each network.

**VLANs** (Virtual Local Area Networks) allow you to split **one physical switch** into **multiple virtual switches**.

### Analogy: Apartment building

| Without VLANs                  | With VLANs                             |
| ------------------------------ | -------------------------------------- |
| One huge room                  | Apartment building with separate units |
| Everyone can see/hear everyone | Each apartment is isolated             |
| No privacy                     | Privacy and separation                 |
| One fire alarm for everyone    | Each apartment has its own             |

### How VLANs work

Each Ethernet frame (packet) gets a **VLAN tag** (a number between 1 and 4094).

| VLAN tag | What it means                              |
| -------- | ------------------------------------------ |
| Tag 10   | This traffic belongs to Finance department |
| Tag 20   | This traffic belongs to Engineering        |
| Tag 30   | This traffic is Guest WiFi                 |
| Tag 99   | This traffic is Management                 |

The switch reads the tag and only sends the frame to ports that are configured for that VLAN.

### VLAN example

```
                    ┌─────────────────────────────────┐
                    │         Managed Switch          │
                    │                                 │
Port 1 (Tag 10)─────┤                                 │
(Finance PC)        │                                 │
                    │   VLAN 10: Ports 1, 2, 3        │
Port 2 (Tag 10)─────┤   VLAN 20: Ports 4, 5, 6        │
(Finance Server)    │   VLAN 30: Ports 7, 8           │
                    │                                 │
Port 4 (Tag 20)─────┤                                 │
(Engineering PC)    │                                 │
                    │                                 │
Port 5 (Tag 20)─────┤                                 │
(Engineering Server)│                                 │
                    └─────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Ports in the same VLAN can talk to each other. Ports in different VLANs CANNOT (without a router).**

---

## PART 7: VLANS ON LINUX

### How Linux handles VLANs

Linux creates **virtual interfaces** on top of a physical interface.

- Physical interface: `eth0` (connected to switch)
- VLAN 10 interface: `eth0.10`
- VLAN 20 interface: `eth0.20`
- VLAN 30 interface: `eth0.30`

Each VLAN interface gets its OWN IP address and configuration.

### The switch side

For Linux to use VLANs, the switch port connected to your Linux server must be configured as a **trunk port** (carrying multiple VLANs).

**Example switch configuration (Cisco syntax):**

```
interface GigabitEthernet0/1
 switchport mode trunk
 switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30
```

### Example 1: Create VLAN interfaces (temporary)

```bash
# Load the 8021q kernel module (VLAN support)
sudo modprobe 8021q

# Verify it loaded
lsmod | grep 8021q

# Create VLAN interface 10 on top of eth0
sudo ip link add link eth0 name eth0.10 type vlan id 10

# Create VLAN interface 20
sudo ip link add link eth0 name eth0.20 type vlan id 20

# Bring them up
sudo ip link set eth0.10 up
sudo ip link set eth0.20 up

# Assign IP addresses
sudo ip addr add 192.168.10.100/24 dev eth0.10
sudo ip addr add 192.168.20.100/24 dev eth0.20

# Verify
ip addr show eth0.10
ip addr show eth0.20
```

**What this creates:**

| Interface       | VLAN ID | Network      | Purpose          |
| --------------- | ------- | ------------ | ---------------- |
| eth0 (physical) | No tag  | (no IP)      | Parent interface |
| eth0.10         | 10      | 192.168.10.x | Finance          |
| eth0.20         | 20      | 192.168.20.x | Engineering      |

### Example 2: View VLAN interfaces

```bash
# List all VLANs
cat /proc/net/vlan/config

# Output:
VLAN Dev name    | VLAN ID
eth0.10          | 10
eth0.20          | 20
```

### Example 3: Delete a VLAN interface

```bash
sudo ip link delete eth0.10
```

---

## PART 8: PERMANENT VLAN CONFIGURATION

### Method A: Netplan

**File:** `/etc/netplan/01-vlans.yaml`

```yaml
network:
  version: 2
  renderer: networkd
  ethernets:
    eth0: {}
  vlans:
    eth0.10:
      id: 10
      link: eth0
      addresses:
        - 192.168.10.100/24
      gateway4: 192.168.10.1
      nameservers:
        addresses:
          - 8.8.8.8
    eth0.20:
      id: 20
      link: eth0
      addresses:
        - 192.168.20.100/24
```

**Apply:**

```bash
sudo netplan try
```

### Method B: ifupdown (traditional)

**File:** `/etc/network/interfaces`

```bash
# Physical interface (no IP address - just the parent)
auto eth0
iface eth0 inet manual

# VLAN 10
auto eth0.10
iface eth0.10 inet static
    address 192.168.10.100
    netmask 255.255.255.0
    gateway 192.168.10.1
    vlan-raw-device eth0

# VLAN 20
auto eth0.20
iface eth0.20 inet static
    address 192.168.20.100
    netmask 255.255.255.0
    vlan-raw-device eth0
```

**Apply:**

```bash
sudo systemctl restart networking
```

### Method C: systemd-networkd

**File:** `/etc/systemd/network/eth0.10.netdev`

```ini
[NetDev]
Name=eth0.10
Kind=vlan

[VLAN]
Id=10
```

**File:** `/etc/systemd/network/eth0.10.network`

```ini
[Match]
Name=eth0.10

[Network]
Address=192.168.10.100/24
Gateway=192.168.10.1
DNS=8.8.8.8
```

**File:** `/etc/systemd/network/eth0.20.netdev`

```ini
[NetDev]
Name=eth0.20
Kind=vlan

[VLAN]
Id=20
```

**File:** `/etc/systemd/network/eth0.20.network`

```ini
[Match]
Name=eth0.20

[Network]
Address=192.168.20.100/24
```

**Apply:**

```bash
sudo systemctl restart systemd-networkd
```

---

## PART 9: BONDING + VLANS TOGETHER (ADVANCED)

You can combine bonding and VLANs! This is common for high-availability servers.

**The stack:**

```
Physical interfaces: eth0 + eth1
         ↓
Bond interface: bond0 (both cables)
         ↓
VLANs on bond: bond0.10, bond0.20, bond0.30
         ↓
IP addresses on each VLAN
```

### Netplan example (bonding + VLANs)

**File:** `/etc/netplan/02-bonding-vlans.yaml`

```yaml
network:
  version: 2
  renderer: networkd
  bonds:
    bond0:
      interfaces:
        - eth0
        - eth1
      parameters:
        mode: 802.3ad
        lacp-rate: fast
  ethernets:
    eth0: {}
    eth1: {}
  vlans:
    bond0.10:
      id: 10
      link: bond0
      addresses:
        - 192.168.10.100/24
      gateway4: 192.168.10.1
    bond0.20:
      id: 20
      link: bond0
      addresses:
        - 192.168.20.100/24
    bond0.30:
      id: 30
      link: bond0
      addresses:
        - 192.168.30.100/24
```

**What this gives you:**

- Redundancy (bond0 - two cables)
- Increased bandwidth (mode 4 LACP)
- Network isolation (VLANs)
- All on ONE physical server

---

## PART 10: TROUBLESHOOTING BONDS AND VLANS

### Bonding problem: Bond not coming up

**Check if bonding module is loaded:**

```bash
lsmod | grep bonding
```

**If not loaded:**

```bash
sudo modprobe bonding
echo "bonding" | sudo tee -a /etc/modules
```

### Bonding problem: Link failures not detected

**Check MII monitoring:**

```bash
cat /proc/net/bonding/bond0 | grep "MII Polling Interval"
```

**If 0:** Monitoring is disabled. Add `miimon=100` to your configuration.

### VLAN problem: Can't see other devices on same VLAN

**Check VLAN exists:**

```bash
ip link show eth0.10
```

**Check VLAN tag is being added:**

```bash
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -e vlan
```

**Look for:**

```
vlan 10, p 0, M 0, ethertype IPv4
```

This confirms the VLAN tag is being added.

### VLAN problem: Parent interface must be up

The physical interface (eth0) must be UP for VLANs to work.

```bash
ip link set eth0 up
```

**Note:** eth0 does NOT need an IP address. It just needs to be UP.

---

## PART 11: PRACTICAL LABS

### Lab 1: Create a simple bond (mode 1 - active-backup)

**Requirements:** Two network cables connected (can be to same switch or different switches)

```bash
# Step 1: Load bonding module
sudo modprobe bonding

# Step 2: Create bond
sudo ip link add bond0 type bond mode 1
sudo ip link set bond0 up

# Step 3: Add interfaces
sudo ip link set eth0 down
sudo ip link set eth1 down
sudo ip link set eth0 master bond0
sudo ip link set eth1 master bond0
sudo ip link set eth0 up
sudo ip link set eth1 up

# Step 4: Configure IP
sudo ip addr add 192.168.1.200/24 dev bond0

# Step 5: Test
ping 192.168.1.1

# Step 6: Check status
cat /proc/net/bonding/bond0

# Step 7: Test failover
sudo ip link set eth0 down
cat /proc/net/bonding/bond0 | grep "Currently Active Slave"
# Should show eth1
```

### Lab 2: Create VLANs on a single interface

```bash
# Step 1: Load VLAN module
sudo modprobe 8021q

# Step 2: Create VLAN interfaces
sudo ip link add link eth0 name eth0.100 type vlan id 100
sudo ip link add link eth0 name eth0.200 type vlan id 200

# Step 3: Bring them up
sudo ip link set eth0.100 up
sudo ip link set eth0.200 up

# Step 4: Assign IPs
sudo ip addr add 10.0.100.10/24 dev eth0.100
sudo ip addr add 10.0.200.10/24 dev eth0.200

# Step 5: Verify
ip addr show eth0.100
ip addr show eth0.200

# Step 6: See VLANs
cat /proc/net/vlan/config
```

---

## SUMMARY OF TOPIC 11

| Concept                | One-sentence explanation                                               |
| ---------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Bonding                | Combining multiple network interfaces into one logical interface       |
| Mode 1 (active-backup) | One active, one backup. Redundancy only. Works with any switch.        |
| Mode 4 (802.3ad/LACP)  | All interfaces active. Speed + redundancy. Requires managed switch.    |
| VLAN                   | Splitting one physical network into multiple isolated virtual networks |
| VLAN tag               | Number (1-4094) that identifies which VLAN a packet belongs to         |
| Trunk port             | Switch port that carries multiple VLANs (connected to server)          |
| Access port            | Switch port that carries ONE VLAN (connected to regular PC)            |
| `bond0`                | Common name for bond interface                                         |
| `eth0.10`              | VLAN 10 on top of eth0                                                 |

### Bonding modes quick reference

| Mode | Name           | Redundancy | Speed Increase      | Switch Required |
| ---- | -------------- | ---------- | ------------------- | --------------- |
| 0    | balance-rr     | Yes        | Yes                 | Yes (static)    |
| 1    | active-backup  | Yes        | No                  | No              |
| 2    | balance-xor    | Yes        | Yes                 | Yes (static)    |
| 3    | broadcast      | Yes        | No (all interfaces) | No              |
| 4    | 802.3ad (LACP) | Yes        | Yes                 | Yes (LACP)      |
| 5    | balance-tlb    | Yes        | Yes (TX only)       | No              |
| 6    | balance-alb    | Yes        | Yes (TX + RX)       | No              |

### Common bonding commands

| What you want                 | Command                                                 |
| ----------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- |
| Check bond status             | `cat /proc/net/bonding/bond0`                           |
| List bonds                    | `ip link show type bond`                                |
| Which interfaces are in bond? | `ip link show \| grep master`                           |
| Force failover (mode 1)       | `echo eth1 > /sys/class/net/bond0/bonding/active_slave` |

### Common VLAN commands

| What you want         | Command                                                   |
| --------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------- |
| Load VLAN module      | `sudo modprobe 8021q`                                     |
| Create VLAN           | `sudo ip link add link eth0 name eth0.10 type vlan id 10` |
| List VLANs            | `cat /proc/net/vlan/config`                               |
| Delete VLAN           | `sudo ip link delete eth0.10`                             |
| See VLAN tags on wire | `sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -e vlan`                            |

---

# TOPIC 12: LINUX BRIDGE & VIRTUAL MACHINES

## Before we start

I will **never assume you know anything**. Every concept, every command, every file is explained from absolute zero.

Your mother is watching. I will not fail.

---

## PART 1: WHAT IS A LINUX BRIDGE?

### The problem a bridge solves

You have:

- A physical computer running Linux (called the "host")
- Virtual machines (VMs) running on that host (like KVM, VirtualBox, QEMU)

**Question:** How do VMs talk to:

- Each other?
- The host computer?
- The physical network (internet)?

**Answer:** A **Linux bridge** connects virtual interfaces together, just like a physical network switch connects physical computers.

### Analogy: Virtual switch

| Physical world           | Virtual world (Linux)                             |
| ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------- |
| Physical Ethernet switch | Linux bridge                                      |
| Physical computer        | Virtual machine                                   |
| Network cable            | Virtual Ethernet cable (veth pair, tap interface) |
| Switch port              | Bridge port                                       |

A Linux bridge is a **software switch** inside your Linux kernel.

### Visual diagram

```
                    PHYSICAL WORLD
                    ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
                    │            Physical Switch          │
                    │                                     │
                    │  Port 1    Port 2    Port 3    Port 4│
                    └─────┬───────┬─────────┬───────┬─────┘
                          │       │         │       │
                    Cable │       │         │       │ Cable
                          │       │         │       │
                    ┌─────┴───────┴─────────┴───────┴─────┐
                    │         Physical Computer           │
                    │         (Linux Host)                │
                    │  ┌─────────────────────────────┐    │
                    │  │      Linux Bridge (br0)     │    │
                    │  │  ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐   │    │
                    │  │  │tap0│ │tap1│ │eth0│   │    │
                    │  │  └──┬──┘ └──┬──┘ └──┬──┘   │    │
                    │  └─────┼───────┼───────┼───────┘    │
                    │        │       │       │            │
                    │    ┌───┴───┐ ┌───┴───┐ │            │
                    │    │ VM 1  │ │ VM 2  │ │            │
                    │    └───────┘ └───────┘ │            │
                    │                        │            │
                    │                  Physical cable     │
                    │                  to physical switch │
                    └─────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**What the diagram shows:**

- `eth0` = Physical network card (connected to real switch)
- `tap0`, `tap1` = Virtual interfaces for VMs
- `br0` = Linux bridge connecting them all
- VMs can talk to each other, the host, and the physical network

---

## PART 2: WHY USE A BRIDGE?

### Option 1: No bridge (default for some VM software)

Each VM gets its own virtual network (like 10.0.0.0/24). VMs can talk to each other but NOT to the physical network.

**Problem:** VMs cannot reach the internet or your physical network.

### Option 2: NAT (Network Address Translation)

The host acts like a router. VMs get private IPs (like 192.168.122.x). The host forwards traffic.

**Problem:**

- VMs are hidden behind the host
- Cannot easily access VMs from the physical network
- Port forwarding required

### Option 3: Bridge (BEST for most servers)

The bridge acts like a physical switch. VMs get IP addresses **on the same network as the host**.

**Benefits:**

- VMs appear as normal devices on your physical network
- Other computers can directly access VMs
- DHCP works normally (router gives IPs to VMs)
- No port forwarding needed

### Comparison

| Feature                         | No bridge | NAT                          | Bridge       |
| ------------------------------- | --------- | ---------------------------- | ------------ |
| VMs talk to each other          | Yes       | Yes                          | Yes          |
| VMs talk to host                | No        | Yes                          | Yes          |
| VMs talk to physical network    | No        | Yes (with NAT)               | Yes (direct) |
| Physical network can access VMs | No        | No (without port forwarding) | Yes          |
| VM gets IP from physical DHCP   | No        | No                           | Yes          |
| Setup complexity                | Simple    | Medium                       | Medium       |

---

## PART 3: BRIDGE COMPONENTS

### Component 1: The bridge itself (`br0`)

This is the virtual switch. It has no physical hardware. It just forwards packets between its ports.

```bash
# Create a bridge
sudo ip link add name br0 type bridge
```

### Component 2: Physical interface (`eth0`)

This is your real network card. You add it to the bridge so the bridge can reach the physical network.

```bash
# Add physical interface to bridge
sudo ip link set eth0 master br0
```

**Important:** When you add `eth0` to a bridge, `eth0` loses its IP address. The bridge gets the IP address instead.

### Component 3: Virtual interfaces (`tap0`, `vnet0`, etc.)

These are the "cables" connecting VMs to the bridge. When you create a VM, it creates one of these.

- **TAP interface** (`tap0`, `tap1`): Emulates a Ethernet device at layer 2. Most common for VMs.
- **VNET interface** (`vnet0`, `vnet1`): Used by KVM/libvirt.

### Component 4: Bridge ports

Any interface added to the bridge becomes a "port" on the virtual switch.

```bash
# View bridge ports
bridge link show
```

---

## PART 4: CREATING A BRIDGE (TEMPORARY - COMMAND LINE)

### Step 1: Create the bridge

```bash
# Create bridge called br0
sudo ip link add name br0 type bridge
```

### Step 2: Add physical interface to bridge

```bash
# First, bring eth0 down
sudo ip link set eth0 down

# Add it to the bridge
sudo ip link set eth0 master br0

# Bring eth0 back up
sudo ip link set eth0 up
```

**At this point, eth0 has no IP address. Your network connection is broken (if you're connected via SSH over eth0, you will be disconnected).**

### Step 3: Configure the bridge with an IP

```bash
# Give br0 the IP address that eth0 used to have
sudo ip addr add 192.168.1.100/24 dev br0

# Bring br0 up
sudo ip link set br0 up

# Add default gateway (if needed)
sudo ip route add default via 192.168.1.1 dev br0
```

### Step 4: Verify

```bash
# Check bridge exists
ip link show br0

# Check bridge ports
bridge link show

# Check IP address
ip addr show br0
```

### Step 5: Create a TAP interface for a VM

```bash
# Create TAP interface (requires tunctl or ip tuntap)
sudo ip tuntap add tap0 mode tap

# Add it to bridge
sudo ip link set tap0 master br0

# Bring it up
sudo ip link set tap0 up
```

Now you can attach a VM to `tap0`.

---

## PART 5: PERMANENT BRIDGE CONFIGURATION

### Method A: Netplan (Ubuntu 18.04+)

**File:** `/etc/netplan/02-bridge.yaml`

```yaml
network:
  version: 2
  renderer: networkd
  ethernets:
    eth0: {}
  bridges:
    br0:
      interfaces:
        - eth0
      addresses:
        - 192.168.1.100/24
      gateway4: 192.168.1.1
      nameservers:
        addresses:
          - 8.8.8.8
          - 8.8.4.4
      parameters:
        stp: true
        forward-delay: 4
```

**Let me explain EVERY piece:**

| Line               | Meaning                                          |
| ------------------ | ------------------------------------------------ |
| `bridges:`         | We are configuring a bridge                      |
| `br0:`             | Name of the bridge                               |
| `interfaces:`      | Which physical interfaces are part of the bridge |
| `- eth0`           | eth0 is a port on the bridge                     |
| `addresses:`       | IP address for the bridge (not for eth0)         |
| `gateway4:`        | Default gateway                                  |
| `nameservers:`     | DNS servers                                      |
| `stp: true`        | Spanning Tree Protocol (prevents network loops)  |
| `forward-delay: 4` | Wait 4 seconds before forwarding (STP safety)    |

**Apply:**

```bash
sudo netplan try
```

### Method B: ifupdown (traditional Debian/Ubuntu)

**File:** `/etc/network/interfaces`

```bash
# Physical interface (no IP - part of bridge)
auto eth0
iface eth0 inet manual

# Bridge interface
auto br0
iface br0 inet static
    address 192.168.1.100
    netmask 255.255.255.0
    gateway 192.168.1.1
    dns-nameservers 8.8.8.8 8.8.4.4
    bridge_ports eth0
    bridge_stp on
    bridge_fd 4
```

**Let me explain bridge-specific options:**

| Option              | Meaning                            |
| ------------------- | ---------------------------------- |
| `bridge_ports eth0` | Which interfaces are in the bridge |
| `bridge_stp on`     | Enable Spanning Tree Protocol      |
| `bridge_fd 4`       | Forward delay (seconds)            |

**Apply:**

```bash
sudo systemctl restart networking
```

### Method C: systemd-networkd

**File:** `/etc/systemd/network/br0.netdev`

```ini
[NetDev]
Name=br0
Kind=bridge

[Bridge]
STP=true
ForwardDelaySec=4
```

**File:** `/etc/systemd/network/br0.network`

```ini
[Match]
Name=br0

[Network]
Address=192.168.1.100/24
Gateway=192.168.1.1
DNS=8.8.8.8
DNS=8.8.4.4
```

**File:** `/etc/systemd/network/eth0.network`

```ini
[Match]
Name=eth0

[Network]
Bridge=br0
```

**Apply:**

```bash
sudo systemctl restart systemd-networkd
```

---

## PART 6: WORKING WITH LIBVIRT (KVM)

If you use KVM with libvirt (the most common VM management on Linux), libvirt can create bridges for you automatically.

### Option 1: Use libvirt's default bridge (NAT)

By default, libvirt creates a bridge called `virbr0` with NAT. VMs get IPs in 192.168.122.x range.

```bash
# List libvirt networks
virsh net-list --all

# Typical output:
# Name                 State      Autostart
# default              active     yes
```

### Option 2: Create a bridged network (VMs on physical network)

**Step 1: Create bridge definition file**

**File:** `/etc/libvirt/qemu/networks/bridged-network.xml`

```xml
<network>
  <name>bridged-network</name>
  <forward mode="bridge"/>
  <bridge name="br0"/>
</network>
```

**Step 2: Define and start the network**

```bash
# Define the network
sudo virsh net-define /etc/libvirt/qemu/networks/bridged-network.xml

# Start it
sudo virsh net-start bridged-network

# Make it autostart
sudo virsh net-autostart bridged-network
```

**Step 3: Tell VMs to use this network**

In virt-manager or `virsh edit VMNAME`, set the network to `bridged-network`.

### Option 3: Use an existing Linux bridge with libvirt

If you already created `br0` manually, tell libvirt to use it:

```bash
# Edit libvirt's qemu configuration
sudo nano /etc/libvirt/qemu.conf
```

Find and uncomment/modify:

```
user = "root"
group = "root"
```

Then edit your VM's XML:

```bash
virsh edit vm-name
```

Change the network section to:

```xml
<interface type='bridge'>
  <source bridge='br0'/>
  <model type='virtio'/>
</interface>
```

---

## PART 7: TROUBLESHOOTING BRIDGES

### Problem 1: Lost network connection after creating bridge (SSH disconnected)

**Symptom:** You were connected via SSH to eth0, then you added eth0 to a bridge, and now you're disconnected.

**Why:** eth0 lost its IP address when added to the bridge.

**Fix (if you have physical/console access):**

```bash
# Give br0 the IP address
sudo ip addr add 192.168.1.100/24 dev br0
sudo ip link set br0 up
sudo ip route add default via 192.168.1.1 dev br0
```

**Prevention:** Do bridge configuration over physical console or use `netplan try` which auto-reverts if you lose connection.

### Problem 2: Bridge exists but VMs cannot reach network

**Check bridge ports:**

```bash
bridge link show
```

**Look for:**

```
5: eth0: <BROADCAST,MULTICAST,UP,LOWER_UP> mtu 1500 master br0 state forwarding
6: tap0: <BROADCAST,MULTICAST,UP> mtu 1500 master br0 state forwarding
```

**If state is `disabled` or `blocking`:** STP is blocking the port. Wait for forward delay (4 seconds) or check for network loops.

**Check if bridge has an IP:**

```bash
ip addr show br0
```

### Problem 3: VMs can talk to each other but not to physical network

**Check if eth0 is in the bridge:**

```bash
bridge link show | grep eth0
```

**Check if physical switch port allows the MAC addresses of VMs:**

Some switches limit the number of MAC addresses per port. Your VMs each have their own MAC address. The switch may see multiple MACs on the same physical port and block them.

**Fix on switch:** Enable "MAC address learning" or increase the MAC limit.

### Problem 4: Bridge works but very slow

**Check for STP issues:**

```bash
# View STP status
brctl showstp br0
```

**If ports are flapping between blocking/forwarding:** You may have a network loop. Check cabling.

**Temporary workaround (not recommended for production):**

```bash
# Disable STP
sudo ip link set br0 type bridge stp_state 0
```

### Problem 5: Cannot delete bridge

**Check if interfaces are still attached:**

```bash
bridge link show
```

**Remove all interfaces first:**

```bash
sudo ip link set eth0 nomaster
sudo ip link set tap0 nomaster
```

**Then delete the bridge:**

```bash
sudo ip link delete br0
```

---

## PART 8: BRIDGE COMMANDS REFERENCE

| Command                                 | What it does                  |
| --------------------------------------- | ----------------------------- |
| `sudo ip link add name br0 type bridge` | Create bridge br0             |
| `sudo ip link set eth0 master br0`      | Add eth0 to bridge            |
| `sudo ip link set eth0 nomaster`        | Remove eth0 from bridge       |
| `bridge link show`                      | Show all bridge ports         |
| `bridge link show dev br0`              | Show ports on specific bridge |
| `brctl show`                            | Show bridges (old command)    |
| `brctl showstp br0`                     | Show STP status (old command) |
| `ip link delete br0`                    | Delete bridge                 |
| `sudo ip tuntap add tap0 mode tap`      | Create TAP interface          |
| `sudo ip tuntap del tap0 mode tap`      | Delete TAP interface          |

### Old commands vs new commands

| Old command (deprecated) | New command                        |
| ------------------------ | ---------------------------------- |
| `brctl addbr br0`        | `ip link add name br0 type bridge` |
| `brctl addif br0 eth0`   | `ip link set eth0 master br0`      |
| `brctl delif br0 eth0`   | `ip link set eth0 nomaster`        |
| `brctl show`             | `bridge link show`                 |
| `brctl showstp br0`      | `bridge link show dev br0`         |

You will still see `brctl` in older tutorials. Learn both.

---

## PART 9: PRACTICAL LABS

### Lab 1: Create a bridge (on a local machine or VM)

**WARNING:** If doing this over SSH, you will lose connection. Do this on a physical machine or local VM.

```bash
# Step 1: Check current IP
ip addr show eth0
# Write down your IP (e.g., 192.168.1.100)

# Step 2: Create bridge
sudo ip link add name br0 type bridge

# Step 3: Add eth0 to bridge
sudo ip link set eth0 master br0

# Step 4: Move IP to bridge
sudo ip addr add 192.168.1.100/24 dev br0
sudo ip link set br0 up

# Step 5: Remove old IP from eth0
sudo ip addr del 192.168.1.100/24 dev eth0

# Step 6: Add default gateway (if needed)
sudo ip route add default via 192.168.1.1 dev br0

# Step 7: Verify
ip addr show br0
bridge link show
ping 8.8.8.8
```

### Lab 2: Create a TAP interface and add to bridge

```bash
# Step 1: Create TAP interface
sudo ip tuntap add tap0 mode tap

# Step 2: Add to bridge
sudo ip link set tap0 master br0

# Step 3: Bring it up
sudo ip link set tap0 up

# Step 4: Verify
bridge link show

# Output should show tap0 as part of br0

# Step 5: Delete TAP interface when done
sudo ip link set tap0 nomaster
sudo ip tuntap del tap0 mode tap
```

### Lab 3: Simulate a VM using a TAP interface

You can simulate a VM using `socat` or by creating a network namespace:

```bash
# Step 1: Create tap0 and add to bridge
sudo ip tuntap add tap0 mode tap
sudo ip link set tap0 master br0
sudo ip link set tap0 up

# Step 2: Give tap0 an IP (simulating VM)
sudo ip addr add 192.168.1.200/24 dev tap0

# Step 3: Now tap0 (acting as a "VM") can talk to the network
ping 192.168.1.1

# Step 4: Clean up
sudo ip addr del 192.168.1.200/24 dev tap0
sudo ip link set tap0 nomaster
sudo ip tuntap del tap0 mode tap
```

---

## SUMMARY OF TOPIC 12

| Concept       | One-sentence explanation                               |
| ------------- | ------------------------------------------------------ |
| Linux bridge  | Virtual switch inside Linux kernel                     |
| Bridge port   | Interface attached to the bridge (physical or virtual) |
| TAP interface | Virtual Ethernet interface for VMs                     |
| `br0`         | Common name for the main bridge                        |
| STP           | Spanning Tree Protocol (prevents network loops)        |
| `eth0`        | Physical interface (becomes a port on the bridge)      |

### Bridge vs other options

| Option    | Use case                                                       |
| --------- | -------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Bridge    | VMs need to be on the same network as physical computers       |
| NAT       | VMs need internet but should be isolated from physical network |
| No bridge | Testing, isolated VMs                                          |

### When to use a bridge

| Scenario                                     | Use bridge?                      |
| -------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------- |
| Server running multiple public-facing VMs    | YES                              |
| Home lab where VMs need IPs from your router | YES                              |
| Testing malware in isolation                 | NO (use NAT or isolated network) |
| Developer testing on laptop                  | Maybe (NAT is simpler)           |

### Common bridge commands

| What you want                | Command                                 |
| ---------------------------- | --------------------------------------- |
| Create bridge                | `sudo ip link add name br0 type bridge` |
| Add interface to bridge      | `sudo ip link set eth0 master br0`      |
| Remove interface from bridge | `sudo ip link set eth0 nomaster`        |
| List bridge ports            | `bridge link show`                      |
| Delete bridge                | `sudo ip link delete br0`               |
| Create TAP interface         | `sudo ip tuntap add tap0 mode tap`      |
| Delete TAP interface         | `sudo ip tuntap del tap0 mode tap`      |
