# TOPIC 13: ADVANCED ROUTING & POLICY ROUTING

## Before we start

I will **never assume you know anything**. Every concept, every command, every file is explained from absolute zero.

Your mother is watching. I will not fail.

---

## PART 1: WHAT IS POLICY ROUTING?

### The problem policy routing solves

Normal routing (what we learned in Topic 6) uses **only the destination IP address** to decide where to send a packet.

**Normal routing decision:**

> "Where is 8.8.8.8 going? Send it to the default gateway."

But sometimes, destination IP alone is not enough. You need to make routing decisions based on:

| Additional factor      | Example                                                              |
| ---------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Source IP**          | "Traffic from 10.0.0.5 uses ISP A, traffic from 10.0.0.6 uses ISP B" |
| **Incoming interface** | "Traffic from eth0 goes out eth1, traffic from eth2 goes out eth3"   |
| **Protocol/port**      | "Web traffic (port 80) uses cheap ISP, SSH traffic uses fast ISP"    |
| **Packet size**        | "Small packets go one way, large packets another"                    |

**Policy routing** = Routing based on MORE than just destination IP.

### Analogy: Mail delivery

| Normal routing                           | Policy routing                                                              |
| ---------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Mail sorted by destination ZIP code only | Mail sorted by: who sent it, how big it is, what's inside, when it was sent |

### Why do you need this?

| Use case                      | Explanation                                                 |
| ----------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Two internet connections**  | ISP A for normal traffic, ISP B for VoIP/video              |
| **VPN split tunneling**       | Work traffic goes through VPN, personal traffic goes direct |
| **Traffic from specific IPs** | VIPs get faster/better routes                               |
| **Load balancing**            | Distribute traffic across multiple ISPs                     |
| **Failover**                  | If ISP A fails, switch to ISP B                             |

---

## PART 2: NORMAL ROUTING vs POLICY ROUTING

### Normal routing (single routing table)

```
                    ┌─────────────────┐
                    │   Main Routing  │
                    │      Table      │
                    └────────┬────────┘
                             │
                    ┌────────┴────────┐
                    │ Destination IP  │
                    │ 8.8.8.8?        │
                    └────────┬────────┘
                             │
                    ┌────────┴────────┐
                    │ Send to default │
                    │   gateway       │
                    └─────────────────┘
```

**Limitation:** ONE table. ONE set of rules. All traffic follows the same decision process.

### Policy routing (multiple routing tables)

```
                    ┌─────────────────┐
                    │   Routing       │
                    │   Policy        │
                    │   Database      │
                    └────────┬────────┘
                             │
                    ┌────────┴────────┐
                    │ Source IP?      │
                    │ Interface?      │
                    │ Port?           │
                    └────────┬────────┘
                             │
         ┌───────────────────┼───────────────────┐
         │                   │                   │
    ┌────┴────┐         ┌────┴────┐         ┌────┴────┐
    │ Table   │         │ Table   │         │ Table   │
    │ 100     │         │ 200     │         │ Main    │
    │ (ISP A) │         │ (ISP B) │         │         │
    └─────────┘         └─────────┘         └─────────┘
```

**Power:** You can have MANY routing tables and choose which one to use based on custom rules.

---

## PART 3: LINUX ROUTING TABLES (THE NUMBERS)

### Default tables

Linux has 255 possible routing tables (0-254). Three are pre-defined:

| Table ID | Name      | Purpose                                                       |
| -------- | --------- | ------------------------------------------------------------- |
| 255      | `local`   | Local routes (127.0.0.1, broadcast addresses) - DO NOT TOUCH  |
| 254      | `main`    | The "normal" routing table (where `ip route show` reads from) |
| 253      | `default` | Used for some advanced scenarios                              |

### View all tables

```bash
# View all routing tables
ip route show table all

# View specific table by name
ip route show table main

# View specific table by number
ip route show table 100
```

### Table naming

You can give tables human-readable names in `/etc/iproute2/rt_tables`

```bash
cat /etc/iproute2/rt_tables
```

**Typical contents:**

```
#
# reserved values
#
255     local
254     main
253     default
0       unspec
#
# local
#
#1      inr.ruhep
```

**Adding custom table names:**

```bash
# Edit the file
sudo nano /etc/iproute2/rt_tables
```

Add these lines:

```
100     isp1
200     isp2
150     vpn
```

Now you can refer to table `isp1` instead of table `100`.

---

## PART 4: POLICY ROUTING COMPONENTS

### Component 1: Multiple routing tables

Instead of one table, you create multiple tables, each with different routes.

**Table 100 (ISP A):**

```bash
sudo ip route add default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0 table 100
```

**Table 200 (ISP B):**

```bash
sudo ip route add default via 10.0.0.1 dev eth1 table 200
```

### Component 2: Routing policy database (RPDB)

The RPDB contains rules that say: "For traffic matching X, use table Y".

```bash
# View all rules
ip rule show
```

**Typical output:**

```
0:      from all lookup local
32766:  from all lookup main
32767:  from all lookup default
```

**What this means:**

| Rule priority | Condition | Action                                |
| ------------- | --------- | ------------------------------------- |
| 0             | from all  | use table `local` (highest priority)  |
| 32766         | from all  | use table `main` (normal routing)     |
| 32767         | from all  | use table `default` (lowest priority) |

**How it works:**

1. Kernel checks rules in order (lowest number = highest priority)
2. First rule that matches, uses the specified table
3. If no route found in that table, continues to next rule

### Component 3: Rules

Rules have three parts:

```
PRIORITY: from CONDITION to CONDITION iif INTERFACE oif INTERFACE lookup TABLE
```

| Part     | Example              | Meaning                        |
| -------- | -------------------- | ------------------------------ |
| PRIORITY | `100`                | Lower number = higher priority |
| from     | `from 192.168.1.100` | Source IP address              |
| to       | `to 8.8.8.8`         | Destination IP address         |
| iif      | `iif eth0`           | Incoming interface             |
| oif      | `oif eth1`           | Outgoing interface             |
| lookup   | `lookup 100`         | Which table to use             |

---

## PART 5: BASIC POLICY ROUTING EXAMPLES

### Example 1: Source-based routing (two ISPs)

**Scenario:** You have two internet connections:

- ISP A (eth0, gateway 192.168.1.1)
- ISP B (eth1, gateway 10.0.0.1)

Traffic from IP 192.168.100.10 uses ISP A.
Traffic from IP 192.168.100.20 uses ISP B.

**Step 1: Add routes to custom tables**

```bash
# Table 100 (ISP A)
sudo ip route add default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0 table 100
sudo ip route add 192.168.100.0/24 dev eth0 table 100

# Table 200 (ISP B)
sudo ip route add default via 10.0.0.1 dev eth1 table 200
sudo ip route add 192.168.100.0/24 dev eth1 table 200
```

**Step 2: Add rules to select table based on source IP**

```bash
# Traffic from 192.168.100.10 uses table 100 (ISP A)
sudo ip rule add from 192.168.100.10 lookup 100 priority 100

# Traffic from 192.168.100.20 uses table 200 (ISP B)
sudo ip rule add from 192.168.100.20 lookup 200 priority 200

# All other traffic uses main table (default)
# (main table already has default rule priority 32766)
```

**Step 3: Verify**

```bash
# Check rules
ip rule show

# Test which table would be used
ip route get 8.8.8.8 from 192.168.100.10
# Should show table 100

ip route get 8.8.8.8 from 192.168.100.20
# Should show table 200
```

### Example 2: Interface-based routing (VPN)

**Scenario:** Traffic coming in on eth0 (LAN) goes out normally.
Traffic coming in on tun0 (VPN) goes back out tun0.

```bash
# Table 150 (VPN)
sudo ip route add default via 10.8.0.1 dev tun0 table 150

# Rule: Traffic from VPN interface uses VPN table
sudo ip rule add iif tun0 lookup 150 priority 150

# Traffic from LAN uses main table (already has rule)
```

### Example 3: Port-based routing

**Scenario:**

- Web traffic (port 80, 443) uses fast, expensive ISP (eth0)
- Everything else uses cheap, slow ISP (eth1)

```bash
# Table 100 (Fast ISP)
sudo ip route add default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0 table 100

# Table 200 (Cheap ISP)
sudo ip route add default via 10.0.0.1 dev eth1 table 200

# Rules: Match on source port (using iptables and marks)
# Step 1: Mark packets from web server
sudo iptables -t mangle -A OUTPUT -p tcp --sport 80 -j MARK --set-mark 1
sudo iptables -t mangle -A OUTPUT -p tcp --sport 443 -j MARK --set-mark 1

# Step 2: Route marked packets using table 100
sudo ip rule add fwmark 1 lookup 100 priority 100

# All other packets use main table (which points to table 200)
sudo ip route add default via 10.0.0.1 dev eth1
```

**Let me explain marks:**

- `fwmark` = firewall mark (a number you attach to a packet)
- `iptables -t mangle` = special table for packet modification
- `--set-mark 1` = attach mark "1" to the packet
- `ip rule add fwmark 1` = route packets with mark 1 using table 100

---

## PART 6: MULTI-WAN LOAD BALANCING

### Scenario: Two ISPs, both used simultaneously

```
ISP A (eth0): 192.168.1.1, bandwidth 100 Mbps
ISP B (eth1): 10.0.0.1, bandwidth 100 Mbps
Total: 200 Mbps
```

### Step 1: Create tables for each ISP

```bash
# Table 100 (ISP A)
sudo ip route add default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0 table 100
sudo ip route add 192.168.1.0/24 dev eth0 table 100

# Table 200 (ISP B)
sudo ip route add default via 10.0.0.1 dev eth1 table 200
sudo ip route add 10.0.0.0/24 dev eth1 table 200
```

### Step 2: Add equal-cost multipath (ECMP) route to main table

```bash
# Add both default routes with equal weight
sudo ip route add default scope global \
    nexthop via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0 weight 1 \
    nexthop via 10.0.0.1 dev eth1 weight 1
```

**What this does:**

- Linux will distribute traffic across both gateways
- `weight 1` = equal distribution (1:1 ratio)
- For 100 Mbps + 50 Mbps, use `weight 2:1`

### Step 3: Connection tracking (prevent broken connections)

When load balancing, you want the same connection to use the same ISP.

```bash
# Use iptables to mark connections, not just packets
sudo iptables -t mangle -A OUTPUT -m state --state NEW -j CONNMARK --restore-mark
sudo iptables -t mangle -A OUTPUT -m state --state NEW -m statistic --mode nth --every 2 --packet 0 -j MARK --set-mark 1
sudo iptables -t mangle -A OUTPUT -m state --state NEW -m statistic --mode nth --every 2 --packet 1 -j MARK --set-mark 2
sudo iptables -t mangle -A OUTPUT -j CONNMARK --save-mark

# Route based on mark
sudo ip rule add fwmark 1 table 100 priority 100
sudo ip rule add fwmark 2 table 200 priority 200
```

---

## PART 7: ISP FAILOVER (WAN REDUNDANCY)

### Scenario: Use ISP A normally. If ISP A fails, use ISP B.

### Step 1: Create tables

```bash
# Table 100 (Primary - ISP A)
sudo ip route add default via 192.168.1.1 dev eth0 table 100

# Table 200 (Backup - ISP B)
sudo ip route add default via 10.0.0.1 dev eth1 table 200
```

### Step 2: Add rule to use primary by default

```bash
# All traffic uses table 100 (ISP A) by default
sudo ip rule add from all lookup 100 priority 100
```

### Step 3: Script to detect failure and switch

**Create failover script:** `/usr/local/bin/wan-failover.sh`

```bash
#!/bin/bash

PRIMARY_GW="192.168.1.1"
BACKUP_GW="10.0.0.1"
PRIMARY_TABLE=100
BACKUP_TABLE=200

# Check if primary gateway is reachable
if ping -c 3 -W 2 $PRIMARY_GW > /dev/null 2>&1; then
    # Primary is up
    if ip rule show | grep -q "lookup $PRIMARY_TABLE priority 100"; then
        # Already using primary, do nothing
        exit 0
    else
        # Switch back to primary
        ip rule del priority 100 2>/dev/null
        ip rule add from all lookup $PRIMARY_TABLE priority 100
        logger "WAN: Switched back to primary gateway $PRIMARY_GW"
    fi
else
    # Primary is down
    if ip rule show | grep -q "lookup $BACKUP_TABLE priority 100"; then
        # Already using backup, do nothing
        exit 0
    else
        # Switch to backup
        ip rule del priority 100 2>/dev/null
        ip rule add from all lookup $BACKUP_TABLE priority 100
        logger "WAN: Primary down! Switched to backup gateway $BACKUP_GW"
    fi
fi
```

**Run every 30 seconds via cron:**

```bash
sudo crontab -e
```

Add:

```
*/30 * * * * * /usr/local/bin/wan-failover.sh
```

---

## PART 8: PERMANENT POLICY ROUTING CONFIGURATION

### Method: NetworkManager (easiest for desktops)

NetworkManager supports policy routing via connection profiles:

```bash
# Create two connections
nmcli con add type ethernet con-name "ISP-A" ifname eth0
nmcli con add type ethernet con-name "ISP-B" ifname eth1

# Set routing table for ISP-A
nmcli con mod "ISP-A" ipv4.route-table 100
nmcli con mod "ISP-A" ipv4.routing-rules "priority 100 from 192.168.100.10 table 100"

# Set routing table for ISP-B
nmcli con mod "ISP-B" ipv4.route-table 200
nmcli con mod "ISP-B" ipv4.routing-rules "priority 200 from 192.168.100.20 table 200"
```

### Method: systemd-networkd

**File:** `/etc/systemd/network/10-eth0.network`

```ini
[Match]
Name=eth0

[Network]
Address=192.168.1.100/24
Gateway=192.168.1.1

[Route]
Destination=0.0.0.0/0
Gateway=192.168.1.1
Table=100

[RoutingPolicyRule]
From=192.168.100.10
Table=100
Priority=100
```

**File:** `/etc/systemd/network/20-eth1.network`

```ini
[Match]
Name=eth1

[Network]
Address=10.0.0.100/24
Gateway=10.0.0.1

[Route]
Destination=0.0.0.0/0
Gateway=10.0.0.1
Table=200

[RoutingPolicyRule]
From=192.168.100.20
Table=200
Priority=200
```

### Method: Netplan (Ubuntu)

**File:** `/etc/netplan/03-policy-routing.yaml`

```yaml
network:
  version: 2
  renderer: networkd
  ethernets:
    eth0:
      addresses:
        - 192.168.1.100/24
      routes:
        - to: default
          via: 192.168.1.1
          table: 100
      routing-policy:
        - from: 192.168.100.10
          table: 100
          priority: 100
    eth1:
      addresses:
        - 10.0.0.100/24
      routes:
        - to: default
          via: 10.0.0.1
          table: 200
      routing-policy:
        - from: 192.168.100.20
          table: 200
          priority: 200
```

---

## PART 9: COMMON POLICY ROUTING COMMANDS

| Command                                      | What it does                                  |
| -------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------- |
| `ip rule show`                               | List all routing rules                        |
| `ip rule add from 10.0.0.5 lookup 100`       | Add rule: source 10.0.0.5 uses table 100      |
| `ip rule add to 8.8.8.8 lookup 200`          | Add rule: destination 8.8.8.8 uses table 200  |
| `ip rule add iif eth0 lookup 150`            | Add rule: incoming on eth0 uses table 150     |
| `ip rule add fwmark 1 lookup 100`            | Add rule: packets marked with 1 use table 100 |
| `ip rule del priority 100`                   | Delete rule with priority 100                 |
| `ip rule flush`                              | Delete all rules (except default)             |
| `ip route show table 100`                    | Show routes in table 100                      |
| `ip route add default via 1.1.1.1 table 100` | Add default route to table 100                |

### Rule priorities

| Priority range | Typical use                      |
| -------------- | -------------------------------- |
| 0-1000         | User-defined high priority rules |
| 1000-2000      | VPN rules                        |
| 2000-3000      | ISP selection rules              |
| 3000-32766     | Lower priority rules             |
| 32766          | Default main table               |
| 32767          | Default fallback table           |

---

## PART 10: TROUBLESHOOTING POLICY ROUTING

### Problem 1: Rule not matching

**Check rule order:**

```bash
ip rule show
```

Rules are checked in **priority order** (lowest number first). If a higher priority rule matches, lower priority rules are never checked.

**Debug with:**

```bash
# Trace route decision
ip route get 8.8.8.8 from 192.168.100.10

# Show which table was used
ip route get 8.8.8.8 from 192.168.100.10 | grep table
```

### Problem 2: Asymmetric routing

**Symptom:** Traffic goes out ISP A but returns via ISP B (causes connection issues).

**Fix:** Ensure return traffic uses the same ISP.

```bash
# Use iptables to mark return traffic based on incoming interface
sudo iptables -t mangle -A PREROUTING -i eth0 -j MARK --set-mark 100
sudo iptables -t mangle -A PREROUTING -i eth1 -j MARK --set-mark 200

# Route based on mark
sudo ip rule add fwmark 100 lookup 100
sudo ip rule add fwmark 200 lookup 200
```

### Problem 3: VPN routes not working

**Check if rule exists:**

```bash
ip rule show | grep tun0
```

**Check if table has routes:**

```bash
ip route show table 150
```

**Check if interface is up:**

```bash
ip link show tun0
```

---

## PART 11: PRACTICAL LABS

### Lab 1: Source-based routing (simulate with namespaces)

```bash
# Create two network namespaces (simulating two computers)
sudo ip netns add ns1
sudo ip netns add ns2

# Create veth pairs to connect namespaces to bridge
sudo ip link add veth1 type veth peer name veth1-br
sudo ip link add veth2 type veth peer name veth2-br

# Move one end to each namespace
sudo ip link set veth1 netns ns1
sudo ip link set veth2 netns ns2

# Create bridge
sudo ip link add br0 type bridge
sudo ip link set br0 up

# Connect bridge to namespaces
sudo ip link set veth1-br master br0
sudo ip link set veth2-br master br0
sudo ip link set veth1-br up
sudo ip link set veth2-br up

# Give bridge an IP
sudo ip addr add 192.168.100.1/24 dev br0

# Configure namespace IPs
sudo ip netns exec ns1 ip addr add 192.168.100.10/24 dev veth1
sudo ip netns exec ns1 ip link set veth1 up
sudo ip netns exec ns2 ip addr add 192.168.100.20/24 dev veth2
sudo ip netns exec ns2 ip link set veth2 up

# Add policy routing based on source IP
sudo ip rule add from 192.168.100.10 lookup 100 priority 100
sudo ip rule add from 192.168.100.20 lookup 200 priority 200

# Add routes to tables
sudo ip route add default dev br0 table 100
sudo ip route add default dev br0 table 200

# Test
sudo ip netns exec ns1 ip route get 8.8.8.8
```

### Lab 2: Add and delete rules

```bash
# List current rules
ip rule show

# Add a rule
sudo ip rule add from 10.0.0.5 lookup 100 priority 1000

# Verify
ip rule show

# Delete rule
sudo ip rule del priority 1000

# Verify it's gone
ip rule show
```

---

## SUMMARY OF TOPIC 13

| Concept                        | One-sentence explanation                                                       |
| ------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Policy routing                 | Routing based on more than just destination IP (source, interface, port, etc.) |
| Routing table                  | Collection of routes (main, default, plus custom ones)                         |
| Routing policy database (RPDB) | List of rules that determine which table to use                                |
| Rule priority                  | Lower number = higher priority (checked first)                                 |
| `ip rule`                      | Command to manage policy routing rules                                         |
| `fwmark`                       | Firewall mark (number attached to a packet for routing decisions)              |
| ECMP                           | Equal Cost Multi-Path (load balancing across multiple gateways)                |

### Rule matching order

```
Priority 0:   table local (always first)
Priority 100: Custom rule - from 192.168.1.10 lookup 100
Priority 200: Custom rule - from 192.168.1.20 lookup 200
Priority 32766: from all lookup main (normal routing)
Priority 32767: from all lookup default (fallback)
```

### When to use policy routing

| Scenario                        | Solution                         |
| ------------------------------- | -------------------------------- |
| Two ISPs, different source IPs  | Source-based rules               |
| VPN split tunneling             | Interface-based rules (iif tun0) |
| QoS: prioritize certain traffic | Port or fwmark-based rules       |
| Load balancing                  | ECMP or weighted rules           |
| Failover                        | Script + rule replacement        |

### Common pitfalls

| Pitfall              | Prevention                                              |
| -------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- |
| Asymmetric routing   | Ensure return path matches forward path                 |
| Rules too broad      | Use specific conditions and priorities                  |
| Missing table routes | Verify each table has routes for local networks         |
| Rules lost on reboot | Use permanent configuration (Netplan, systemd-networkd) |

---

# TOPIC 14: NETWORK NAMESPACES & CONTAINER NETWORKING

## Before we start

I will **never assume you know anything**. Every concept, every command, every file is explained from absolute zero.

Your mother is watching. I will not fail.

---

## PART 1: WHAT IS A NETWORK NAMESPACE?

### The problem namespaces solve

You have one Linux computer. You want to run multiple programs that each need their own:

- Network interfaces (eth0, eth1, etc.)
- IP addresses
- Routing tables
- Firewall rules
- ARP tables
- Ports (two programs both wanting port 80)

**Without namespaces:** Everything conflicts. Only one program can use port 80. Only one routing table exists.

**With namespaces:** Each program gets its OWN isolated copy of the entire network stack.

### Analogy: Apartment buildings

| Without namespaces                 | With namespaces                        |
| ---------------------------------- | -------------------------------------- |
| One house with one address         | Apartment building with separate units |
| One door number (port 80)          | Each apartment has its own door number |
| One mailbox (routing table)        | Each apartment has its own mailbox     |
| One phone line (network interface) | Each apartment has its own phone       |

**A network namespace is like a virtual computer inside your real computer.**

### What each namespace has (ISOLATED)

| Component                     | Is it isolated?                                  |
| ----------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------ |
| Network interfaces (eth0, lo) | YES - each namespace has its own                 |
| IP addresses                  | YES - same IP can exist in different namespaces  |
| Routing table                 | YES - each namespace has its own `ip route`      |
| ARP table                     | YES - each namespace has its own `ip neigh`      |
| Firewall rules (iptables)     | PARTIALLY (we'll explain)                        |
| Port numbers                  | YES - port 80 can be used in multiple namespaces |

### What is NOT isolated

- Physical hardware (the actual network card)
- Kernel modules
- System-wide resources (CPU, RAM, disk - though other namespaces exist for those)

---

## PART 2: WHY ARE NAMESPACES IMPORTANT?

### Container networking (Docker, Podman, LXC)

Every container gets its own network namespace. That's why you can run 10 containers all using port 80 on the same host.

```
                    ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
                    │           Linux Host                    │
                    │                                         │
                    │  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐       │
                    │  │ Namespace 1 │  │ Namespace 2 │       │
                    │  │ (Container) │  │ (Container) │       │
                    │  │             │  │             │       │
                    │  │ IP: 10.0.0.1│  │ IP: 10.0.0.2│       │
                    │  │ Port 80     │  │ Port 80     │       │
                    │  └──────┬──────┘  └──────┬──────┘       │
                    │         │                │               │
                    │         └───────┬────────┘               │
                    │                 │                        │
                    │         ┌───────┴───────┐                │
                    │         │ Linux Bridge │                │
                    │         │   (docker0)  │                │
                    │         └───────┬───────┘                │
                    │                 │                        │
                    │         ┌───────┴───────┐                │
                    │         │   eth0 (host) │                │
                    │         │ 192.168.1.100 │                │
                    │         └───────┬───────┘                │
                    └─────────────────┼─────────────────────────┘
                                      │
                                Physical Network
```

### Virtual machines (KVM, VirtualBox)

Each VM gets its own network namespace (though VMs also have isolated kernels).

### Network troubleshooting

You can create temporary namespaces to test network configurations without affecting your main system.

### Security isolation

Run untrusted programs in a namespace where they cannot see or affect your main network.

---

## PART 3: WORKING WITH NETWORK NAMESPACES

### View all namespaces

```bash
# List all network namespaces
ip netns list

# If empty, no namespaces exist
```

### Create a namespace

```bash
# Create a namespace called "blue"
sudo ip netns add blue

# Create another called "red"
sudo ip netns add red

# Verify they exist
ip netns list
```

**Output:**

```
blue
red
```

### Run commands inside a namespace

```bash
# Run a command inside the blue namespace
sudo ip netns exec blue ip link show

# Run bash inside the blue namespace (get a shell)
sudo ip netns exec blue bash

# Now you are in the blue namespace's shell
# Any network commands you run affect ONLY blue
ip link show
ip addr show
exit  # to leave
```

### What does a new namespace look like?

```bash
sudo ip netns exec blue ip link show
```

**Output:**

```
1: lo: <LOOPBACK> mtu 65536 qdisc noop state DOWN mode DEFAULT
```

**Notice:**

- Only the loopback interface (`lo`) exists
- It is DOWN (not working)
- No eth0, no wlan0, no IP addresses

**A new namespace is completely empty.** You must create everything yourself.

### Bring up loopback inside namespace

```bash
sudo ip netns exec blue ip link set lo up
```

### Add a virtual Ethernet (veth) pair

A **veth pair** is like a virtual cable with two ends. You put one end in one namespace, the other end in another namespace (or the host).

```bash
# Create a veth pair: veth-blue and veth-blue-br
sudo ip link add veth-blue type veth peer name veth-blue-br

# Move one end into the blue namespace
sudo ip link set veth-blue netns blue

# Now:
# - veth-blue is INSIDE the blue namespace
# - veth-blue-br is on the HOST (default namespace)

# Rename inside namespace (optional, easier to remember)
sudo ip netns exec blue ip link set veth-blue name eth0

# Bring up both ends
sudo ip netns exec blue ip link set eth0 up
sudo ip link set veth-blue-br up
```

### Assign IP addresses

```bash
# Inside blue namespace, give eth0 an IP
sudo ip netns exec blue ip addr add 10.0.0.1/24 dev eth0

# On the host, give veth-blue-br an IP (if you want host to talk to blue)
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.2/24 dev veth-blue-br
```

### Test connectivity

```bash
# From host, ping blue
ping 10.0.0.1

# From blue, ping host
sudo ip netns exec blue ping 10.0.0.2

# From blue, ping something on the internet (will fail - no route)
sudo ip netns exec blue ping 8.8.8.8
```

---

## PART 4: CONNECTING NAMESPACES TO EACH OTHER

### Two namespaces connected directly (veth pair)

```bash
# Create namespaces
sudo ip netns add red
sudo ip netns add blue

# Create veth pair connecting them
sudo ip link add veth-red type veth peer name veth-blue

# Move ends to namespaces
sudo ip link set veth-red netns red
sudo ip link set veth-blue netns blue

# Rename inside namespaces
sudo ip netns exec red ip link set veth-red name eth0
sudo ip netns exec blue ip link set veth-blue name eth0

# Bring up interfaces
sudo ip netns exec red ip link set eth0 up
sudo ip netns exec blue ip link set eth0 up
sudo ip netns exec red ip link set lo up
sudo ip netns exec blue ip link set lo up

# Assign IPs (they must be on the same network)
sudo ip netns exec red ip addr add 10.0.0.1/24 dev eth0
sudo ip netns exec blue ip addr add 10.0.0.2/24 dev eth0

# Test
sudo ip netns exec red ping 10.0.0.2
```

**Visual:**

```
┌─────────────┐      ┌─────────────┐
│   red       │      │   blue      │
│  namespace  │      │  namespace  │
│             │      │             │
│  eth0       │      │  eth0       │
│  10.0.0.1   │      │  10.0.0.2   │
└──────┬──────┘      └──────┬──────┘
       │                    │
       └──────────┬─────────┘
                  │
            veth pair
         (virtual cable)
```

### Multiple namespaces connected via a bridge

```bash
# Create bridge on host
sudo ip link add br0 type bridge
sudo ip link set br0 up

# Create three namespaces
for ns in red blue green; do
    sudo ip netns add $ns

    # Create veth pair
    sudo ip link add veth-$ns type veth peer name veth-$ns-br

    # Move one end to namespace
    sudo ip link set veth-$ns netns $ns

    # Rename inside namespace to eth0
    sudo ip netns exec $ns ip link set veth-$ns name eth0

    # Connect other end to bridge
    sudo ip link set veth-$ns-br master br0
    sudo ip link set veth-$ns-br up

    # Bring up interfaces in namespace
    sudo ip netns exec $ns ip link set eth0 up
    sudo ip netns exec $ns ip link set lo up

    # Assign IPs (all on same network)
    sudo ip netns exec $ns ip addr add 10.0.0.$((100 + $(echo $ns | tr 'rgb' '123'))) /24 dev eth0
done

# Give bridge an IP (optional, for host to talk to namespaces)
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.254/24 dev br0

# Test
sudo ip netns exec red ping 10.0.0.101   # blue
sudo ip netns exec red ping 10.0.0.102   # green
```

**Visual:**

```
┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐
│   red   │ │  blue   │ │  green  │
│10.0.0.1 │ │10.0.0.2 │ │10.0.0.3 │
└────┬────┘ └────┬────┘ └────┬────┘
     │           │           │
     │           │           │
  ┌──┴───────────┴───────────┴──┐
  │      Linux Bridge (br0)     │
  └──────────────┬──────────────┘
                 │
         ┌───────┴───────┐
         │   Host (eth0) │
         └───────────────┘
```

---

## PART 5: CONNECTING NAMESPACES TO THE OUTSIDE WORLD

### Problem: Namespace cannot reach internet

Your namespace has an IP (10.0.0.1) but no route to 8.8.8.8.

### Solution 1: NAT (like Docker)

```bash
# Step 1: Give namespace a default route through the host's bridge
sudo ip netns exec red ip route add default via 10.0.0.254

# Step 2: Enable IP forwarding on host
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.ip_forward=1

# Step 3: Add NAT rule on host (masquerade traffic from namespace)
sudo iptables -t nat -A POSTROUTING -s 10.0.0.0/24 -o eth0 -j MASQUERADE

# Step 4: Allow forwarding (if default policy is DROP)
sudo iptables -A FORWARD -i br0 -o eth0 -j ACCEPT
sudo iptables -A FORWARD -i eth0 -o br0 -j ACCEPT

# Step 5: Test from namespace
sudo ip netns exec red ping 8.8.8.8
```

### Solution 2: Direct routing (namespace has real IP on physical network)

```bash
# Give namespace an IP from your physical network (192.168.1.x)
sudo ip netns exec red ip addr add 192.168.1.200/24 dev eth0

# Add default route through your real router
sudo ip netns exec red ip route add default via 192.168.1.1

# Now namespace can talk directly to physical network
# No NAT needed! But your physical switch must accept the MAC address.
```

---

## PART 6: DELETING AND CLEANING UP

### Delete a namespace

```bash
# Delete namespace (removes all interfaces inside it)
sudo ip netns del red
sudo ip netns del blue
sudo ip netns del green
```

### Delete veth pairs on host

When you delete a namespace, the veth ends inside it are automatically removed. The host ends remain.

```bash
# Remove host-side veth ends
sudo ip link delete veth-red-br
sudo ip link delete veth-blue-br
```

### Delete bridge

```bash
sudo ip link delete br0
```

### Complete cleanup script

```bash
#!/bin/bash
for ns in red blue green; do
    sudo ip netns del $ns 2>/dev/null
done

for veth in $(ip link show | grep "veth.*-br" | cut -d: -f2 | awk '{print $2}'); do
    sudo ip link delete $veth 2>/dev/null
done

sudo ip link delete br0 2>/dev/null

echo "Cleanup complete"
```

---

## PART 7: DOCKER NETWORKING (HOW IT WORKS UNDER THE HOOD)

### Docker's default network (bridge)

When you install Docker, it creates:

```bash
# Check Docker's network namespace (not visible with ip netns list by default)
docker run -d --name nginx nginx

# Docker puts containers in their own network namespaces
# Docker creates a bridge called docker0

ip link show docker0
```

**Docker's setup:**

1. Creates bridge `docker0` (usually 172.17.0.1/16)
2. For each container:
   - Creates veth pair
   - One end in container (eth0)
   - Other end in `docker0` bridge
   - Assigns IP from 172.17.0.0/16 range
   - Sets up NAT to let containers reach internet

### How to see Docker's network namespaces

```bash
# Docker container IDs
docker ps -q

# Find container's PID
docker inspect --format '{{.State.Pid}}' container_name

# Create symlink to see namespace (docker hides them by default)
sudo ln -s /proc/$(docker inspect --format '{{.State.Pid}}' container_name)/ns/net /var/run/netns/container_name

# Now you can see it
ip netns list

# Execute commands inside container's namespace
sudo ip netns exec container_name ip addr show
```

---

## PART 8: PRACTICAL LABS

### Lab 1: Create and explore a namespace

```bash
# Step 1: Create namespace
sudo ip netns add lab1

# Step 2: List namespaces
ip netns list

# Step 3: Look inside (no interfaces except lo)
sudo ip netns exec lab1 ip link show

# Step 4: Bring up loopback
sudo ip netns exec lab1 ip link set lo up

# Step 5: Try to ping (will fail - no default route)
sudo ip netns exec lab1 ping 8.8.8.8

# Step 6: Exit (we'll delete it later)
sudo ip netns del lab1
```

### Lab 2: Two namespaces communicating

```bash
# Step 1: Create namespaces
sudo ip netns add alice
sudo ip netns add bob

# Step 2: Create veth pair
sudo ip link add veth-alice type veth peer name veth-bob

# Step 3: Move ends to namespaces
sudo ip link set veth-alice netns alice
sudo ip link set veth-bob netns bob

# Step 4: Rename to eth0
sudo ip netns exec alice ip link set veth-alice name eth0
sudo ip netns exec bob ip link set veth-bob name eth0

# Step 5: Bring up interfaces
sudo ip netns exec alice ip link set lo up
sudo ip netns exec alice ip link set eth0 up
sudo ip netns exec bob ip link set lo up
sudo ip netns exec bob ip link set eth0 up

# Step 6: Assign IPs
sudo ip netns exec alice ip addr add 10.0.0.1/24 dev eth0
sudo ip netns exec bob ip addr add 10.0.0.2/24 dev eth0

# Step 7: Test communication
sudo ip netns exec alice ping 10.0.0.2

# Step 8: Clean up
sudo ip netns del alice
sudo ip netns del bob
```

### Lab 3: Namespace with internet access (NAT)

```bash
# Step 1: Create namespace
sudo ip netns ns internet

# Step 2: Create veth pair
sudo ip link add veth-ns type veth peer name veth-ns-br
sudo ip link set veth-ns netns internet
sudo ip netns exec internet ip link set veth-ns name eth0

# Step 3: Create bridge (or use docker0 if exists)
sudo ip link add br-ns type bridge
sudo ip link set br-ns up

# Step 4: Connect to bridge
sudo ip link set veth-ns-br master br-ns
sudo ip link set veth-ns-br up

# Step 5: Configure namespace
sudo ip netns exec internet ip link set lo up
sudo ip netns exec internet ip link set eth0 up
sudo ip netns exec internet ip addr add 172.20.0.2/24 dev eth0

# Step 6: Give bridge an IP
sudo ip addr add 172.20.0.1/24 dev br-ns

# Step 7: Add default route in namespace
sudo ip netns exec internet ip route add default via 172.20.0.1

# Step 8: Enable IP forwarding
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.ip_forward=1

# Step 9: Add NAT
sudo iptables -t nat -A POSTROUTING -s 172.20.0.0/24 -o eth0 -j MASQUERADE
sudo iptables -A FORWARD -i br-ns -o eth0 -j ACCEPT
sudo iptables -A FORWARD -i eth0 -o br-ns -j ACCEPT

# Step 10: Test
sudo ip netns exec internet ping 8.8.8.8

# Step 11: Clean up
sudo ip netns del internet
sudo ip link delete br-ns
sudo iptables -t nat -D POSTROUTING -s 172.20.0.0/24 -o eth0 -j MASQUERADE
```

---

## PART 9: TROUBLESHOOTING NAMESPACES

### Problem 1: "Cannot assign requested address"

```bash
sudo ip netns exec blue ip addr add 10.0.0.1/24 dev eth0
# Error: Cannot assign requested address
```

**Cause:** Interface `eth0` doesn't exist or is DOWN.

**Fix:**

```bash
# Check if interface exists
sudo ip netns exec blue ip link show

# Bring it up
sudo ip netns exec blue ip link set eth0 up
```

### Problem 2: Ping works one way but not the other

```bash
# Alice can ping Bob, but Bob cannot ping Alice
```

**Cause:** Firewall or missing return route.

**Fix:** Check Bob's routing table:

```bash
sudo ip netns exec bob ip route show
```

If Bob doesn't have a route back to Alice's network, add it.

### Problem 3: Cannot see namespace with `ip netns list`

```bash
ip netns list
# (empty) but you created namespaces
```

**Cause:** Namespace files were deleted or not created properly.

**Fix:** Namespaces are stored in `/var/run/netns/`. Check if the directory exists and has files.

### Problem 4: Docker containers not showing in `ip netns list`

**Cause:** Docker hides its namespaces by default.

**Fix:**

```bash
# Find container PID
docker inspect --format '{{.State.Pid}}' container_name

# Create symlink
sudo ln -s /proc/PID/ns/net /var/run/netns/container_name

# Now it appears
ip netns list
```

---

## SUMMARY OF TOPIC 14

| Concept              | One-sentence explanation                       |
| -------------------- | ---------------------------------------------- |
| Network namespace    | Isolated copy of the entire network stack      |
| veth pair            | Virtual Ethernet cable (two connected ends)    |
| `ip netns`           | Command to manage network namespaces           |
| `ip netns exec`      | Run a command inside a namespace               |
| Container networking | Each container gets its own network namespace  |
| NAT                  | Allow namespace to reach internet through host |

### Namespace commands quick reference

| Command                        | What it does               |
| ------------------------------ | -------------------------- |
| `sudo ip netns add NAME`       | Create namespace           |
| `sudo ip netns del NAME`       | Delete namespace           |
| `ip netns list`                | List all namespaces        |
| `sudo ip netns exec NAME CMD`  | Run CMD inside namespace   |
| `sudo ip netns exec NAME bash` | Get shell inside namespace |

### veth commands quick reference

| Command                                           | What it does                |
| ------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------- |
| `sudo ip link add A type veth peer name B`        | Create veth pair            |
| `sudo ip link set A netns NAME`                   | Move interface to namespace |
| `sudo ip netns exec NAME ip link set A name eth0` | Rename inside namespace     |
| `sudo ip link set A master BRIDGE`                | Add to bridge               |

### When to use network namespaces

| Use case                    | Why                                           |
| --------------------------- | --------------------------------------------- |
| Container runtimes (Docker) | Isolate containers from each other and host   |
| Testing network config      | Test without affecting production             |
| Security isolation          | Run untrusted code in isolated network        |
| VPN split tunneling         | Specific applications use VPN, others don't   |
| Learning networking         | Create entire virtual networks on one machine |

---

# TOPIC 15: TRAFFIC CONTROL (TC) & QUALITY OF SERVICE (QoS)

## Before we start

I will **never assume you know anything**. Every concept, every command, every number is explained from absolute zero.

Your mother is watching. I will not fail.

---

## PART 1: WHAT IS TRAFFIC CONTROL?

### The problem traffic control solves

You have a network link (like your internet connection). Multiple programs want to send data at the same time.

**Without traffic control:**

- One program can use ALL the bandwidth
- Other programs get nothing
- Important traffic (like SSH) slows down because a download is happening

**With traffic control:** You can manage bandwidth, prioritize traffic, add delays (for testing), and limit speeds.

### What you can do with TC

| Feature                | What it does                        | Example                               |
| ---------------------- | ----------------------------------- | ------------------------------------- |
| **Rate limiting**      | Limit bandwidth to a specific speed | Limit downloads to 1 Mbps             |
| **Prioritization**     | Important traffic goes first        | SSH > web browsing > downloads        |
| **Latency simulation** | Add artificial delay                | Simulate satellite link (500ms delay) |
| **Packet loss**        | Drop random packets                 | Test how your app handles loss        |
| **Traffic shaping**    | Smooth out bursts                   | Prevent "burst then idle" patterns    |

### Analogy: Highway traffic management

| Without TC                 | With TC                     |
| -------------------------- | --------------------------- |
| One lane, chaos            | Multiple lanes with rules   |
| Ambulance stuck in traffic | Emergency lane for priority |
| Rush hour gridlock         | HOV lanes, metered onramps  |
| No speed limits            | Speed limits enforced       |

---

## PART 2: TC COMPONENTS (THE BUILDING BLOCKS)

### Component 1: Queuing disciplines (qdisc)

A **qdisc** (queue discipline) is a rule that decides:

- Which order packets go out
- Which packets get dropped when the queue is full

Think of it like a **checkout line at a grocery store**:

| Type of queue                   | How it works                          | Analogy                           |
| ------------------------------- | ------------------------------------- | --------------------------------- |
| FIFO (First In First Out)       | First packet in, first packet out     | Regular checkout line             |
| PRIO (Priority)                 | High priority line always goes first  | Express lane + regular lane       |
| SFQ (Stochastic Fair Queuing)   | Round-robin between connections       | One person per register, rotates  |
| HTB (Hierarchical Token Bucket) | Multiple levels with bandwidth limits | Different lanes with speed limits |

### Component 2: Classes

A **class** is a sub-queue with its own rules and bandwidth limits.

**Analogy:** A highway with multiple lanes:

- Highway = qdisc (the overall queue)
- Lanes = classes (each with its own speed limit)
- Cars = packets

### Component 3: Filters

A **filter** decides which class a packet belongs to.

**Analogy:** A toll booth that directs cars to different lanes:

- Destination IP = which lane to use
- Source port = which lane to use
- Packet size = which lane to use

### The hierarchy

```
                         ┌─────────────────┐
                         │   Root qdisc    │
                         │   (on eth0)     │
                         └────────┬────────┘
                                  │
                    ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐
                    │             │             │
              ┌─────┴─────┐  ┌─────┴─────┐  ┌─────┴─────┐
              │ Class 1:  │  │ Class 2:  │  │ Class 3:  │
              │ SSH (50%) │  │ Web (30%) │  │ Other(20%)│
              └─────┬─────┘  └─────┬─────┘  └─────┬─────┘
                    │             │             │
              ┌─────┴─────┐  ┌─────┴─────┐  ┌─────┴─────┐
              │  qdisc   │  │  qdisc   │  │  qdisc   │
              │  (FIFO)  │  │  (SFQ)   │  │  (FIFO)  │
              └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘
```

**How packets flow:**

1. Packet arrives at root qdisc
2. Filter examines packet (source IP, port, etc.)
3. Filter sends packet to appropriate class
4. Class's internal qdisc queues the packet
5. Packet is sent out when bandwidth is available

---

## PART 3: BASIC TC COMMANDS

### View current qdiscs

```bash
# Show qdiscs on all interfaces
tc qdisc show

# Show on specific interface
tc qdisc show dev eth0

# Show with details
tc -s qdisc show dev eth0
```

### Typical output (no custom shaping)

```
qdisc noqueue 0: dev eth0 root refcnt 2
```

This means "no queue discipline" (packets go out immediately).

### View classes

```bash
tc class show dev eth0
```

### View filters

```bash
tc filter show dev eth0
```

### Delete all qdiscs (reset interface to normal)

```bash
# Remove all traffic control on eth0
sudo tc qdisc del dev eth0 root

# Verify it's gone
tc qdisc show dev eth0
```

---

## PART 4: SIMPLE RATE LIMITING

### Example 1: Limit outgoing bandwidth to 1 Mbps

```bash
# Add a TBF (Token Bucket Filter) qdisc
# limit = 1 mbit, burst = 100kb, latency = 50ms
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root tbf rate 1mbit burst 100kb latency 50ms
```

**Let me explain EVERY parameter:**

| Parameter | What it means                             | Example value                  |
| --------- | ----------------------------------------- | ------------------------------ |
| `tbf`     | Token Bucket Filter (simple rate limiter) |                                |
| `rate`    | Maximum speed                             | `1mbit` (1 megabit per second) |
| `burst`   | How much can be sent in a short burst     | `100kb` (100 kilobytes)        |
| `latency` | Maximum time a packet can wait            | `50ms` (50 milliseconds)       |

**How TBF works:**

- Tokens are added to a bucket at `rate` speed
- Each packet uses tokens equal to its size
- If bucket has enough tokens, packet goes immediately
- If not enough tokens, packet waits
- Bucket size = `burst` (can't store more than this)

**Units you can use:**

- `kbit` / `mbit` / `gbit` (kilobits, megabits, gigabits per second)
- `kbps` / `mbps` / `gbps` (same as above)
- `kb` / `mb` / `gb` (kilobytes, megabytes, gigabytes)
- `ms` / `s` (milliseconds, seconds)

### Example 2: Limit incoming bandwidth (ingress)

TC normally controls **outgoing** traffic. For incoming, you need to use `ingress`:

```bash
# Create ingress qdisc
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 ingress

# Add filter to limit incoming to 1 Mbps
sudo tc filter add dev eth0 parent ffff: protocol ip u32 match u32 0 0 police rate 1mbit burst 100kb drop
```

**Note:** Ingress policing drops packets (doesn't queue them). For more flexible incoming shaping, you need to shape on your router or switch.

### Example 3: Test your rate limit

```bash
# In one terminal, run a speed test (download a file)
wget http://speedtest.tele2.net/100MB.zip

# In another terminal, monitor traffic
tc -s qdisc show dev eth0

# Look for "dropped" or "overlimit" packets
```

---

## PART 5: PRIORITY QUEUING (PRIO)

### What PRIO does

PRIO creates multiple bands (queues). Band 0 has highest priority, band 2 has lowest.

**Traffic mapping:**

- Band 0: Always sent first
- Band 1: Sent only if band 0 is empty
- Band 2: Sent only if bands 0 and 1 are empty

### Example: Prioritize SSH over everything

```bash
# Step 1: Create PRIO qdisc with 3 bands
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root handle 1: prio bands 3

# Step 2: Set band priorities (0=highest, 2=lowest)
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 parent 1:1 handle 10: pfifo
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 parent 1:2 handle 20: pfifo
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 parent 1:3 handle 30: pfifo

# Step 3: Add filter to send SSH (port 22) to band 0 (highest)
sudo tc filter add dev eth0 protocol ip parent 1:0 prio 1 u32 match ip dport 22 0xffff flowid 1:1

# Step 4: Send HTTP (port 80) to band 1 (medium)
sudo tc filter add dev eth0 protocol ip parent 1:0 prio 2 u32 match ip dport 80 0xffff flowid 1:2

# Step 5: Everything else goes to band 2 (lowest - default)
```

**Let me explain the filter syntax:**

| Piece                      | Meaning                                                |
| -------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------ |
| `protocol ip`              | IPv4 packets                                           |
| `parent 1:0`               | Attach to root qdisc (handle 1)                        |
| `prio 1`                   | Priority of this filter (lower number = checked first) |
| `u32`                      | Match on arbitrary bits in packet                      |
| `match ip dport 22 0xffff` | Match destination port 22 (SSH)                        |
| `flowid 1:1`               | Send to class 1:1 (band 0)                             |

### Test priority queuing

```bash
# Generate high priority traffic (SSH) - actually using SSH itself
# Your SSH session already uses port 22, so it should get priority

# Generate low priority traffic (HTTP download)
curl -O http://speedtest.tele2.net/100MB.zip &

# Check statistics
tc -s class show dev eth0
```

---

## PART 6: HIERARCHICAL TOKEN BUCKET (HTB)

### What HTB does

HTB allows you to:

- Split bandwidth into multiple classes
- Assign guaranteed minimum bandwidth to each class
- Allow classes to borrow unused bandwidth

### Analogy: Office internet with departments

```
Total bandwidth: 100 Mbps

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                         HTB Root                             │
│                      Rate: 100 mbit                          │
│                     Ceil: 100 mbit                           │
│                                                              │
│    ┌────────────────┐  ┌────────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐ │
│    │  Engineering   │  │    Sales       │  │   Guest      │ │
│    │  Min: 40 mbit  │  │  Min: 30 mbit  │  │  Min: 10 mbit│ │
│    │  Max: 80 mbit  │  │  Max: 60 mbit  │  │  Max: 20 mbit│ │
│    └────────────────┘  └────────────────┘  └──────────────┘ │
│                                                              │
│  - Engineering gets 40 Mbps minimum, can use up to 80       │
│  - Sales gets 30 Mbps minimum, can use up to 60             │
│  - Guest gets 10 Mbps minimum, can use up to 20             │
│  - Unused bandwidth is shared                               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### Example: Three-class HTB

```bash
# Step 1: Create HTB root qdisc
# 1: is the handle (1:0 for root, 1:1, 1:2 for children)
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root handle 1: htb default 30

# Step 2: Add root class (total bandwidth)
sudo tc class add dev eth0 parent 1: classid 1:1 htb rate 100mbit ceil 100mbit

# Step 3: Add child classes
# Class 1:10 - SSH (high priority, guaranteed 1mbit, can use up to 5mbit)
sudo tc class add dev eth0 parent 1:1 classid 1:10 htb rate 1mbit ceil 5mbit prio 0

# Class 1:20 - Web (medium priority, guaranteed 20mbit, can use up to 50mbit)
sudo tc class add dev eth0 parent 1:1 classid 1:20 htb rate 20mbit ceil 50mbit prio 1

# Class 1:30 - Everything else (low priority, guaranteed 10mbit, rest unused)
sudo tc class add dev eth0 parent 1:1 classid 1:30 htb rate 10mbit ceil 100mbit prio 2

# Step 4: Add qdiscs to each class (FIFO is fine)
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 parent 1:10 handle 10: pfifo
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 parent 1:20 handle 20: pfifo
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 parent 1:30 handle 30: pfifo

# Step 5: Add filters to direct traffic
# SSH (port 22) -> class 1:10
sudo tc filter add dev eth0 parent 1: protocol ip prio 1 u32 match ip dport 22 0xffff flowid 1:10

# HTTP (port 80) and HTTPS (443) -> class 1:20
sudo tc filter add dev eth0 parent 1: protocol ip prio 2 u32 match ip dport 80 0xffff flowid 1:20
sudo tc filter add dev eth0 parent 1: protocol ip prio 2 u32 match ip dport 443 0xffff flowid 1:20

# Everything else -> class 1:30 (default)
```

**Let me explain HTB parameters:**

| Parameter | Meaning                                                |
| --------- | ------------------------------------------------------ |
| `rate`    | Guaranteed minimum bandwidth                           |
| `ceil`    | Maximum bandwidth (can go up to this if available)     |
| `prio`    | Lower number = higher priority for borrowing bandwidth |
| `parent`  | Parent class (1:1 is root class)                       |
| `classid` | Unique ID for this class (1:10 = handle 1, class 10)   |

### Understanding rate vs ceil

| Scenario                               | What happens                                            |
| -------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- |
| Total bandwidth used < total available | All classes get at least their `rate`                   |
| A class needs more than its `rate`     | It can borrow unused bandwidth up to `ceil`             |
| Multiple classes need more             | Priority (`prio`) decides who borrows first             |
| Total demand > total bandwidth         | `rate` guarantees are honored, other traffic is dropped |

---

## PART 7: LATENCY AND LOSS SIMULATION (FOR TESTING)

### Example 1: Add 100ms delay to all traffic

```bash
# Add netem (Network Emulator) qdisc
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root netem delay 100ms

# Test with ping
ping 8.8.8.8
# You'll see ~100ms extra latency
```

### Example 2: Add variable delay (jitter)

```bash
# 100ms delay ± 20ms variation
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root netem delay 100ms 20ms

# 100ms delay, 20ms jitter, 25% correlation (realistic)
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root netem delay 100ms 20ms 25%
```

### Example 3: Add packet loss

```bash
# Drop 5% of packets
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root netem loss 5%

# Drop 1% with 25% correlation (burst loss)
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root netem loss 1% 25%
```

### Example 4: Combine delay, jitter, and loss

```bash
# 100ms delay, 10ms jitter, 2% loss
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root netem delay 100ms 10ms loss 2%
```

### Example 5: Simulate satellite link

```bash
# Satellite: 500ms delay, 20ms jitter, 1% loss, 2% duplication
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root netem delay 500ms 20ms loss 1% duplicate 2%
```

### Example 6: Simulate slow ADSL line

```bash
# Rate limit to 10 Mbps with 50ms latency
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root handle 1: htb
sudo tc class add dev eth0 parent 1: classid 1:1 htb rate 10mbit
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 parent 1:1 handle 10: netem delay 50ms
```

### Test and remove

```bash
# Test
ping -c 10 8.8.8.8

# Remove simulation
sudo tc qdisc del dev eth0 root
```

---

## PART 8: TC COMMANDS REFERENCE

### Viewing

| Command                     | What it does                        |
| --------------------------- | ----------------------------------- |
| `tc qdisc show dev eth0`    | Show qdiscs on eth0                 |
| `tc -s qdisc show dev eth0` | Show with statistics (drops, bytes) |
| `tc class show dev eth0`    | Show classes                        |
| `tc -s class show dev eth0` | Show class statistics               |
| `tc filter show dev eth0`   | Show filters                        |

### Adding

| Command                                                                                 | What it does     |
| --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------- |
| `tc qdisc add dev eth0 root tbf rate 1mbit burst 10kb latency 50ms`                     | Rate limit       |
| `tc qdisc add dev eth0 root prio bands 3`                                               | Priority queuing |
| `tc qdisc add dev eth0 root htb`                                                        | HTB root         |
| `tc qdisc add dev eth0 root netem delay 100ms`                                          | Add latency      |
| `tc class add dev eth0 parent 1: classid 1:1 htb rate 100mbit`                          | HTB class        |
| `tc filter add dev eth0 parent 1:0 protocol ip u32 match ip dport 22 0xffff flowid 1:1` | Add filter       |

### Deleting

| Command                         | What it does          |
| ------------------------------- | --------------------- |
| `tc qdisc del dev eth0 root`    | Remove all TC on eth0 |
| `tc qdisc del dev eth0 ingress` | Remove ingress qdisc  |

### Changing

| Command                                                                | What it does |
| ---------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------ |
| `tc qdisc change dev eth0 root tbf rate 2mbit burst 20kb latency 50ms` | Change rate  |

---

## PART 9: PRACTICAL USE CASES

### Use case 1: Limit bandwidth for a specific IP (rate limit per user)

```bash
# Create HTB root
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root handle 1: htb default 999

# Default class (unlimited)
sudo tc class add dev eth0 parent 1: classid 1:999 htb rate 100mbit

# Class for IP 192.168.1.50 (limit to 5 Mbps)
sudo tc class add dev eth0 parent 1: classid 1:50 htb rate 5mbit ceil 5mbit

# Filter traffic from 192.168.1.50 to class 1:50
sudo tc filter add dev eth0 parent 1: protocol ip prio 1 u32 match ip src 192.168.1.50 flowid 1:50
```

### Use case 2: Guarantee bandwidth for VoIP

```bash
# Total: 100 Mbps
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root handle 1: htb default 30

sudo tc class add dev eth0 parent 1: classid 1:1 htb rate 100mbit

# VoIP (SIP + RTP) - ports 5060 (SIP), 10000-20000 (RTP)
# Guarantee 5 Mbps
sudo tc class add dev eth0 parent 1:1 classid 1:10 htb rate 5mbit ceil 10mbit prio 0

# Everything else
sudo tc class add dev eth0 parent 1:1 classid 1:20 htb rate 10mbit ceil 100mbit prio 1

# Filter VoIP
sudo tc filter add dev eth0 parent 1: protocol ip prio 1 u32 match ip dport 5060 0xffff flowid 1:10
sudo tc filter add dev eth0 parent 1: protocol ip prio 1 u32 match ip dport 10000 0xff00 flowid 1:10
```

### Use case 3: Burst control (prevent bursting)

```bash
# Without burst control:
# 10 seconds of 100 Mbps, 90 seconds of 0 Mbps (bursty)

# With burst control (smooth):
# 100 seconds at 10 Mbps (smooth)

# Limit to 10 Mbps average, max burst 50 Mbps for 1 second
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root tbf rate 10mbit burst 1mbit latency 10ms
```

### Use case 4: Testing application with high latency

```bash
# Add 200ms latency to all traffic
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root netem delay 200ms

# Run your application test

# Remove when done
sudo tc qdisc del dev eth0 root
```

---

## PART 10: TROUBLESHOOTING TC

### Problem 1: "RTNETLINK answers: No such file or directory"

```bash
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root tbf rate 1mbit burst 10kb latency 50ms
# Error: RTNETLINK answers: No such file or directory
```

**Cause:** Interface doesn't exist or module not loaded.

**Fix:**

```bash
# Check interface exists
ip link show eth0

# Load sch_htb module (if using HTB)
sudo modprobe sch_htb

# Load sch_netem module (if using netem)
sudo modprobe sch_netem
```

### Problem 2: Traffic not being shaped

**Check that qdisc exists:**

```bash
tc qdisc show dev eth0
```

**Check that filters are matching:**

```bash
# Add a trace to see which class packets go to
tc filter add dev eth0 parent 1: protocol ip prio 1 u32 match ip dport 22 0xffff action ok
```

**Check that traffic is actually going through the interface:**

```bash
# Monitor traffic
ip -s link show eth0
```

### Problem 3: Netem not working

```bash
# Check netem module is loaded
lsmod | grep netem

# Load if needed
sudo modprobe sch_netem
```

### Problem 4: TC rules disappear after reboot

TC rules are **temporary**. They must be reapplied after reboot.

**Make them permanent:**

```bash
# Save current TC configuration
tc qdisc show dev eth0 > /etc/tc-rules

# Create startup script
sudo nano /etc/network/if-up.d/tc-rules
```

```bash
#!/bin/bash
if [ "$IFACE" = "eth0" ]; then
    tc qdisc add dev eth0 root tbf rate 1mbit burst 10kb latency 50ms
fi
```

```bash
sudo chmod +x /etc/network/if-up.d/tc-rules
```

---

## PART 11: PRACTICAL LABS

### Lab 1: Basic rate limiting

```bash
# Step 1: Check current speed (download a file)
wget -O /dev/null http://speedtest.tele2.net/10MB.zip

# Step 2: Add rate limit (1 Mbps)
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root tbf rate 1mbit burst 10kb latency 50ms

# Step 3: Test speed again (should be ~1 Mbps)
wget -O /dev/null http://speedtest.tele2.net/10MB.zip

# Step 4: Remove limit
sudo tc qdisc del dev eth0 root

# Step 5: Test speed again (back to normal)
wget -O /dev/null http://speedtest.tele2.net/10MB.zip
```

### Lab 2: Latency simulation

```bash
# Step 1: Normal ping
ping -c 5 8.8.8.8

# Step 2: Add 200ms delay
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root netem delay 200ms

# Step 3: Ping again (200ms higher)
ping -c 5 8.8.8.8

# Step 4: Remove delay
sudo tc qdisc del dev eth0 root

# Step 5: Ping again (normal latency)
ping -c 5 8.8.8.8
```

### Lab 3: Priority queuing (requires generating traffic)

```bash
# Step 1: Create PRIO qdisc
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root handle 1: prio bands 3

# Step 2: Add FIFO qdiscs to each band
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 parent 1:1 handle 10: pfifo
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 parent 1:2 handle 20: pfifo
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 parent 1:3 handle 30: pfifo

# Step 3: Send SSH (port 22) to band 0
sudo tc filter add dev eth0 parent 1: protocol ip prio 1 u32 match ip dport 22 0xffff flowid 1:1

# Step 4: Monitor statistics
watch -n 1 'tc -s class show dev eth0'

# Step 5: Generate HTTP traffic (curl) and SSH traffic (your existing session)
# You'll see band 0 (SSH) has fewer drops than band 2

# Step 6: Clean up
sudo tc qdisc del dev eth0 root
```

## SUMMARY OF TOPIC 15

| Concept              | One-sentence explanation                                            |
| -------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Traffic Control (TC) | Manage bandwidth, prioritization, and latency on network interfaces |
| qdisc                | Queue discipline (rules for ordering packets)                       |
| TBF                  | Token Bucket Filter (simple rate limiter)                           |
| PRIO                 | Priority queuing (high priority traffic goes first)                 |
| HTB                  | Hierarchical Token Bucket (multiple classes with min/max bandwidth) |
| Netem                | Network Emulator (adds delay, loss, duplication)                    |
| Class                | Sub-queue with its own bandwidth limits                             |
| Filter               | Decides which class a packet belongs to                             |

### Quick reference: Which qdisc to use

| What you want                              | Use this qdisc |
| ------------------------------------------ | -------------- |
| Simple speed limit                         | TBF            |
| Prioritize some traffic over others        | PRIO           |
| Multiple classes with guaranteed bandwidth | HTB            |
| Add latency for testing                    | Netem          |
| Simulate packet loss                       | Netem          |

### Common TC patterns

| Task                 | Command                                                             |
| -------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Rate limit to 1 Mbps | `tc qdisc add dev eth0 root tbf rate 1mbit burst 10kb latency 50ms` |
| Add 100ms delay      | `tc qdisc add dev eth0 root netem delay 100ms`                      |
| Drop 5% packets      | `tc qdisc add dev eth0 root netem loss 5%`                          |
| Remove all TC        | `tc qdisc del dev eth0 root`                                        |
| View stats           | `tc -s qdisc show dev eth0`                                         |
