# TOPIC 16: NETWORK PERFORMANCE TUNING

## Before we start

I will **never assume you know anything**. Every concept, every command, every number is explained from absolute zero.

Your mother is watching. I will not fail.

---

## PART 1: WHY DO WE NEED NETWORK TUNING?

### The problem

Your network hardware (NIC) can handle 10 Gbps. Your CPU is fast. Your RAM is plentiful.

But your file transfer is only getting 100 Mbps. **Why?**

**Default Linux settings are optimized for general use, not for high performance.**

| Scenario                       | Default Linux    | Tuned Linux |
| ------------------------------ | ---------------- | ----------- |
| 10 Gbps file transfer          | 1-2 Gbps         | 9+ Gbps     |
| Database with many connections | Connection drops | Stable      |
| Real-time video streaming      | Buffering/lag    | Smooth      |
| High-latency satellite link    | Slow throughput  | Optimized   |

### What we can tune

| Tuning area                | What it does                                            |
| -------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Buffer sizes**           | How much data can be "in flight" before acknowledgement |
| **TCP congestion control** | How Linux reacts to network congestion                  |
| **Queue lengths**          | How many packets can wait before being dropped          |
| **NIC offloading**         | Let the network card do work (free CPU)                 |
| **Interrupt coalescing**   | Batch interrupts to reduce CPU load                     |

### Important warning

**Do not tune blindly.** The wrong settings can make performance WORSE. Change ONE setting at a time. Test before and after.

---

## PART 2: HOW LINUX NETWORK STACK WORKS (SIMPLIFIED)

### The path of a packet

```
Application (web server, database)
         │
         ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│  Socket buffer (application)    │  ← Tuning: net.core.rmem_default
└─────────────────────────────────┘
         │
         ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│  TCP buffer (kernel)            │  ← Tuning: net.ipv4.tcp_rmem
└─────────────────────────────────┘
         │
         ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│  Queue (waiting for NIC)        │  ← Tuning: net.core.netdev_max_backlog
└─────────────────────────────────┘
         │
         ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│  Network Interface Card (NIC)   │  ← Tuning: ring buffers, offloading
└─────────────────────────────────┘
         │
         ▼
      Wire
```

### Two key concepts: Buffers and Windows

**Buffer:** Memory where Linux stores packets waiting to be processed.

**Window:** The amount of data a sender can send before receiving an acknowledgement.

**Analogy: Restaurant**

| Concept | Analogy                                                          |
| ------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Buffer  | Kitchen size (how many orders can be in progress)                |
| Window  | How many dishes the chef can send before waiter confirms receipt |

If the kitchen is too small (small buffer), orders get dropped. If the chef waits for confirmation after every dish (small window), service is slow.

---

## PART 3: VIEWING CURRENT SETTINGS

### All network settings are in `/proc/sys/net/`

```bash
# View all TCP-related settings
sysctl -a | grep tcp

# View specific setting
sysctl net.ipv4.tcp_rmem

# View memory settings
sysctl net.core.rmem_default
sysctl net.core.rmem_max
```

### Understanding the output format

```bash
sysctl net.ipv4.tcp_rmem
```

**Output:**

```
net.ipv4.tcp_rmem = 4096 131072 6291456
```

**Three numbers:**
| Position | Name | Meaning |
|----------|------|---------|
| First | min | Minimum buffer size (bytes) |
| Second | default | Default buffer size (bytes) |
| Third | max | Maximum buffer size (bytes) |

---

## PART 4: BUFFER SIZES (THE MOST IMPORTANT TUNING)

### The Bandwidth-Delay Product (BDP)

**BDP** tells you how much data can be "in flight" between two computers.

**Formula:**

```
BDP = Bandwidth × Round Trip Time (RTT)
```

**Example 1 (fast local network):**

- Bandwidth: 1 Gbps = 125,000,000 bytes/sec
- RTT: 0.5 ms = 0.0005 sec
- BDP = 125,000,000 × 0.0005 = 62,500 bytes (61 KB)

**Example 2 (high-latency long-distance):**

- Bandwidth: 100 Mbps = 12,500,000 bytes/sec
- RTT: 150 ms = 0.15 sec
- BDP = 12,500,000 × 0.15 = 1,875,000 bytes (1.8 MB)

**Your buffer must be AT LEAST the BDP to achieve full speed.**

### Check your RTT

```bash
# Ping to see RTT (round trip time)
ping -c 5 google.com

# Look for "rtt avg"
# Example: rtt min/avg/max/mdev = 12.3/12.5/12.8/0.2 ms
```

### Calculate your needed buffer

```bash
# Example: 1 Gbps connection, 50ms RTT
# 1 Gbps = 125,000,000 bytes/sec
# 50ms = 0.05 seconds
# BDP = 125,000,000 × 0.05 = 6,250,000 bytes (6.25 MB)

# Your buffer should be at least 6.25 MB (6,250,000 bytes)
```

### Tuning buffer sizes

**Step 1: View current values**

```bash
# Receive buffers (data coming IN)
sysctl net.ipv4.tcp_rmem
# Example: 4096 131072 6291456
#         4KB  128KB   6MB

# Send buffers (data going OUT)
sysctl net.ipv4.tcp_wmem
# Example: 4096 16384 4194304
#         4KB  16KB   4MB

# Core memory settings
sysctl net.core.rmem_max
sysctl net.core.wmem_max
```

**Step 2: Calculate your optimal values**

| Connection type  | Typical RTT | BDP for 1 Gbps | Recommended buffer |
| ---------------- | ----------- | -------------- | ------------------ |
| Same data center | 0.5 ms      | 62 KB          | 256 KB             |
| Within country   | 20 ms       | 2.5 MB         | 4 MB               |
| Across ocean     | 150 ms      | 18.75 MB       | 32 MB              |
| Satellite        | 500 ms      | 62.5 MB        | 64 MB              |

**Step 3: Apply temporary changes (to test)**

```bash
# Set receive buffer (min, default, max)
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.tcp_rmem="4096 1048576 16777216"
#                               4KB   1MB     16MB

# Set send buffer
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.tcp_wmem="4096 1048576 16777216"

# Set max core buffers (must be >= TCP max)
sudo sysctl -w net.core.rmem_max=16777216
sudo sysctl -w net.core.wmem_max=16777216

# Set default core buffers
sudo sysctl -w net.core.rmem_default=1048576
sudo sysctl -w net.core.wmem_default=1048576
```

**Step 4: Test performance**

```bash
# Test with iperf3 (install first: sudo apt install iperf3)
# On server: iperf3 -s
# On client: iperf3 -c server_ip

# Before and after tuning
```

**Step 5: Make permanent**

Add to `/etc/sysctl.conf` or `/etc/sysctl.d/99-network.conf`:

```bash
# /etc/sysctl.d/99-network.conf
net.core.rmem_max = 16777216
net.core.wmem_max = 16777216
net.core.rmem_default = 1048576
net.core.wmem_default = 1048576
net.ipv4.tcp_rmem = 4096 1048576 16777216
net.ipv4.tcp_wmem = 4096 1048576 16777216
```

Apply:

```bash
sudo sysctl -p /etc/sysctl.d/99-network.conf
```

---

## PART 5: TCP WINDOW SCALING

### The problem

In old TCP, the window size field was only 16 bits (max 65,535 bytes). That's tiny!

**Solution:** Window scaling (RFC 1323). Allows windows up to 1 GB.

### Check if window scaling is enabled

```bash
sysctl net.ipv4.tcp_window_scaling
```

**Should be `1` (enabled).** Almost all modern Linux has it on by default.

### View actual window sizes in use

```bash
# During a connection, see window size
ss -t -i

# Look for "rto: ... rtt: ... wscale:"
# wscale = window scaling factor
```

---

## PART 6: TCP CONGESTION CONTROL

### What is congestion control?

When a network is busy, packets get dropped. TCP has algorithms to detect congestion and slow down.

Different algorithms work better for different scenarios.

### Available algorithms

```bash
# View available algorithms
sysctl net.ipv4.tcp_available_congestion_control

# Example output:
# cubic reno bbr

# View current algorithm
sysctl net.ipv4.tcp_congestion_control

# Example output:
# cubic
```

### Algorithm comparison

| Algorithm | Best for                   | Characteristics                                  |
| --------- | -------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------ |
| **cubic** | General internet (default) | Aggressive, good for high-speed                  |
| **reno**  | Older networks             | Conservative, fair                               |
| **bbr**   | High latency, high loss    | Google's algorithm, very good for long distances |
| **vegas** | Low latency                | Proactive, avoids bufferbloat                    |

### Changing congestion control

```bash
# Temporary change (until reboot)
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.tcp_congestion_control=bbr

# Test performance with iperf3

# Make permanent
echo "net.ipv4.tcp_congestion_control=bbr" | sudo tee -a /etc/sysctl.d/99-network.conf
```

### BBR (Bottleneck Bandwidth and RTT)

BBR is Google's algorithm. It's excellent for:

- High-latency links (satellite, intercontinental)
- Links with packet loss
- Cloud environments

```bash
# Enable BBR (requires kernel 4.9+)
# Check kernel version
uname -r

# Enable if 4.9 or newer
sudo modprobe tcp_bbr
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.tcp_congestion_control=bbr

# Verify
sysctl net.ipv4.tcp_congestion_control
# Should show "bbr"
```

---

## PART 7: QUEUE LENGTHS (BACKLOG)

### netdev_max_backlog

**What it does:** Maximum number of packets waiting in the queue before being processed by the kernel.

**Default:** 1000

**When to increase:** High-traffic servers, 10 Gbps networks

```bash
# Check current
sysctl net.core.netdev_max_backlog

# Increase to 5000
sudo sysctl -w net.core.netdev_max_backlog=5000

# Permanent
echo "net.core.netdev_max_backlog=5000" | sudo tee -a /etc/sysctl.d/99-network.conf
```

### somaxconn

**What it does:** Maximum number of pending connections waiting for a listening socket (like web server).

**Default:** 128

**When to increase:** Busy web servers, high connection rate

```bash
# Check current
sysctl net.core.somaxconn

# Increase to 1024
sudo sysctl -w net.core.somaxconn=1024

# Permanent
echo "net.core.somaxconn=1024" | sudo tee -a /etc/sysctl.d/99-network.conf

# ALSO need to update application settings (nginx: listen 80 backlog=1024)
```

### tcp_max_syn_backlog

**What it does:** Maximum number of half-open connections (SYN received, not yet ACKed).

**Default:** 1024

**When to increase:** Under SYN flood attack or high connection rate

```bash
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.tcp_max_syn_backlog=4096
```

---

## PART 8: TIME VALUES (FASTER RECOVERY)

### tcp_fin_timeout

**What it does:** How long a closed connection stays in FIN-WAIT-2 state (seconds).

**Default:** 60 seconds

**When to decrease:** Servers with many short-lived connections

```bash
# Reduce to 30 seconds
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.tcp_fin_timeout=30
```

### tcp_tw_reuse

**What it does:** Reuse TIME-WAIT sockets for new connections.

**Default:** 0 (disabled)

**When to enable:** Web servers with many connections

```bash
# Enable (safe on modern Linux)
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.tcp_tw_reuse=1
```

**Note:** `tcp_tw_recycle` is DEPRECATED. DO NOT USE.

### tcp_keepalive

**What it does:** Check if idle connection is still alive.

| Parameter              | Default        | Meaning                                  |
| ---------------------- | -------------- | ---------------------------------------- |
| `tcp_keepalive_time`   | 7200 (2 hours) | Start sending keepalives after idle time |
| `tcp_keepalive_intvl`  | 75             | Seconds between keepalives               |
| `tcp_keepalive_probes` | 9              | Number of probes before declaring dead   |

**When to decrease:** Servers with mobile clients, cloud environments

```bash
# Check idle connections every 15 minutes
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.tcp_keepalive_time=900
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.tcp_keepalive_intvl=30
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.tcp_keepalive_probes=5
```

---

## PART 9: NIC OFFLOADING

### What is offloading?

Moving work from the CPU to the Network Interface Card (NIC).

**Checksum offloading:** NIC calculates TCP/UDP checksums (saves CPU).

**Large Receive Offload (LRO):** NIC combines small packets into large ones.

**Generic Receive Offload (GRO):** Like LRO but software-based.

### View offloading settings

```bash
# View current offload settings
ethtool -k eth0

# Look for:
# rx-checksumming: on
# tx-checksumming: on
# tcp-segmentation-offload: on
# generic-receive-offload: on
```

### When to disable offloading

Sometimes offloading causes problems:

- Virtual machines (packets get corrupted)
- Packet capture (tcpdump shows huge packets)
- Some NIC drivers have bugs

### Disable offloading (if needed)

```bash
# Turn off checksum offloading
sudo ethtool -K eth0 rx off tx off

# Turn off TSO (TCP Segmentation Offload)
sudo ethtool -K eth0 tso off

# Turn off GRO (Generic Receive Offload)
sudo ethtool -K eth0 gro off

# Make permanent (via systemd service)
```

### When to KEEP offloading enabled

- Most physical servers
- High-speed networks (10 Gbps+)
- When CPU is the bottleneck

---

## PART 10: RING BUFFERS

### What are ring buffers?

Memory areas used by the NIC to store packets before they are processed by the kernel.

**If ring buffer is too small:** Packets are dropped (rx_dropped).

**If ring buffer is too large:** More latency, more memory usage.

### View ring buffer size

```bash
# View current ring buffer sizes
ethtool -g eth0

# Example output:
# Ring parameters for eth0:
# Pre-set maximums:
# RX:     4096
# TX:     4096
# Current hardware settings:
# RX:     256
# TX:     256
```

### Check for drops

```bash
# View interface statistics
ip -s link show eth0

# Look for "dropped" under RX
#  RX: bytes  packets  errors  dropped  missed  mcast
```

### Increase ring buffer size

```bash
# Set RX ring buffer to 1024
sudo ethtool -G eth0 rx 1024

# Set TX ring buffer to 1024
sudo ethtool -G eth0 tx 1024

# Set both
sudo ethtool -G eth0 rx 1024 tx 1024
```

### Make permanent

Create systemd service: `/etc/systemd/system/ringbuffer.service`

```ini
[Unit]
Description=Set network ring buffers
After=network.target

[Service]
Type=oneshot
ExecStart=/usr/sbin/ethtool -G eth0 rx 1024 tx 1024
RemainAfterExit=yes

[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target
```

```bash
sudo systemctl enable ringbuffer.service
sudo systemctl start ringbuffer.service
```

---

## PART 11: COMPLETE PERFORMANCE TUNING PROFILE

### For a high-performance web server

```bash
# /etc/sysctl.d/99-web-server.conf

# Buffer sizes (for 10 Gbps, up to 50ms RTT)
net.core.rmem_max = 33554432
net.core.wmem_max = 33554432
net.core.rmem_default = 1048576
net.core.wmem_default = 1048576
net.ipv4.tcp_rmem = 4096 1048576 33554432
net.ipv4.tcp_wmem = 4096 1048576 33554432

# Queue lengths
net.core.netdev_max_backlog = 5000
net.core.somaxconn = 1024
net.ipv4.tcp_max_syn_backlog = 4096

# TCP tuning
net.ipv4.tcp_congestion_control = bbr
net.ipv4.tcp_window_scaling = 1
net.ipv4.tcp_tw_reuse = 1
net.ipv4.tcp_fin_timeout = 30

# Memory tuning
net.ipv4.tcp_mem = 786432 1048576 1572864
# min, pressure, max (in pages, 4KB each)

# Keepalive (detect dead connections faster)
net.ipv4.tcp_keepalive_time = 300
net.ipv4.tcp_keepalive_intvl = 15
net.ipv4.tcp_keepalive_probes = 5
```

### For a high-latency (satellite) connection

```bash
# Very large buffers (BDP for 50 Mbps, 600ms RTT = 3.75 MB)
net.core.rmem_max = 16777216
net.core.wmem_max = 16777216
net.ipv4.tcp_rmem = 4096 4194304 16777216
net.ipv4.tcp_wmem = 4096 4194304 16777216

# BBR is excellent for high latency
net.ipv4.tcp_congestion_control = bbr

# Enable SACK (selective acknowledgment)
net.ipv4.tcp_sack = 1
net.ipv4.tcp_dsack = 1
```

### For a database server (many short connections)

```bash
# Reuse TIME-WAIT connections
net.ipv4.tcp_tw_reuse = 1
net.ipv4.tcp_fin_timeout = 15

# Larger port range (for many outgoing connections)
net.ipv4.ip_local_port_range = 1024 65535

# Fast recovery
net.ipv4.tcp_slow_start_after_idle = 0
```

---

## PART 12: TESTING PERFORMANCE

### iperf3 (bandwidth test)

**Install:**

```bash
sudo apt install iperf3   # Debian/Ubuntu
sudo dnf install iperf3   # RHEL/Fedora
```

**Server side:**

```bash
iperf3 -s
```

**Client side:**

```bash
iperf3 -c server_ip

# Parallel streams (better for high bandwidth)
iperf3 -c server_ip -P 4

# Reverse direction (test download)
iperf3 -c server_ip -R

# UDP test (measure loss)
iperf3 -c server_ip -u -b 100M
```

### netperf (more detailed)

```bash
# Install
sudo apt install netperf

# Server
netserver

# Client - TCP test
netperf -H server_ip -l 60

# TCP test with larger buffers
netperf -H server_ip -l 60 -s 1M -S 1M
```

### Tools to monitor performance

```bash
# View dropped packets
ip -s link show eth0

# View TCP statistics
ss -s

# View detailed TCP info
nstat -z | grep Tcp

# Real-time monitoring (install: sudo apt install nethogs)
sudo nethogs eth0

# Bandwidth monitoring
sudo iftop
```

---

## PART 13: TROUBLESHOOTING PERFORMANCE

### Problem 1: Low throughput, no drops

**Symptom:** iperf3 shows 100 Mbps on a 1 Gbps link.

**Likely cause:** Buffer too small for RTT (window limited).

**Check:**

```bash
# View TCP statistics during transfer
ss -t -i

# Look for "snd_wnd" (send window)
# If it's small (less than BDP), increase buffers
```

**Fix:** Increase `tcp_rmem` and `tcp_wmem`.

### Problem 2: Packet drops increasing

**Symptom:**

```bash
ip -s link show eth0
# RX: dropped increasing
```

**Likely cause:** Ring buffer too small or netdev_max_backlog too small.

**Fix:**

```bash
# Increase ring buffer
ethtool -G eth0 rx 1024

# Increase backlog
sysctl -w net.core.netdev_max_backlog=5000
```

### Problem 3: High CPU usage during network transfer

**Likely cause:** Offloading disabled or inefficient driver.

**Check:**

```bash
ethtool -k eth0 | grep "offload"
```

**Fix:** Enable offloading:

```bash
ethtool -K eth0 rx on tx on tso on gro on
```

### Problem 4: Connection timeouts in logs

**Likely cause:** Keepalive too slow or fin_timeout too high.

**Fix:** Decrease `tcp_fin_timeout` and `tcp_keepalive_time`.

---

## PART 14: PRACTICAL LABS

### Lab 1: Measure performance before and after tuning

```bash
# Step 1: Establish baseline
iperf3 -c localhost
# (or between two real machines)

# Step 2: Record baseline speed

# Step 3: Apply buffer tuning
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.tcp_rmem="4096 1048576 16777216"
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.tcp_wmem="4096 1048576 16777216"

# Step 4: Test again
iperf3 -c localhost

# Step 5: Compare results
```

### Lab 2: Test different congestion control algorithms

```bash
# Step 1: Check available algorithms
sysctl net.ipv4.tcp_available_congestion_control

# Step 2: Test with cubic (default)
iperf3 -c server_ip

# Step 3: Switch to bbr
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.tcp_congestion_control=bbr

# Step 4: Test again
iperf3 -c server_ip

# Step 5: Compare
```

### Lab 3: Simulate high latency and tune for it

```bash
# Step 1: Add latency (simulate satellite)
sudo tc qdisc add dev eth0 root netem delay 300ms

# Step 2: Test performance (will be terrible)
iperf3 -c server_ip

# Step 3: Increase buffers for high BDP
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.tcp_rmem="4096 4194304 33554432"
sudo sysctl -w net.ipv4.tcp_wmem="4096 4194304 33554432"

# Step 4: Test again (should improve)
iperf3 -c server_ip

# Step 5: Clean up
sudo tc qdisc del dev eth0 root
```

---

## SUMMARY OF TOPIC 16

| Concept            | One-sentence explanation                                   |
| ------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------- |
| Buffer             | Memory where Linux stores packets waiting to be processed  |
| BDP                | Bandwidth × RTT (how much data can be in flight)           |
| Window scaling     | Allows TCP windows larger than 64KB                        |
| Congestion control | Algorithm that detects and reacts to network congestion    |
| BBR                | Modern congestion control (Google) - good for high latency |
| Offloading         | NIC does work instead of CPU (checksums, segmentation)     |
| Ring buffer        | NIC memory before kernel processes packets                 |

### Key sysctl settings to remember

| Setting                           | Purpose                              | Typical value    |
| --------------------------------- | ------------------------------------ | ---------------- |
| `net.core.rmem_max`               | Max receive buffer                   | 16-32 MB         |
| `net.core.wmem_max`               | Max send buffer                      | 16-32 MB         |
| `net.ipv4.tcp_rmem`               | TCP receive buffer (min,default,max) | 4KB, 1MB, 16MB   |
| `net.ipv4.tcp_wmem`               | TCP send buffer (min,default,max)    | 4KB, 1MB, 16MB   |
| `net.ipv4.tcp_congestion_control` | Congestion algorithm                 | `bbr` or `cubic` |
| `net.core.netdev_max_backlog`     | Packet queue length                  | 1000-5000        |

### One-line summary per use case

| Use case                      | Main tuning                               |
| ----------------------------- | ----------------------------------------- |
| High bandwidth (10 Gbps+)     | Increase buffers, enable offloading       |
| High latency (satellite)      | Increase buffers, use BBR                 |
| Many connections (web server) | Enable tcp_tw_reuse, increase somaxconn   |
| Low CPU (cloud VM)            | Increase offloading                       |
| Database (short connections)  | Decrease fin_timeout, increase port range |

---

# TOPIC 17: VXLAN & TUNNELING

## Before we start

I will **never assume you know anything**. Every concept, every command, every number is explained from absolute zero.

Your mother is watching. I will not fail.

---

## PART 1: WHAT IS A TUNNEL?

### The problem tunnels solve

You have two computers in different locations (different data centers, different clouds, different countries). They need to communicate as if they were on the **same network**.

**Without a tunnel:** They can only communicate via the internet. Their traffic is visible to anyone along the path.

**With a tunnel:** You create a virtual "pipe" between them. Everything inside the pipe is hidden.

### Analogy: The envelope inside an envelope

| Without tunnel                   | With tunnel                             |
| -------------------------------- | --------------------------------------- |
| You write "SECRET" on a postcard | You put the postcard inside an envelope |
| Anyone can read it               | Only the recipient opens the envelope   |
| The postcard goes directly       | The envelope has its own address        |
| No privacy                       | Privacy guaranteed                      |

### What a tunnel does

```
Original packet:
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ MAC │ IP (192.168.1.10 → 192.168.1.20) │ DATA       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

After encapsulation (tunnel):
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Outer MAC │ Outer IP (203.0.113.5 → 203.0.113.10) │ Tunnel Header │ Original Packet │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**The original packet becomes the PAYLOAD of a new packet.**

### Types of tunnels

| Tunnel type    | What it's used for                             | Layer   |
| -------------- | ---------------------------------------------- | ------- |
| **IPIP**       | Simple IP-over-IP                              | Layer 3 |
| **GRE**        | Generic Routing Encapsulation (many protocols) | Layer 3 |
| **VXLAN**      | Virtual eXtensible LAN (data center, cloud)    | Layer 2 |
| **IPsec**      | Secure VPN                                     | Layer 3 |
| **WireGuard**  | Modern secure VPN                              | Layer 3 |
| **SSH tunnel** | Port forwarding                                | Layer 4 |

---

## PART 2: WHY VXLAN?

### The problem VLANs couldn't solve

VLANs have a limit of **4094** (12-bit VLAN ID). In large data centers with thousands of tenants, that's not enough.

**VXLAN solves this with a 24-bit ID: 16 MILLION possible networks.**

### VXLAN vs VLAN

| Feature              | VLAN                  | VXLAN                                |
| -------------------- | --------------------- | ------------------------------------ |
| Maximum networks     | 4,094                 | 16,777,216                           |
| Works across routers | No (layer 2 only)     | Yes (encapsulated in UDP)            |
| Standard             | 802.1Q                | RFC 7348                             |
| Used in              | Small/medium networks | Data centers, cloud (AWS, OpenStack) |

### How VXLAN works

VXLAN encapsulates your original Ethernet frame inside a UDP packet.

```
Original frame (from VM):
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ MAC │ IP (10.0.0.1 → 10.0.0.2) │ DATA              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

VXLAN encapsulation:
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Outer MAC │ Outer IP (192.168.1.10 → 192.168.2.20) │ UDP │ VXLAN Header │ Original Frame │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**What each part is:**

| Part           | What it contains                          |
| -------------- | ----------------------------------------- |
| Outer MAC      | MAC addresses of physical hosts (not VMs) |
| Outer IP       | IP addresses of physical hosts (not VMs)  |
| UDP            | Dest port 4789 (standard VXLAN port)      |
| VXLAN Header   | 24-bit VXLAN Network Identifier (VNI)     |
| Original Frame | The VM's Ethernet frame (MAC, IP, data)   |

### VXLAN components

| Component    | What it does                                              |
| ------------ | --------------------------------------------------------- |
| **VTEP**     | VXLAN Tunnel End Point (the tunnel endpoint on each host) |
| **VNI**      | VXLAN Network Identifier (like VLAN ID, but 24-bit)       |
| **Underlay** | The physical network that carries VXLAN packets           |
| **Overlay**  | The virtual network created by VXLAN                      |

---

## PART 3: SETTING UP A VXLAN TUNNEL (COMMAND LINE)

### Simple VXLAN between two hosts

**Host A:** IP 192.168.1.10, wants to create VXLAN with VNI 100

**Host B:** IP 192.168.2.20, wants to create VXLAN with VNI 100

### Step by step on Host A

```bash
# Step 1: Create VXLAN interface
# vxlan100 is the interface name
# id 100 is the VNI (must match on both ends)
# remote 192.168.2.20 is the other host's IP
# dstport 4789 is the standard VXLAN port
# dev eth0 is the physical interface to send encapsulated packets
sudo ip link add vxlan100 type vxlan id 100 remote 192.168.2.20 dstport 4789 dev eth0

# Step 2: Bring the interface up
sudo ip link set vxlan100 up

# Step 3: Assign an IP address to the VXLAN interface
# This is the IP for the overlay network
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.1/24 dev vxlan100

# Step 4: Verify
ip addr show vxlan100
ip link show vxlan100
```

### Step by step on Host B

```bash
# Step 1: Create VXLAN interface (same VNI, pointing to Host A)
sudo ip link add vxlan100 type vxlan id 100 remote 192.168.1.10 dstport 4789 dev eth0

# Step 2: Bring it up
sudo ip link set vxlan100 up

# Step 3: Assign IP (different IP, same network)
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.2/24 dev vxlan100

# Step 4: Test
ping 10.0.0.1
```

**What just happened?** You created a virtual network (10.0.0.0/24) that connects Host A and Host B through the physical network (192.168.1.0 and 192.168.2.0). The VXLAN tunnel carries the traffic.

### View VXLAN details

```bash
# Show VXLAN interface details
ip -d link show vxlan100

# Show bridge fdb entries (MAC addresses learned over VXLAN)
bridge fdb show dev vxlan100
```

**Example output:**

```
00:00:00:00:00:00 dev vxlan100 dst 192.168.2.20 via eth0
```

---

## PART 4: MULTICAST VXLAN (MULTIPLE HOSTS)

### The problem with point-to-point VXLAN

With `remote` specified, VXLAN only connects to ONE other host. What if you have 10 hosts?

**Solution:** Use multicast. All hosts join the same multicast group. When one host sends, all hosts receive.

### Setting up multicast VXLAN

**On all hosts (same configuration):**

```bash
# Create VXLAN with multicast (no remote specified)
# group 239.1.1.100 is the multicast group address
sudo ip link add vxlan100 type vxlan id 100 group 239.1.1.100 dstport 4789 dev eth0

# Bring up
sudo ip link set vxlan100 up

# Assign IP (each host gets a different IP on same network)
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.1/24 dev vxlan100   # Host A
# sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.2/24 dev vxlan100 # Host B
# sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.3/24 dev vxlan100 # Host C

# Test
ping 10.0.0.2
```

### Checking multicast membership

```bash
# View multicast groups
ip maddr show dev eth0

# Look for the group address (239.1.1.100)
```

---

## PART 5: BRIDGE + VXLAN (CONNECTING VMS)

### The setup

You have multiple physical hosts. Each host runs VMs. You want VMs on different hosts to be on the **same layer 2 network**.

**Solution:** Create a bridge on each host, add the VXLAN interface to the bridge, and add VM interfaces to the bridge.

### Architecture

```
Host A                                   Host B
┌─────────────────────────┐             ┌─────────────────────────┐
│  ┌─────────────────────┐│             │  ┌─────────────────────┐│
│  │   VM 1              ││             │  │   VM 3              ││
│  │   IP: 10.0.0.10     ││             │  │   IP: 10.0.0.30     ││
│  │   MAC: aa:aa:aa     ││             │  │   MAC: cc:cc:cc     ││
│  └──────┬──────────────┘│             │  └──────┬──────────────┘│
│         │ tap0          │             │         │ tap0          │
│  ┌──────┴──────────────┐│             │  ┌──────┴──────────────┐│
│  │      br0 (bridge)   ││             │  │      br0 (bridge)   ││
│  │   ┌────┴────┐       ││             │  │   ┌────┴────┐       ││
│  │   │ vxlan100│       ││             │  │   │ vxlan100│       ││
│  │   └────┬────┘       ││             │  │   └────┬────┘       ││
│  └──────┬──────────────┘│             │  └──────┬──────────────┘│
│         │ eth0          │             │         │ eth0          │
│     192.168.1.10        │             │     192.168.2.20        │
└─────────┼───────────────┘             └─────────┼───────────────┘
          │                                       │
          └───────────────┬───────────────────────┘
                          │
                    Physical Network
```

### Step-by-step configuration on Host A

```bash
# Step 1: Create VXLAN interface (multicast)
sudo ip link add vxlan100 type vxlan id 100 group 239.1.1.100 dstport 4789 dev eth0

# Step 2: Create bridge
sudo ip link add br0 type bridge

# Step 3: Add VXLAN to bridge
sudo ip link set vxlan100 master br0

# Step 4: Bring interfaces up
sudo ip link set vxlan100 up
sudo ip link set br0 up

# Step 5: (Optional) Give bridge an IP for management
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.1/24 dev br0

# Step 6: Create tap interface for VM (if not already created by VM software)
sudo ip tuntap add tap0 mode tap
sudo ip link set tap0 master br0
sudo ip link set tap0 up
```

### Same configuration on Host B

```bash
# Same commands, but bridge gets a different IP (or no IP)
sudo ip link add vxlan100 type vxlan id 100 group 239.1.1.100 dstport 4789 dev eth0
sudo ip link add br0 type bridge
sudo ip link set vxlan100 master br0
sudo ip link set vxlan100 up
sudo ip link set br0 up

# Optional: bridge IP (different)
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.2/24 dev br0
```

Now VM 1 (on Host A) and VM 3 (on Host B) can communicate as if they are on the same switch.

---

## PART 6: OTHER TUNNEL TYPES

### GRE (Generic Routing Encapsulation)

**Simpler than VXLAN.** Can encapsulate many protocols (not just Ethernet).

```bash
# Create GRE tunnel between two hosts
# Host A (192.168.1.10) and Host B (192.168.2.20)

# On Host A:
sudo ip tunnel add gre1 mode gre remote 192.168.2.20 local 192.168.1.10 ttl 255
sudo ip link set gre1 up
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.1/30 dev gre1

# On Host B:
sudo ip tunnel add gre1 mode gre remote 192.168.1.10 local 192.168.2.20 ttl 255
sudo ip link set gre1 up
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.2/30 dev gre1

# Test
ping 10.0.0.2
```

### IPIP (IP over IP)

**Simplest tunnel.** Only carries IPv4 inside IPv4.

```bash
# On Host A:
sudo ip tunnel add ipip1 mode ipip remote 192.168.2.20 local 192.168.1.10
sudo ip link set ipip1 up
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.1/30 dev ipip1

# On Host B:
sudo ip tunnel add ipip1 mode ipip remote 192.168.1.10 local 192.168.2.20
sudo ip link set ipip1 up
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.2/30 dev ipip1
```

### Comparison

| Tunnel type | Layer | Encapsulation   | Best for                  |
| ----------- | ----- | --------------- | ------------------------- |
| IPIP        | 3     | IP in IP        | Simple IPv4-only networks |
| GRE         | 3     | Many protocols  | General purpose           |
| VXLAN       | 2     | Ethernet in UDP | Data center, large scale  |
| Geneve      | 2     | Ethernet in UDP | More flexible than VXLAN  |

---

## PART 7: TROUBLESHOOTING VXLAN

### Problem 1: Cannot ping across VXLAN

```bash
# Check VXLAN interface exists
ip link show vxlan100

# Check VXLAN interface is UP
ip link show vxlan100 | grep UP

# Check IP address is assigned
ip addr show vxlan100

# Check routes (should be directly connected)
ip route show dev vxlan100

# Check multicast (if using multicast)
ip maddr show dev eth0 | grep 239.1.1.100

# Check firewall (UDP port 4789 must be open)
sudo iptables -L -n | grep 4789
```

### Problem 2: ARP not working across VXLAN

```bash
# Check bridge FDB entries
bridge fdb show dev vxlan100

# Manually add MAC mapping (if needed)
sudo bridge fdb append 00:11:22:33:44:55 dev vxlan100 dst 192.168.2.20

# Enable learning
sudo ip link set vxlan100 type vxlan learning on
```

### Problem 3: VXLAN works but slow

**Check MTU issues:**

VXLAN adds overhead:

- Original MTU: 1500
- VXLAN overhead: ~50 bytes
- Effective MTU: ~1450

```bash
# Set lower MTU on VXLAN interface
sudo ip link set vxlan100 mtu 1450

# Also set on bridge
sudo ip link set br0 mtu 1450

# Test with ping
ping -M do -s 1400 10.0.0.2
```

### Problem 4: Multicast not working

```bash
# Test multicast connectivity
# On Host A, send multicast packet
ping -I eth0 239.1.1.100

# On Host B, capture multicast
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 host 239.1.1.100

# If no packets, your switch/routers don't support multicast
# Switch to point-to-point VXLAN or use EVPN (advanced)
```

---

## PART 8: PERMANENT VXLAN CONFIGURATION

### Method: systemd-networkd

**File:** `/etc/systemd/network/vxlan100.netdev`

```ini
[NetDev]
Name=vxlan100
Kind=vxlan

[VXLAN]
Id=100
Remote=192.168.2.20
DestinationPort=4789
TOS=1
TTL=64
```

**File:** `/etc/systemd/network/vxlan100.network`

```ini
[Match]
Name=vxlan100

[Network]
Address=10.0.0.1/24
```

**File:** `/etc/systemd/network/br0.netdev`

```ini
[NetDev]
Name=br0
Kind=bridge
```

**File:** `/etc/systemd/network/br0.network`

```ini
[Match]
Name=br0

[Network]
Address=10.0.0.1/24
```

**File:** `/etc/systemd/network/vxlan100-bridge.network`

```ini
[Match]
Name=vxlan100

[Network]
Bridge=br0
```

### Method: Netplan (Ubuntu)

**File:** `/etc/netplan/02-vxlan.yaml`

```yaml
network:
  version: 2
  renderer: networkd
  tunnels:
    vxlan100:
      mode: vxlan
      id: 100
      remote: 192.168.2.20
      link: eth0
      addresses:
        - 10.0.0.1/24
  bridges:
    br0:
      interfaces:
        - vxlan100
      addresses:
        - 10.0.0.1/24
```

---

## PART 9: VXLAN IN THE REAL WORLD (CLOUD, DOCKER)

### AWS VPC

Amazon's Virtual Private Cloud (VPC) uses VXLAN-like technology. When you create a VPC, you get a virtual network across multiple availability zones.

### Docker overlays

Docker's overlay network driver uses VXLAN (or similar) to connect containers across hosts.

```bash
# Create overlay network in Docker
docker network create -d overlay my-overlay

# Containers on different hosts can now communicate
```

### Kubernetes CNI (Calico, Flannel, Cilium)

Most Kubernetes networking solutions use VXLAN for overlay networking.

**Flannel VXLAN backend:**

```yaml
net-conf.json: |
  {
    "Network": "10.244.0.0/16",
    "Backend": {
      "Type": "vxlan"
    }
  }
```

---

## PART 10: PRACTICAL LABS

### Lab 1: Simple point-to-point VXLAN

**Requires:** Two Linux machines (or VMs) on same network (can ping each other)

```bash
# On Host A (192.168.1.10)
sudo ip link add vxlan100 type vxlan id 100 remote 192.168.1.20 dstport 4789 dev eth0
sudo ip link set vxlan100 up
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.1/24 dev vxlan100

# On Host B (192.168.1.20)
sudo ip link add vxlan100 type vxlan id 100 remote 192.168.1.10 dstport 4789 dev eth0
sudo ip link set vxlan100 up
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.2/24 dev vxlan100

# Test
ping 10.0.0.2   # from Host A
ping 10.0.0.1   # from Host B
```

### Lab 2: VXLAN with bridge and tap (simulate VMs)

```bash
# On Host A (192.168.1.10)
# Create VXLAN
sudo ip link add vxlan100 type vxlan id 100 group 239.1.1.100 dstport 4789 dev eth0

# Create bridge
sudo ip link add br0 type bridge
sudo ip link set vxlan100 master br0

# Create tap interface (simulating a VM)
sudo ip tuntap add tap0 mode tap
sudo ip link set tap0 master br0

# Bring all up
sudo ip link set vxlan100 up
sudo ip link set br0 up
sudo ip link set tap0 up

# Give tap0 an IP (simulating VM configuration)
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.10/24 dev tap0

# On Host B (192.168.1.20) - same commands
# Give tap0 a different IP
sudo ip addr add 10.0.0.20/24 dev tap0

# Test ping from tap0 on Host A
ping -I tap0 10.0.0.20
```

### Lab 3: Capture VXLAN packets

```bash
# On Host A, start ping
ping 10.0.0.2

# In another terminal, capture VXLAN traffic
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -e -n udp port 4789

# You'll see:
# 192.168.1.10.4789 > 192.168.1.20.4789: UDP, length X
# This is the encapsulated packet!
```

---

## SUMMARY OF TOPIC 17

| Concept   | One-sentence explanation                                       |
| --------- | -------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Tunnel    | Encapsulating one packet inside another to cross networks      |
| VXLAN     | Layer 2 overlay network over UDP (16 million networks)         |
| VTEP      | VXLAN Tunnel End Point (the tunnel endpoint on each host)      |
| VNI       | VXLAN Network Identifier (24-bit, like VLAN ID)                |
| Underlay  | Physical network carrying VXLAN packets                        |
| Overlay   | Virtual network created by VXLAN                               |
| GRE       | Generic Routing Encapsulation (simpler tunnel)                 |
| Multicast | One-to-many communication (used for VXLAN with multiple hosts) |

### VXLAN commands quick reference

| Command                                                                          | What it does                |
| -------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------- |
| `ip link add vxlan100 type vxlan id 100 remote 10.0.0.2 dstport 4789 dev eth0`   | Create point-to-point VXLAN |
| `ip link add vxlan100 type vxlan id 100 group 239.1.1.100 dstport 4789 dev eth0` | Create multicast VXLAN      |
| `ip link set vxlan100 master br0`                                                | Add VXLAN to bridge         |
| `ip -d link show vxlan100`                                                       | Show VXLAN details          |
| `bridge fdb show dev vxlan100`                                                   | Show MAC learning table     |
| `tcpdump -i eth0 udp port 4789`                                                  | Capture VXLAN packets       |

### When to use which tunnel

| Use case                          | Tunnel type      |
| --------------------------------- | ---------------- |
| Simple point-to-point VPN         | WireGuard, IPsec |
| Connect VMs across hosts          | VXLAN            |
| Data center with many tenants     | VXLAN (16M VNI)  |
| Simple IP-only tunnel             | GRE or IPIP      |
| Container networking (Docker/K8s) | VXLAN or overlay |

---

# TOPIC 18: NETWORK OBSERVABILITY & MONITORING

## Before we start

I will **never assume you know anything**. Every concept, every command, every number is explained from absolute zero.

Your mother is watching. I will not fail.

---

## PART 1: WHAT IS NETWORK OBSERVABILITY?

### The problem observability solves

Your network is working. Everything seems fine. But then:

- A user complains: "The website is slow"
- A server crashes mysteriously
- Bandwidth usage spikes at 3 AM
- Packets are being dropped, but you don't know where

**Without observability:** You are blind. You guess. You restart things randomly.

**With observability:** You see exactly what's happening. You find the problem in minutes.

### Observability vs Monitoring

| Term              | Meaning                                           |
| ----------------- | ------------------------------------------------- |
| **Monitoring**    | Collecting metrics (bandwidth, drops, errors)     |
| **Observability** | Understanding what's happening from those metrics |

**Analogy: Car dashboard**

| Monitoring             | Observability                            |
| ---------------------- | ---------------------------------------- |
| Speedometer shows 0    | Engine stalled? No fuel? Broken belt?    |
| Fuel gauge shows empty | You need fuel                            |
| Check engine light on  | Multiple possible causes; need diagnosis |

### What we will cover

| Tool                   | What it shows                 | When to use                           |
| ---------------------- | ----------------------------- | ------------------------------------- |
| `iftop`                | Bandwidth per connection      | Find who is using bandwidth           |
| `nethogs`              | Bandwidth per process         | Find which program is using bandwidth |
| `iptraf-ng`            | Detailed traffic statistics   | Analyze traffic patterns              |
| `nload`                | Real-time bandwidth graphs    | Quick bandwidth check                 |
| `bmon`                 | Multiple interface monitoring | Monitor many interfaces at once       |
| `vnstat`               | Historical bandwidth data     | Track usage over time                 |
| `Prometheus + Grafana` | Enterprise monitoring         | Production servers                    |
| `tcpdump`              | Packet capture                | Deep inspection                       |

---

## PART 2: iftop (BANDWIDTH PER CONNECTION)

### What is iftop?

`iftop` shows you **which connections** are using bandwidth. It's like `top` but for network traffic.

### Install iftop

```bash
# Debian/Ubuntu
sudo apt install iftop

# RHEL/CentOS/Fedora
sudo dnf install iftop
```

### Basic usage

```bash
# Monitor eth0 (requires sudo)
sudo iftop -i eth0
```

**What you will see:**

```
                   192.168.1.100              => 8.8.8.8             1.23Mb  2.45Mb  3.67Mb
                                            <=                      1.10Mb  2.20Mb  3.30Mb
                   192.168.1.100              => 192.168.1.50         234Kb   567Kb   890Kb
                                            <=                       123Kb   345Kb   567Kb

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
TX:             cum:   45.6MB   peak:   5.2Mb  rates:   1.2Mb   2.3Mb   3.4Mb
RX:                    32.1MB           4.1Mb            0.9Mb   1.8Mb   2.7Mb
TOTAL:                 77.7MB           9.3Mb            2.1Mb   4.1Mb   6.1Mb
```

**Let me explain EVERY piece:**

| Column        | Meaning                                                |
| ------------- | ------------------------------------------------------ |
| Left side     | Source IP (or hostname)                                |
| `=>`          | Traffic going OUT from your computer                   |
| `<=`          | Traffic coming IN to your computer                     |
| Right side    | Destination IP (or hostname)                           |
| Three numbers | 2-second average, 10-second average, 40-second average |

**Bottom lines:**
| Row | Meaning |
|-----|---------|
| `TX` | Transmit (data sent out) |
| `RX` | Receive (data received) |
| `cum` | Cumulative total since starting |
| `peak` | Peak rate seen |
| `rates` | 2s/10s/40s averages |

### Useful iftop options

```bash
# Don't resolve hostnames (faster, cleaner)
sudo iftop -i eth0 -n

# Show port numbers
sudo iftop -i eth0 -P

# Show only traffic to/from specific IP
sudo iftop -i eth0 -F 192.168.1.0/24

# Show only traffic not to/from specific IP
sudo iftop -i eth0 -f "not host 192.168.1.1"

# Run for 10 seconds then exit (for scripts)
sudo iftop -i eth0 -t -s 10
```

### iftop keyboard shortcuts

| Key | Action                          |
| --- | ------------------------------- |
| `h` | Help                            |
| `n` | Toggle name resolution (on/off) |
| `p` | Toggle port display             |
| `P` | Pause display                   |
| `q` | Quit                            |
| `→` | Sort by column 2                |
| `←` | Sort by column 1                |

---

## PART 3: nethogs (BANDWIDTH PER PROCESS)

### What is nethogs?

`iftop` shows connections. `nethogs` shows **which PROGRAM** is using bandwidth.

### Install nethogs

```bash
sudo apt install nethogs   # Debian/Ubuntu
sudo dnf install nethogs   # RHEL/Fedora
```

### Basic usage

```bash
# Monitor eth0
sudo nethogs eth0
```

**What you will see:**

```
NetHogs version 0.8.7

    PID USER     PROGRAM               DEV    SENT      RECEIVED
   2345 root     /usr/sbin/sshd        eth0   0.123     0.456 KB/sec
   5678 www-data /usr/sbin/apache2     eth0   1.234     5.678 KB/sec
   9012 user     /usr/bin/firefox      eth0   0.789     3.456 KB/sec
   3456 user     /usr/bin/curl         eth0   2.345     4.567 KB/sec
```

**Let me explain EVERY column:**

| Column   | Meaning                                   |
| -------- | ----------------------------------------- |
| PID      | Process ID (use `ps` to see more details) |
| USER     | User running the program                  |
| PROGRAM  | The program using bandwidth               |
| DEV      | Which interface                           |
| SENT     | Upload speed (KB/sec or MB/sec)           |
| RECEIVED | Download speed                            |

### Useful nethogs options

```bash
# Refresh every 2 seconds (default 1)
sudo nethogs -d 2 eth0

# Show traffic in KB/sec (default)
sudo nethogs -d 1 eth0

# Show traffic in MB/sec
sudo nethogs -d 1 -v 3 eth0

# Monitor multiple interfaces
sudo nethogs eth0 wlan0
```

### nethogs keyboard shortcuts

| Key | Action                                           |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------ |
| `q` | Quit                                             |
| `m` | Cycle units (KB/sec, MB/sec, KB total, MB total) |
| `r` | Sort by received                                 |
| `s` | Sort by sent                                     |

---

## PART 4: nload (REAL-TIME BANDWIDTH GRAPHS)

### What is nload?

`nload` shows a simple graph of bandwidth usage. Great for quick visibility.

### Install nload

```bash
sudo apt install nload   # Debian/Ubuntu
sudo dnf install nload   # RHEL/Fedora
```

### Basic usage

```bash
# Monitor eth0
nload eth0

# Monitor all interfaces
nload
```

**What you will see:**

```
Device eth0 [192.168.1.100] (1/2)
================================================================================
Incoming:

                     ##############################
                     ##############################
                     ##############################
                     ##############################
Curr: 1.23 Mbit/s    Avg: 1.10 Mbit/s    Min: 0.89 Mbit/s    Max: 1.45 Mbit/s
TTL: 125.00 Mbit

Outgoing:

                     ######################
                     ######################
                     ######################
Curr: 0.45 Mbit/s    Avg: 0.40 Mbit/s    Min: 0.23 Mbit/s    Max: 0.56 Mbit/s
TTL: 125.00 Mbit
```

**Let me explain:**

| Term | Meaning                               |
| ---- | ------------------------------------- |
| Curr | Current bandwidth usage               |
| Avg  | Average since starting                |
| Min  | Minimum seen                          |
| Max  | Maximum seen                          |
| TTL  | Total data transferred since starting |

### nload keyboard shortcuts

| Key       | Action            |
| --------- | ----------------- |
| `←` / `→` | Switch interfaces |
| `F2`      | Show options menu |
| `q`       | Quit              |

---

## PART 5: iptraf-ng (DETAILED STATISTICS)

### What is iptraf-ng?

`iptraf-ng` is a comprehensive network statistics tool with multiple views.

### Install iptraf-ng

```bash
sudo apt install iptraf-ng   # Debian/Ubuntu
sudo dnf install iptraf-ng   # RHEL/Fedora
```

### Basic usage

```bash
sudo iptraf-ng
```

**Main menu:**

```
    ┌───────────────────────────── IPTraf-ng ──────────────────────────────┐

    │  │ IP traffic monitor                                                │

    │  │ Detailed interface statistics                                     │
    │  │ Statistical breakdowns                                            │
    │  │ LAN station monitor                                               │
    │  │ Configure...                                                      │
    │  │ Exit                                                              │
    │                                                                      │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### Most useful views

**1. IP traffic monitor (real-time connections):**

┌─────────── IP Traffic Monitor ────────────┐
│TCP (20)  UDP (5)  Other (3)  Total (28)   │
│                                           │
│Source              Dest              Packets
│192.168.1.100:22    192.168.1.50:54321  1234
│192.168.1.100:45678 8.8.8.8:443         5678
└───────────────────────────────────────────┘

┌───────── General Interface Statistics ─────────┐

│                                                │

│Broadcast: 1234       Multicast: 5678          │
│Errors: 0              Drops: 12               │

## PART 6: vnstat (HISTORICAL BANDWIDTH)

### What is vnstat?

`vnstat` runs as a daemon and logs bandwidth usage over time. You can see usage by hour, day, month, year.

### Install vnstat

```bash
sudo apt install vnstat   # Debian/Ubuntu
sudo dnf install vnstat   # RHEL/Fedora
```

### Setup

```bash
# Create database for eth0
sudo vnstat -i eth0

# Start the daemon
sudo systemctl enable vnstat
sudo systemctl start vnstat
```

### Basic usage

```bash
# Show daily usage
vnstat -d

# Show monthly usage
vnstat -m

# Show hourly for today
vnstat -h

# Show live traffic (like nload)
vnstat -l

# Show top 10 days
vnstat -d --top
```

**Example output:**

```
 eth0  /  daily

     day         rx      |     tx      |    total
-------------------------+-------------+-------------
   2025-01-19    2.45 GB |    1.23 GB  |    3.68 GB
   2025-01-18    3.12 GB |    1.89 GB  |    5.01 GB
   2025-01-17    1.89 GB |    0.98 GB  |    2.87 GB
-------------------------+-------------+-------------
   estimated         --  |       --    |       --
```

### vnstat keyboard shortcuts (live mode)

```bash
# Press 'q' to quit
vnstat -l
```

---

## PART 7: bmon (MULTIPLE INTERFACES)

### What is bmon?

`bmon` shows graphs for all interfaces simultaneously. Great for servers with multiple NICs.

### Install bmon

```bash
sudo apt install bmon   # Debian/Ubuntu
sudo dnf install bmon   # RHEL/Fedora
```

### Basic usage

```bash
bmon
```

**What you will see:**

```
┌─ Interfaces ──────────────────────┐ ┌─ eth0 192.168.1.100 ─────────────────┐
│ lo          0.00 B/s 0.00 B/s     │ │                                       │
│ eth0        1.23 MB/s 0.45 MB/s   │ │ In:  ████████████░░░░░░░░  1.23 MB/s │
│ wlan0       0.00 B/s 0.00 B/s     │ │ Out: ████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  0.45 MB/s │
└───────────────────────────────────┘ └───────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### bmon keyboard shortcuts

| Key       | Action           |
| --------- | ---------------- |
| `↑` / `↓` | Select interface |
| `g`       | Show graph       |
| `d`       | Show details     |
| `q`       | Quit             |

## PART 8: ss AND ip (COMMAND LINE STATISTICS)

### ss - socket statistics (review from Topic 4)

```bash
# Show all TCP sockets with statistics
ss -t -e -i

# Show listening ports with process names
sudo ss -tulpn

# Show connection summary
ss -s
```

**Example output of `ss -s`:**

```
Total: 525 (kernel 0)
TCP:   43 (estab 12, closed 19, orphaned 0, synrecv 0, timewait 19/0), ports 0

Transport Total     IP        IPv6
RAW       0         0         0
UDP       5         3         2
TCP       24        20        4
INET      29        23        6
FRAG      0         0         0
```

### ip - interface statistics

```bash
# Show interface statistics
ip -s link show eth0

# Show detailed statistics
ip -s -s link show eth0
```

**Example output:**

```
2: eth0: <BROADCAST,MULTICAST,UP,LOWER_UP> mtu 1500 qdisc fq_codel state UP mode DEFAULT group default qlen 1000
    link/ether 52:54:00:12:34:56 brd ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff
    RX: bytes  packets  errors  dropped  missed  mcast
    456789012  3456789  0       12       0       1234
    TX: bytes  packets  errors  dropped  carrier collisions
    123456789  2345678  0       0        0       0
```

## PART 9: tcpdump (PACKET CAPTURE)

### What is tcpdump?

`tcpdump` captures actual packets. It shows you exactly what is on the wire.

### Basic usage

```bash
# Capture all packets on eth0 (requires sudo)
sudo tcpdump -i eth0

# Capture only 10 packets
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -c 10

# Save to file for later analysis
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -w capture.pcap

# Read from file
tcpdump -r capture.pcap
```

### Useful filters

```bash
# Capture only SSH traffic (port 22)
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 port 22

# Capture only from specific IP
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 src 192.168.1.50

# Capture only to specific IP
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 dst 8.8.8.8

# Capture only HTTP
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 port 80 or port 443

# Capture only between two IPs
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 host 192.168.1.10 and host 192.168.1.20

# Capture only non-SSH traffic
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 not port 22

# Capture with more detail
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -v -e -n
```

### Common tcpdump flags

| Flag                  | Meaning                                            |
| --------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- |
| `-i eth0`             | Interface                                          |
| `-c 10`               | Capture 10 packets then exit                       |
| `-w file.pcap`        | Write to file                                      |
| `-r file.pcap`        | Read from file                                     |
| `-n`                  | No name resolution (shows IPs, not hostnames)      |
| `-nn`                 | No port name resolution (shows port 22, not "ssh") |
| `-v` / `-vv` / `-vvv` | Verbose output                                     |
| `-e`                  | Show MAC addresses                                 |
| `-s 1500`             | Capture full packet (not just header)              |

---

## PART 10: PROMETHEUS + GRAFANA (ENTERPRISE MONITORING)

### What is Prometheus?

Prometheus is a time-series database that collects metrics from servers.

### What is Grafana?

Grafana is a dashboard that visualizes Prometheus data.

### Node Exporter (collects network metrics)

Node Exporter is a Prometheus exporter that collects system metrics including network.

**Install Node Exporter:**

```bash
# Download
wget https://github.com/prometheus/node_exporter/releases/download/v1.7.0/node_exporter-1.7.0.linux-amd64.tar.gz
tar xvf node_exporter-1.7.0.linux-amd64.tar.gz
cd node_exporter-1.7.0.linux-amd64

# Run (as service)
./node_exporter
```

**Network metrics Node Exporter collects:**

- `node_network_receive_bytes_total` - bytes received
- `node_network_transmit_bytes_total` - bytes sent
- `node_network_receive_drop_total` - packets dropped
- `node_network_receive_errs_total` - errors
- `node_network_receive_packets_total` - packets

**Example Prometheus query:**

```promql
# Rate of bytes received per second
rate(node_network_receive_bytes_total{interface="eth0"}[5m])
```

### Quick dashboard in Grafana

Once Prometheus and Grafana are connected, you can create dashboards showing:

1. **Bandwidth usage graph** - over time
2. **Packet drop alerts** - when drops > 0
3. **Error rates** - when errors exceed threshold
4. **Top talkers** - which IPs are using bandwidth

---

## PART 11: TROUBLESHOOTING SCENARIOS

### Scenario 1: "The internet is slow"

```bash
# Step 1: Check bandwidth usage
sudo iftop -i eth0

# Step 2: Check if a specific connection is using all bandwidth
# Look for high rates in iftop

# Step 3: Find which program is causing it
sudo nethogs eth0

# Step 4: Check for packet drops
ip -s link show eth0 | grep dropped

# Step 5: Check connection stats
ss -t -i
```

### Scenario 2: "My server is dropping packets"

```bash
# Step 1: Check interface drops
ip -s link show eth0

# If dropped > 0:
# - Ring buffer too small
# - Interrupt rate too high

# Step 2: Check ring buffer
ethtool -g eth0

# Step 3: Increase ring buffer
ethtool -G eth0 rx 1024

# Step 4: Monitor again
watch -n 1 'ip -s link show eth0'
```

### Scenario 3: "I need to see what's happening at 3 AM"

```bash
# vnstat keeps history
vnstat -d
vnstat -h

# Or set up Prometheus to graph historical data
```

### Scenario 4: "I suspect a specific IP is attacking me"

```bash
# Capture packets from that IP
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 src attacker_ip -c 1000

# Count connections from that IP
ss -t -n | grep attacker_ip | wc -l

# Monitor bandwidth from that IP
sudo iftop -i eth0 -f "host attacker_ip"
```

---

## PART 12: QUICK REFERENCE CARD

### When to use which tool

| Question                                       | Tool                      |
| ---------------------------------------------- | ------------------------- |
| Who is using all my bandwidth (by connection)? | `sudo iftop -i eth0`      |
| Which program is using bandwidth?              | `sudo nethogs eth0`       |
| What's the current speed?                      | `nload eth0`              |
| How much bandwidth did I use yesterday?        | `vnstat -d`               |
| Are packets being dropped?                     | `ip -s link show eth0`    |
| What packets are actually on the wire?         | `sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -n` |
| How many connections do I have?                | `ss -s`                   |
| What ports are open on my server?              | `sudo ss -tulpn`          |

### One-liner commands for quick checks

```bash
# Quick bandwidth (watch every second)
watch -n 1 'cat /sys/class/net/eth0/statistics/rx_bytes; cat /sys/class/net/eth0/statistics/tx_bytes'

# Quick drops check
watch -n 1 'ip -s link show eth0 | grep -A 4 RX:'

# Top connections by packets (requires nethogs)
sudo nethogs eth0

# Save bandwidth history for 24 hours
sudo iftop -i eth0 -t -s 86400 > bandwidth.log
```

---

## PART 13: PRACTICAL LABS

### Lab 1: Generate traffic and watch with iftop

```bash
# Terminal 1: Start iftop
sudo iftop -i eth0 -n -P

# Terminal 2: Generate traffic
curl -O http://speedtest.tele2.net/100MB.zip

# Watch bandwidth spike in iftop
```

### Lab 2: Find the process causing high bandwidth

```bash
# Terminal 1: Start nethogs
sudo nethogs eth0

# Terminal 2: Start a download
wget http://speedtest.tele2.net/100MB.zip

# Terminal 3: Do something else
ping -i 0.1 8.8.8.8

# nethogs shows both processes and their bandwidth usage
```

### Lab 3: Capture and analyze packets

```bash
# Terminal 1: Capture packets
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -c 50 -w capture.pcap

# Terminal 2: Generate some traffic
ping -c 10 google.com
curl google.com

# Terminal 1: Stop capture (after 50 packets)

# Analyze with tcpdump
tcpdump -r capture.pcap -n

# Analyze with more detail
tcpdump -r capture.pcap -n -v

# Filter in analysis
tcpdump -r capture.pcap -n icmp
```

### Lab 4: Set up vnstat for historical data

```bash
# Step 1: Install vnstat
sudo apt install vnstat -y

# Step 2: Create database
sudo vnstat -i eth0

# Step 3: Start service
sudo systemctl start vnstat
sudo systemctl enable vnstat

# Step 4: Wait a few minutes, then check
vnstat -d
vnstat -h

# Step 5: Generate some traffic
curl -O http://speedtest.tele2.net/10MB.zip

# Step 6: Check again after a minute
vnstat -d
```

---

## SUMMARY OF TOPIC 18

| Tool                 | Best for                 | Command                |
| -------------------- | ------------------------ | ---------------------- |
| `iftop`              | Bandwidth per connection | `sudo iftop -i eth0`   |
| `nethogs`            | Bandwidth per process    | `sudo nethogs eth0`    |
| `nload`              | Quick real-time graph    | `nload eth0`           |
| `vnstat`             | Historical usage         | `vnstat -d`            |
| `iptraf-ng`          | Detailed statistics      | `sudo iptraf-ng`       |
| `bmon`               | Multiple interfaces      | `bmon`                 |
| `ss`                 | Connection statistics    | `ss -s`                |
| `ip -s link`         | Interface errors/drops   | `ip -s link show eth0` |
| `tcpdump`            | Packet capture           | `sudo tcpdump -i eth0` |
| Prometheus + Grafana | Enterprise monitoring    | Set up Node Exporter   |

### The observability workflow

```
1. See current bandwidth: iftop or nload
       ↓
2. Find which program: nethogs
       ↓
3. Check for drops/errors: ip -s link
       ↓
4. Capture suspicious traffic: tcpdump
       ↓
5. Analyze capture: tcpdump -r
       ↓
6. Set up long-term monitoring: vnstat or Prometheus
```

# TOPIC 19: eBPF & XDP FOR NETWORKING

## Before we start

I will **never assume you know anything**. Every concept, every command, every number is explained from absolute zero.

Your mother is watching. I will not fail.

---

## PART 1: WHAT IS eBPF?

### The problem eBPF solves

The Linux kernel is a black box. To change how it handles packets, you normally have to:

1. Write kernel code in C
2. Compile it into the kernel
3. Reboot the entire system

**That's slow, dangerous, and requires deep kernel knowledge.**

**eBPF** (Extended Berkeley Packet Filter) lets you run **sandboxed programs inside the kernel** without recompiling or rebooting.

### Analogy: The kernel as a hotel

| Without eBPF                                       | With eBPF                                        |
| -------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------ |
| To change hotel policy, you must rebuild the hotel | You can add instructions at specific checkpoints |
| You need construction permits (kernel compilation) | You just write a note (eBPF program)             |
| Takes hours, requires shutdown                     | Instant, no downtime                             |

### What eBPF can do

| Use case                   | What eBPF does                                      |
| -------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------- |
| **Network filtering**      | Drop packets faster than iptables                   |
| **Load balancing**         | Distribute traffic at XDP (eXpress Data Path) level |
| **Tracing**                | Observe kernel functions in real time               |
| **Security**               | Detect intrusions at line rate                      |
| **Performance monitoring** | Track latency without overhead                      |

### eBPF vs Traditional Tools

| Traditional Tool | eBPF Alternative | Advantage                     |
| ---------------- | ---------------- | ----------------------------- |
| `tcpdump`        | `bpftool` + XDP  | Process packets before kernel |
| `iptables`       | XDP firewall     | 10-100x faster                |
| `perf`           | `bpftrace`       | More flexible, programmable   |
| `netstat`        | eBPF maps        | Custom metrics                |

---

## PART 2: WHAT IS XDP (eXpress Data Path)?

### The problem XDP solves

Normal network path:

```
NIC → Kernel network stack → iptables → Application
         ↑
    Lots of processing
```

When a packet arrives, the kernel does many things:

- Allocate memory
- Parse headers
- Route lookup
- Filtering (iptables)
- Then finally to application

**XDP** hooks into the **NIC driver directly** - BEFORE any kernel processing.

```
XDP path:
NIC → XDP program (your eBPF code) → DROP/PASS/REDIRECT
         ↑
    Minimal overhead, no memory allocation
```

### XDP actions

| Action         | What it does                                       |
| -------------- | -------------------------------------------------- |
| `XDP_DROP`     | Silently drop packet (fastest)                     |
| `XDP_PASS`     | Let packet continue to normal kernel stack         |
| `XDP_TX`       | Send packet back out same NIC (for load balancers) |
| `XDP_REDIRECT` | Send packet to another NIC or CPU                  |

### Performance comparison

| Technology                | Packets per second (approx) |
| ------------------------- | --------------------------- |
| iptables                  | 1-5 million                 |
| XDP (generic)             | 10-20 million               |
| XDP (driver-native)       | 20-50 million               |
| XDP with hardware offload | 100+ million                |

**XDP can be 10-100x faster than iptables.**

---

uname -r

# Install development tools

sudo apt update
sudo apt install -y clang llvm libbpf-dev linux-tools-common linux-tools-generic

# RHEL/Fedora
sudo dnf install -y clang llvm libbpf-devel kernel-devel bpftool
```

bpftool --version

# On Ubuntu, may need to install separately
sudo apt install linux-tools-$(uname -r)
```

### Install bpftrace (tracing language)

```bash

sudo apt install bpftrace

# RHEL/Fedora
sudo dnf install bpftrace
```

## PART 4: YOUR FIRST eBPF PROGRAM (XDP DROP)

### A simple XDP program that drops all packets

**File:** `xdp-drop.c`

```c
// Include eBPF helper functions
#include <linux/bpf.h>
#include <bpf/bpf_helpers.h>

// Define license (required)
SEC("xdp")
int xdp_drop_all(struct xdp_md *ctx) {
    // Return XDP_DROP - drop every packet
    return XDP_DROP;
}

// License must be GPL for eBPF helpers
char _license[] SEC("license") = "GPL";
```

### Compile the eBPF program

```bash
# Compile C to eBPF bytecode
clang -O2 -target bpf -c xdp-drop.c -o xdp-drop.o

# View the compiled eBPF instructions
bpftool prog dump xdp-drop.o
```

### Load and attach the program

```bash
# Attach to eth0 (WARNING: This will drop ALL traffic on eth0!)
sudo bpftool net attach xdpdrv name xdp_drop_all dev eth0

# Verify it's attached
sudo bpftool net list

# View statistics
sudo bpftool prog show
```

### Remove the program (VERY IMPORTANT)

```bash
# Detach from interface
sudo bpftool net detach xdpdrv dev eth0

# Verify it's gone
sudo bpftool net list
```

**WARNING:** Do NOT run `xdp-drop.c` on a remote server over SSH. You will lock yourself out immediately.

---

## PART 5: AN XDP FIREWALL (ALLOW SPECIFIC PORTS)

**File:** `xdp-firewall.c`

```c
#include <linux/bpf.h>
#include <linux/if_ether.h>
#include <linux/ip.h>
#include <linux/tcp.h>
#include <bpf/bpf_helpers.h>

// Map to store allowed port list (key: port, value: 1)
struct {
    __uint(type, BPF_MAP_TYPE_HASH);
    __uint(max_entries, 100);
    __type(key, __u16);
    __type(value, __u8);
} allowed_ports SEC(".maps");

SEC("xdp")
int xdp_firewall(struct xdp_md *ctx) {
    void *data_end = (void *)(long)ctx->data_end;
    void *data = (void *)(long)ctx->data;

    // Parse Ethernet header
    struct ethhdr *eth = data;
    if ((void *)(eth + 1) > data_end)
        return XDP_PASS;

    // Check if IPv4
    if (eth->h_proto != __constant_htons(ETH_P_IP))
        return XDP_PASS;

    // Parse IP header
    struct iphdr *ip = (void *)(eth + 1);
    if ((void *)(ip + 1) > data_end)
        return XDP_PASS;

    // Check if TCP
    if (ip->protocol != IPPROTO_TCP)
        return XDP_PASS;

    // Parse TCP header
    struct tcphdr *tcp = (void *)(ip + 1);
    if ((void *)(tcp + 1) > data_end)
        return XDP_PASS;

    // Get destination port (convert from network byte order)
    __u16 dest_port = __bpf_ntohs(tcp->dest);

    // Look up in allowed_ports map
    __u8 *allowed = bpf_map_lookup_elem(&allowed_ports, &dest_port);

    if (allowed) {
        // Port is allowed - pass to kernel
        return XDP_PASS;
    }

    // Port not allowed - drop
    return XDP_DROP;
}

char _license[] SEC("license") = "GPL";
```

### Compile and load with allowed ports

```bash
# Compile
clang -O2 -target bpf -c xdp-firewall.c -o xdp-firewall.o

# Load the program
sudo bpftool prog load xdp-firewall.o /sys/fs/bpf/xdp-firewall

# Add allowed port 22 (SSH) to the map
sudo bpftool map update pinned /sys/fs/bpf/xdp-firewall/allowed_ports key 22 value 1

# Add allowed port 80 (HTTP)
sudo bpftool map update pinned /sys/fs/bpf/xdp-firewall/allowed_ports key 80 value 1

# Add allowed port 443 (HTTPS)
sudo bpftool map update pinned /sys/fs/bpf/xdp-firewall/allowed_ports key 443 value 1

# Attach to interface
sudo bpftool net attach xdpdrv pinned /sys/fs/bpf/xdp-firewall dev eth0
```

---

## PART 6: XDP LOAD BALANCER (MAGLEV)

### What is Maglev?

Maglev is Google's load balancer. XDP can implement similar functionality at massive scale.

**File:** `xdp-lb.c`

```c
#include <linux/bpf.h>
#include <linux/if_ether.h>
#include <linux/ip.h>
#include <linux/tcp.h>
#include <bpf/bpf_helpers.h>

// Map of backend servers (key: hash, value: IP)
struct {
    __uint(type, BPF_MAP_TYPE_ARRAY);
    __uint(max_entries, 10);
    __type(key, __u32);
    __type(value, __u32);
} backends SEC(".maps");

SEC("xdp")
int xdp_load_balancer(struct xdp_md *ctx) {
    void *data_end = (void *)(long)ctx->data_end;
    void *data = (void *)(long)ctx->data;

    struct ethhdr *eth = data;
    if ((void *)(eth + 1) > data_end)
        return XDP_PASS;

    if (eth->h_proto != __constant_htons(ETH_P_IP))
        return XDP_PASS;

    struct iphdr *ip = (void *)(eth + 1);
    if ((void *)(ip + 1) > data_end)
        return XDP_PASS;

    // Simple hash based on source IP
    __u32 hash = ip->saddr;

    // Modulo to pick backend
    __u32 index = hash % 10;
    __u32 *backend_ip = bpf_map_lookup_elem(&backends, &index);

    if (backend_ip) {
        // Replace destination IP with backend IP
        ip->daddr = *backend_ip;

        // Fix checksum (simplified)
        ip->check = 0;

        // Send back out (XDP_TX)
        return XDP_TX;
    }

    return XDP_PASS;
}

char _license[] SEC("license") = "GPL";
```

---

## PART 7: bpftrace (TRACING FOR HUMANS)

### What is bpftrace?

`bpftrace` is a high-level language for eBPF tracing. You can write one-liners to trace kernel events.

### Install bpftrace

```bash
sudo apt install bpftrace   # Debian/Ubuntu
sudo dnf install bpftrace   # RHEL/Fedora
```

### Basic bpftrace examples

```bash
# Trace all execve() system calls (program execution)
sudo bpftrace -e 'tracepoint:syscalls:sys_enter_execve { printf("%s ran %s\n", comm, str(args->filename)); }'

# Trace TCP connections
sudo bpftrace -e 'kprobe:tcp_v4_connect { printf("Connection: %s -> %d.%d.%d.%d\n", comm, args[0], args[1], args[2], args[3]); }'

# Count packets by IP
sudo bpftrace -e 'kprobe:ip_rcv { @[ntop(args->daddr)] = count(); }'

# Trace ping (ICMP) packets
sudo bpftrace -e 'kprobe:icmp_rcv { printf("Ping from %d.%d.%d.%d\n", args[0], args[1], args[2], args[3]); }'

# Trace dropped packets
sudo bpftrace -e 'tracepoint:skb:kfree_skb { printf("Drop: %s\n", __data_loc(args->location)); }'
```

### bpftrace script example

**File:** `tcp-connections.bt`

```bpftrace
#!/usr/bin/env bpftrace

BEGIN {
    printf("Tracing TCP connections... Ctrl-C to stop\n");
}

kprobe:tcp_v4_connect {
    printf("PID %d (%s) connecting to %d.%d.%d.%d:%d\n",
        pid, comm,
        args[0], args[1], args[2], args[3],
        args[4]);
}

kprobe:tcp_v4_accept {
    printf("PID %d (%s) accepted connection from %d.%d.%d.%d:%d\n",
        pid, comm,
        args[0], args[1], args[2], args[3],
        args[4]);
}
```

Run it:

```bash
sudo bpftrace tcp-connections.bt
```

## PART 9: XDP TROUBLESHOOTING

### Problem 1: "Invalid argument" when loading XDP program

**Cause:** Kernel too old or driver doesn't support native XDP.

**Check:**

```bash
# Check kernel version
uname -r

# Check if driver supports XDP
ethtool -i eth0 | grep driver
# Intel ixgbe, i40e, mlx5 support XDP
# Virtual drivers (virtio_net) may have limited support

# Try generic XDP (slower but works anywhere)
sudo bpftool net attach xdpgen name xdp_drop_all dev eth0
```

### Problem 2: XDP program loaded but no effect

**Check:**

```bash
# Verify program is attached
sudo bpftool net list

# Check program statistics
sudo bpftool prog show

# Check if interface is up
ip link show eth0
```

### Problem 3: bpftrace: "Could not get readline"

```bash
# Install readline dev
sudo apt install libreadline-dev   # Debian/Ubuntu
```

## PART 10: PRACTICAL LABS (eBPF)

### Lab 1: XDP drop counter (safe - only drops packets you send)

```bash
# Step 1: Create the drop program
cat > xdp-drop.c << 'EOF'
#include <linux/bpf.h>
#include <bpf/bpf_helpers.h>

SEC("xdp")
int xdp_drop(struct xdp_md *ctx) {
    return XDP_DROP;
}

char _license[] SEC("license") = "GPL";
EOF

# Step 2: Compile
clang -O2 -target bpf -c xdp-drop.c -o xdp-drop.o

# Step 3: Load (using generic XDP - safer for testing)
sudo ip link set dev lo xdp obj xdp-drop.o sec xdp

# Step 4: Test (ping localhost - will fail!)
ping -c 3 127.0.0.1

# Step 5: Remove
sudo ip link set dev lo xdp off
```

### Lab 2: Trace all outgoing connections with bpftrace

```bash
# Trace TCP connects
sudo bpftrace -e 'kprobe:tcp_v4_connect { printf("%s connecting to %d.%d.%d.%d:%d\n", comm, args[0], args[1], args[2], args[3], args[4]); }'

# In another terminal, run:
curl google.com
```

### Lab 3: Monitor network latency

```bash
# Trace ping latency
sudo bpftrace -e 'kprobe:icmp_echo_send { @start[tid] = nsecs; } kretprobe:icmp_echo_send { @usecs = hist((nsecs - @start[tid]) / 1000); delete(@start[tid]); }'
```
