# TOPIC 20: NETWORK AUTOMATION WITH ANSIBLE

## PART 1: WHAT IS ANSIBLE?

### The problem Ansible solves

You have 50 servers. You need to:

- Change DNS servers on all of them
- Update firewall rules
- Configure network interfaces

**Without automation:** SSH into 50 servers, type the same commands 50 times. Errors happen. You forget one.

**With Ansible:** Write one playbook. Run it once. All 50 servers are configured identically.

### What is Ansible?

Ansible is an **automation tool** that:

- Connects to servers via SSH (no agent required)
- Executes tasks in order
- Ensures the desired state

### Ansible vs Other Tools

| Tool    | Requires agent? | Language    | Best for             |
| ------- | --------------- | ----------- | -------------------- |
| Ansible | No              | YAML        | Simple, agentless    |
| Puppet  | Yes             | Ruby        | Large enterprise     |
| Chef    | Yes             | Ruby        | Complex environments |
| Salt    | Optional        | YAML/Python | High-performance     |

### Why Ansible for networking?

| Network task                               | Ansible can do it |
| ------------------------------------------ | ----------------- |
| Configure /etc/network/interfaces          | Yes               |
| Add iptables rules                         | Yes               |
| Change sysctl network parameters           | Yes               |
| Restart networking services                | Yes               |
| Configure network switches (Cisco, Arista) | Yes               |

---

## PART 2: ANSIBLE CONCEPTS

### The architecture

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                    Ansible Control Node                     │
│                    (your laptop or a server)                │
│                                                             │
│  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────────────┐  │
│  │ Inventory   │  │ Playbook    │  │ Modules             │  │
│  │ (which      │  │ (what to    │  │ (how to do it)      │  │
│  │ servers)    │  │ do)         │  │                     │  │
│  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘  └─────────────────────┘  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                              │
                              │ SSH
                              ▼
┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐
│  Server 1   │  │  Server 2   │  │  Server 3   │  │  Server 4   │
│  (target)   │  │  (target)   │  │  (target)   │  │  (target)   │
└─────────────┘  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘
```

### Key terms

| Term             | Meaning                                                    |
| ---------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Control node** | Computer running Ansible (your laptop)                     |
| **Managed node** | Server being configured (target)                           |
| **Inventory**    | List of managed nodes                                      |
| **Playbook**     | YAML file with tasks to execute                            |
| **Task**         | Single unit of work (e.g., "install nginx")                |
| **Module**       | Tool that executes a task (e.g., `apt`, `copy`, `service`) |
| **Role**         | Reusable collection of tasks                               |
| **Fact**         | Information gathered from managed node (IP, OS, etc.)      |

---

## PART 3: INSTALLING ANSIBLE

### On the control node (your laptop/sysadmin workstation)

```bash
# Debian/Ubuntu
sudo apt update
sudo apt install ansible -y

# RHEL/CentOS/Fedora
sudo dnf install ansible -y

# Using pip (any Linux)
pip install --user ansible

# Verify
ansible --version
```

### Inventory file: telling Ansible which servers

**File:** `inventory.ini`

```ini
# Simple inventory
web1 ansible_host=192.168.1.10
web2 ansible_host=192.168.1.11
db1 ansible_host=192.168.1.20

# Grouped inventory
[webservers]
web1 ansible_host=192.168.1.10
web2 ansible_host=192.168.1.11

[databases]
db1 ansible_host=192.168.1.20

[all:vars]
ansible_user=root
ansible_ssh_private_key_file=~/.ssh/id_rsa
```

### Test connectivity

```bash
# Ping all servers
ansible all -i inventory.ini -m ping

# Run command on webservers
ansible webservers -i inventory.ini -m command -a "hostname"

# With inline inventory (no file)
ansible all -i "192.168.1.10,192.168.1.11," -m ping
```

---

## PART 4: NETWORK MODULES IN ANSIBLE

### Core network modules for Linux

| Module           | What it does                         |
| ---------------- | ------------------------------------ |
| `nmcli`          | Configure NetworkManager connections |
| `interface_file` | Manage /etc/network/interfaces       |
| `sysctl`         | Set kernel network parameters        |
| `iptables`       | Manage firewall rules                |
| `firewalld`      | Manage firewalld (RHEL)              |
| `hostname`       | Set system hostname                  |
| `hosts`          | Manage /etc/hosts file               |
| `resolvconf`     | Manage /etc/resolv.conf              |

### Network modules for network devices (switches/routers)

| Module         | For           |
| -------------- | ------------- |
| `ios_config`   | Cisco IOS     |
| `eos_config`   | Arista EOS    |
| `junos_config` | Juniper JunOS |
| `nxos_config`  | Cisco Nexus   |

---

## PART 5: YOUR FIRST NETWORK PLAYBOOK

### Example 1: Configure static IP on Ubuntu

**File:** `static-ip.yml`

```yaml
---
- name: Configure static IP on web servers
  hosts: webservers
  become: yes

  tasks:
    - name: Set static IP using Netplan
      copy:
        dest: /etc/netplan/01-netcfg.yaml
        content: |
          network:
            version: 2
            renderer: networkd
            ethernets:
              eth0:
                addresses:
                  - 192.168.1.100/24
                gateway4: 192.168.1.1
                nameservers:
                  addresses:
                    - 8.8.8.8
                    - 8.8.4.4

    - name: Apply Netplan configuration
      command: netplan apply

    - name: Verify IP address
      command: ip addr show eth0
      register: ip_output

    - name: Display IP
      debug:
        msg: "{{ ip_output.stdout_lines }}"
```

### Run the playbook

```bash
ansible-playbook -i inventory.ini static-ip.yml
```

### Example 2: Configure iptables firewall

**File:** `firewall.yml`

```yaml
---
- name: Configure firewall on all servers
  hosts: all
  become: yes

  tasks:
    - name: Flush existing iptables rules
      iptables:
        chain: INPUT
        policy: ACCEPT
        flush: yes

    - name: Set default policy to DROP
      iptables:
        chain: INPUT
        policy: DROP

    - name: Allow loopback
      iptables:
        chain: INPUT
        in_interface: lo
        jump: ACCEPT

    - name: Allow established connections
      iptables:
        chain: INPUT
        ctstate: ESTABLISHED,RELATED
        jump: ACCEPT

    - name: Allow SSH on webservers
      iptables:
        chain: INPUT
        destination_port: 22
        protocol: tcp
        jump: ACCEPT
      when: "'webservers' in group_names"

    - name: Allow HTTP on webservers
      iptables:
        chain: INPUT
        destination_port: 80
        protocol: tcp
        jump: ACCEPT
      when: "'webservers' in group_names"

    - name: Allow HTTPS on webservers
      iptables:
        chain: INPUT
        destination_port: 443
        protocol: tcp
        jump: ACCEPT
      when: "'webservers' in group_names"
```

### Example 3: Configure DNS servers

**File:** `dns.yml`

```yaml
---
- name: Configure DNS servers
  hosts: all
  become: yes

  tasks:
    - name: Set DNS using Netplan (Ubuntu 18.04+)
      block:
        - name: Read current Netplan
          slurp:
            src: /etc/netplan/01-netcfg.yaml
          register: netplan_content

        - name: Update DNS in Netplan
          copy:
            dest: /etc/netplan/01-netcfg.yaml
            content: |
              network:
                version: 2
                renderer: networkd
                ethernets:
                  eth0:
                    dhcp4: no
                    addresses:
                      - "{{ ansible_default_ipv4.address }}/{{ ansible_default_ipv4.netmask }}"
                    gateway4: "{{ ansible_default_ipv4.gateway }}"
                    nameservers:
                      addresses:
                        - 1.1.1.1
                        - 9.9.9.9
          notify: restart networking

    - name: Alternative - set /etc/resolv.conf (if no Netplan)
      copy:
        dest: /etc/resolv.conf
        content: |
          nameserver 1.1.1.1
          nameserver 9.9.9.9
      when: not ansible_os_family == "Debian" or ansible_distribution_version is version('18.04', '<')

  handlers:
    - name: restart networking
      command: netplan apply
```

---

## PART 6: VARIABLES AND TEMPLATES

### Using variables for network configuration

**File:** `group_vars/webservers.yml`

```yaml
---
# Variables for webservers group
network:
  interface: eth0
  ip_address: 192.168.1.100
  netmask: 24
  gateway: 192.168.1.1
  dns_servers:
    - 8.8.8.8
    - 8.8.4.4
firewall:
  allowed_ports:
    - 22
    - 80
    - 443
```

**File:** `templates/netplan.j2` (Jinja2 template)

```yaml
network:
  version: 2
  renderer: networkd
  ethernets:
    {{ network.interface }}:
      addresses:
        - {{ network.ip_address }}/{{ network.netmask }}
      gateway4: {{ network.gateway }}
      nameservers:
        addresses:
{% for dns in network.dns_servers %}
          - {{ dns }}
{% endfor %}
```

**File:** `configure-network.yml`

```yaml
---
- name: Configure network using variables
  hosts: webservers
  become: yes

  tasks:
    - name: Render Netplan from template
      template:
        src: templates/netplan.j2
        dest: /etc/netplan/01-netcfg.yaml
      notify: apply netplan

    - name: Configure firewall based on variables
      iptables:
        chain: INPUT
        destination_port: "{{ item }}"
        protocol: tcp
        jump: ACCEPT
      loop: "{{ firewall.allowed_ports }}"

  handlers:
    - name: apply netplan
      command: netplan apply
```

---

## PART 7: ADVANCED NETWORK PLAYBOOKS

### Example: Bonding configuration

**File:** `bonding.yml`

```yaml
---
- name: Configure network bonding
  hosts: bond_servers
  become: yes

  tasks:
    - name: Install bonding tools
      apt:
        name: ifenslave
        state: present
      when: ansible_os_family == "Debian"

    - name: Load bonding kernel module
      modprobe:
        name: bonding
        state: present

    - name: Create bond interface configuration (Netplan)
      copy:
        dest: /etc/netplan/02-bonding.yaml
        content: |
          network:
            version: 2
            renderer: networkd
            bonds:
              bond0:
                interfaces:
                  - eth0
                  - eth1
                parameters:
                  mode: active-backup
                  primary: eth0
                  mii-monitor-interval: 100
            ethernets:
              eth0: {}
              eth1: {}
            bonds:
              bond0:
                addresses:
                  - "{{ bond_ip }}/24"
                gateway4: "{{ bond_gateway }}"
                nameservers:
                  addresses:
                    - 8.8.8.8
                    - 8.8.4.4
      notify: apply netplan

  handlers:
    - name: apply netplan
      command: netplan apply
```

### Example: VLAN configuration

**File:** `vlans.yml`

```yaml
---
- name: Configure VLANs
  hosts: vlan_servers
  become: yes

  vars:
    vlans:
      - id: 10
        name: management
        ip: 192.168.10.10
      - id: 20
        name: storage
        ip: 192.168.20.10
      - id: 30
        name: vmotion
        ip: 192.168.30.10

  tasks:
    - name: Load 8021q kernel module
      modprobe:
        name: 8021q
        state: present

    - name: Create VLAN interfaces
      copy:
        dest: "/etc/netplan/03-vlans.yaml"
        content: |
          network:
            version: 2
            renderer: networkd
            ethernets:
              eth0: {}
            vlans:
{% for vlan in vlans %}
              eth0.{{ vlan.id }}:
                id: {{ vlan.id }}
                link: eth0
                addresses:
                  - "{{ vlan.ip }}/24"
{% endfor %}
      notify: apply netplan

  handlers:
    - name: apply netplan
      command: netplan apply
```

### Example: Bridge for VMs

**File:** `bridge.yml`

```yaml
---
- name: Configure bridge for VMs
  hosts: virtualization_hosts
  become: yes

  tasks:
    - name: Create bridge configuration
      copy:
        dest: /etc/netplan/04-bridge.yaml
        content: |
          network:
            version: 2
            renderer: networkd
            ethernets:
              eth0: {}
            bridges:
              br0:
                interfaces:
                  - eth0
                addresses:
                  - "{{ bridge_ip }}/24"
                gateway4: "{{ bridge_gateway }}"
                parameters:
                  stp: true
                  forward-delay: 4
                nameservers:
                  addresses:
                    - 8.8.8.8
      notify: apply netplan

  handlers:
    - name: apply netplan
      command: netplan apply
```

## PART 8: ANSIBLE ROLES FOR NETWORKING

### What is a role?

A role is a **reusable collection** of tasks, variables, templates, and handlers.

### Directory structure for a network role

```
roles/
└── network/
    ├── tasks/
    │   └── main.yml
    ├── handlers/
    │   └── main.yml
    ├── templates/
    │   └── netplan.j2
    ├── vars/
    │   └── main.yml
    ├── defaults/
    │   └── main.yml
    └── meta/
        └── main.yml
```

### Example role: `network` role

**File:** `roles/network/tasks/main.yml`

```yaml
---
- name: Load kernel modules
  modprobe:
    name: "{{ item }}"
    state: present
  loop:
    - bonding
    - 8021q
  when: kernel_modules_enabled

- name: Configure network interfaces
  template:
    src: netplan.j2
    dest: /etc/netplan/01-netcfg.yaml
  notify: apply netplan

- name: Configure sysctl network parameters
  sysctl:
    name: "{{ item.name }}"
    value: "{{ item.value }}"
    state: present
    reload: yes
  loop:
    - { name: net.ipv4.ip_forward, value: 1 }
    - { name: net.ipv4.conf.all.rp_filter, value: 0 }
  when: ip_forward_enabled

- name: Configure firewall
  include_tasks: firewall.yml
  when: configure_firewall
```

**File:** `roles/network/handlers/main.yml`

```yaml
---
- name: apply netplan
  command: netplan apply
```

**File:** `roles/network/defaults/main.yml`

```yaml
---
# Default variables (can be overridden)
kernel_modules_enabled: true
ip_forward_enabled: false
configure_firewall: true

network:
  interface: eth0
  ip_method: dhcp
```

### Using the role in a playbook

**File:** `site.yml`

```yaml
---
- name: Configure all web servers
  hosts: webservers
  become: yes

  roles:
    - role: network
      vars:
        network:
          interface: eth0
          ip_method: static
          ip_address: 192.168.1.100
          netmask: 24
          gateway: 192.168.1.1
```

- name: Gather facts

## PART 10: COMBINING eBPF AND ANSIBLE

### Deploying XDP programs with Ansible

**File:** `deploy-xdp.yml`

```yaml
---
- name: Deploy XDP firewall across all servers
  hosts: all
  become: yes

  tasks:
    - name: Install build dependencies
      apt:
        name:
          - clang
          - llvm
          - libbpf-dev
          - linux-headers-{{ ansible_kernel }}
        state: present
      when: ansible_os_family == "Debian"

    - name: Copy XDP source file
      copy:
        src: files/xdp-firewall.c
        dest: /usr/local/src/xdp-firewall.c
        mode: "0644"

    - name: Compile XDP program
      command:
        cmd: clang -O2 -target bpf -c /usr/local/src/xdp-firewall.c -o /usr/local/src/xdp-firewall.o
        creates: /usr/local/src/xdp-firewall.o

    - name: Load XDP program
      command:
        cmd: bpftool prog load /usr/local/src/xdp-firewall.o /sys/fs/bpf/xdp-firewall
        creates: /sys/fs/bpf/xdp-firewall

    - name: Attach XDP to interface
      command:
        cmd: bpftool net attach xdpdrv pinned /sys/fs/bpf/xdp-firewall dev {{ network_interface }}

    - name: Add allowed ports to XDP map
      command:
        cmd: "bpftool map update pinned /sys/fs/bpf/xdp-firewall/allowed_ports key {{ item }} value 1"
      loop: "{{ allowed_ports }}"

    - name: Create systemd service to reload XDP on boot
      copy:
        dest: /etc/systemd/system/xdp-firewall.service
        content: |
          [Unit]
          Description=XDP Firewall
          After=network.target

          [Service]
          Type=oneshot
          ExecStart=/usr/local/bin/xdp-load.sh
          RemainAfterExit=yes

          [Install]
          WantedBy=multi-user.target

    - name: Create load script
      copy:
        dest: /usr/local/bin/xdp-load.sh
        mode: "0755"
        content: |
          #!/bin/bash
          bpftool prog load /usr/local/src/xdp-firewall.o /sys/fs/bpf/xdp-firewall
          bpftool net attach xdpdrv pinned /sys/fs/bpf/xdp-firewall dev {{ network_interface }}
          {% for port in allowed_ports %}
          bpftool map update pinned /sys/fs/bpf/xdp-firewall/allowed_ports key {{ port }} value 1
          {% endfor %}

    - name: Enable XDP service
      systemd:
        name: xdp-firewall
        enabled: yes
        state: started
```

## PART 11: PRACTICAL LABS (ANSIBLE)

### Lab 1: Set up Ansible control node and manage localhost

```bash
# Step 1: Install Ansible
sudo apt install ansible -y

# Step 2: Create inventory file
cat > inventory.ini << EOF
localhost ansible_connection=local
EOF

# Step 3: Test ping
ansible localhost -i inventory.ini -m ping

# Step 4: Run a command
ansible localhost -i inventory.ini -m command -a "ip addr show"

# Step 5: Run a playbook on localhost
cat > test.yml << EOF
---
- name: Test playbook
  hosts: localhost
  tasks:
    - name: Show network interfaces
      debug:
        msg: "{{ ansible_facts.interfaces }}"

    - name: Show default IPv4
      debug:
        msg: "{{ ansible_default_ipv4.address }}"
EOF

ansible-playbook -i inventory.ini test.yml
```

### Lab 2: Configure DNS using Ansible

```bash
cat > set-dns.yml << EOF
---
- name: Configure DNS
  hosts: localhost
  become: yes

  tasks:
    - name: Backup current resolv.conf
      copy:
        src: /etc/resolv.conf
        dest: /etc/resolv.conf.backup
        remote_src: yes

    - name: Set new DNS servers
      copy:
        dest: /etc/resolv.conf
        content: |
          nameserver 1.1.1.1
          nameserver 9.9.9.9

    - name: Verify DNS resolution
      command: nslookup google.com
      register: dns_test
      ignore_errors: yes

    - name: Show DNS test result
      debug:
        msg: "{{ dns_test.stdout_lines }}"
EOF

ansible-playbook -i inventory.ini set-dns.yml
```

### Lab 3: Add an iptables rule using Ansible

```bash
cat > add-rule.yml << EOF
---
- name: Add firewall rule
  hosts: localhost
  become: yes

  tasks:
    - name: Check if rule exists
      command: iptables -C INPUT -p tcp --dport 8080 -j ACCEPT
      register: rule_exists
      ignore_errors: yes

    - name: Add rule if not exists
      iptables:
        chain: INPUT
        destination_port: 8080
        protocol: tcp
        jump: ACCEPT
      when: rule_exists is failed

    - name: Show current iptables rules
      command: iptables -L INPUT -n
      register: iptables_output

    - name: Display rules
      debug:
        msg: "{{ iptables_output.stdout_lines }}"
EOF

ansible-playbook -i inventory.ini add-rule.yml
```

## SUMMARY OF TOPIC 19 & 20

### eBPF/XDP Summary

| Concept  | One-sentence explanation                                               |
| -------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| eBPF     | Run sandboxed programs inside the Linux kernel without recompiling     |
| XDP      | Hook into NIC driver for packet processing before kernel network stack |
| XDP_DROP | Silently drop packet (fastest action)                                  |
| XDP_PASS | Send packet to normal kernel network stack                             |
| XDP_TX   | Send packet back out same interface (load balancing)                   |
| bpftool  | Manage eBPF programs and maps                                          |
| bpftrace | High-level tracing language for eBPF                                   |

### Ansible Summary

| Concept   | One-sentence explanation                                 |
| --------- | -------------------------------------------------------- |
| Ansible   | Automation tool that configures servers over SSH         |
| Inventory | List of servers to manage                                |
| Playbook  | YAML file with tasks to execute                          |
| Module    | Tool that does specific work (apt, copy, iptables, etc.) |
| Role      | Reusable collection of tasks, variables, templates       |
| Template  | Jinja2 file with variables (generates config files)      |

### When to use each tool

| Task                                      | Tool           |
| ----------------------------------------- | -------------- |
| Extreme high-performance packet filtering | XDP/eBPF       |
| Tracing kernel events (debugging)         | bpftrace       |
| Load balancing at 100 Gbps                | XDP            |
| Configuring 50 servers identically        | Ansible        |
| Deploying XDP programs to many servers    | Ansible + eBPF |
| Infrastructure as Code                    | Ansible        |

# TOPIC 21: SSH & SECURE REMOTE ACCESS

## What is SSH?

SSH stands for **Secure Shell**. It lets you log into a remote computer over the network and run commands on it — as if you were sitting at its keyboard.

Real-world analogy: SSH is a **locked phone call** between two computers. Only they can hear each other. Nobody in the middle can listen or change the conversation.

Before SSH, people used `telnet` — which sent everything including passwords in **plain text** over the wire. Anyone watching the network could read your password. SSH replaced telnet in 1995 and encrypts everything.

## How SSH works (simplified)

1. Your computer (client) connects to the remote computer (server) on **port 22**
2. They exchange cryptographic keys to prove who they are
3. An encrypted tunnel is created
4. All commands and output flow through this tunnel — encrypted

## Installing SSH

```bash
# On the server (to accept connections):
sudo apt install openssh-server   # Debian/Ubuntu
sudo dnf install openssh-server   # RHEL/CentOS/Fedora

# Start and enable the service:
sudo systemctl start sshd
sudo systemctl enable sshd
sudo systemctl status sshd

# On the client (to connect to servers):
sudo apt install openssh-client   # usually pre-installed
```

## Basic SSH usage

```bash
# Connect to a remote server:
ssh username@192.168.1.100

# Connect on a non-default port:
ssh -p 2222 username@192.168.1.100

# Run a single command without opening a shell:
ssh username@192.168.1.100 "df -h"

# Connect with verbose output (useful for debugging):
ssh -v username@192.168.1.100
```

## Key-based authentication (the RIGHT way)

Passwords can be guessed. SSH keys cannot. A key pair has two parts:

- **Private key**: stays on YOUR computer. Never share this.
- **Public key**: goes on the server. It's safe to share.

Think of it like a **padlock and key**. You give the server the padlock (public key). You keep the key (private key). The server locks data with your padlock. Only you can unlock it.

```bash
# Step 1: Generate a key pair on YOUR computer:
ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "your-email@example.com"
# You'll be asked for a file location (press Enter for default: ~/.ssh/id_ed25519)
# You'll be asked for a passphrase (recommended — protects your private key)

# Step 2: Copy your public key to the server:
ssh-copy-id username@192.168.1.100
# This appends your public key to ~/.ssh/authorized_keys on the server

# Step 3: Test it — you should NOT be asked for a password:
ssh username@192.168.1.100
```

## The ~/.ssh/config file

Instead of typing long SSH commands every time, you can create shortcuts:

```bash
# Edit or create your SSH config:
nano ~/.ssh/config
```

Example config:

```
Host myserver
    HostName 192.168.1.100
    User douglas
    Port 22
    IdentityFile ~/.ssh/id_ed25519

Host jumpbox
    HostName 10.0.0.5
    User admin
    Port 2222
    IdentityFile ~/.ssh/jump_key
```

Now you can just type:

```bash
ssh myserver       # equivalent to: ssh -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 -p 22 douglas@192.168.1.100
```

## SSH port forwarding (tunneling)

Port forwarding lets you route traffic through an SSH tunnel. Three types:

### Local port forwarding (-L)

Makes a remote service accessible on your local machine.

```bash
# Syntax: ssh -L local_port:destination_host:destination_port user@ssh_server
ssh -L 8080:localhost:80 user@192.168.1.100
# Now visit http://localhost:8080 on your machine — it connects to port 80 on 192.168.1.100
```

Real use case: Access a web app running on a remote server that has no public IP.

### Remote port forwarding (-R)

Makes a local service accessible on the remote server.

```bash
# Syntax: ssh -R remote_port:localhost:local_port user@ssh_server
ssh -R 9090:localhost:3000 user@192.168.1.100
# Now someone on 192.168.1.100 can visit localhost:9090 — it connects to YOUR port 3000
```

Real use case: Expose a local dev server to a colleague on a remote machine.

### Dynamic port forwarding (-D) — SOCKS proxy

Turns SSH into a SOCKS proxy.

```bash
ssh -D 1080 user@192.168.1.100
# Now configure your browser to use SOCKS5 proxy at localhost:1080
# All browser traffic routes through the remote server
```

## Copying files over SSH

```bash
# scp — secure copy (like cp but over SSH)
scp localfile.txt user@192.168.1.100:/remote/path/
scp user@192.168.1.100:/remote/file.txt ./local/

# Copy entire directory:
scp -r mydir/ user@192.168.1.100:/remote/path/

# rsync — smarter file sync (only transfers changes)
rsync -avz mydir/ user@192.168.1.100:/remote/path/
# -a = archive (preserve permissions, timestamps)
# -v = verbose
# -z = compress during transfer
```

## Hardening SSH (security best practices)

```bash
sudo nano /etc/ssh/sshd_config
```

Key settings to change:

```
Port 2222                    # Change from default 22 (obscurity, not security, but reduces noise)
PermitRootLogin no           # Never allow direct root SSH login
PasswordAuthentication no    # Force key-based auth only (after you've set up keys!)
MaxAuthTries 3               # Limit login attempts
AllowUsers douglas admin     # Whitelist specific users
```

After editing, restart SSH:

```bash
sudo systemctl restart sshd
```

## SSH agent — avoid typing passphrase repeatedly

```bash
# Start the agent:
eval "$(ssh-agent -s)"

# Add your key:
ssh-add ~/.ssh/id_ed25519
# Enter passphrase once — now you won't be asked again in this session

# List loaded keys:
ssh-add -l
```

---

# TOPIC 22: TLS/SSL & PKI BASICS

## What is TLS?

TLS stands for **Transport Layer Security**. SSL is its older name (Secure Sockets Layer). You'll hear both terms, but TLS is what modern systems actually use.

TLS is the technology behind the padlock icon in your browser. When you visit `https://example.com`, TLS encrypts everything between your browser and the server.

Real-world analogy: Imagine mailing a letter in a **locked transparent box**. Anyone can see the box exists, but nobody can read the letter inside or change it.

## Why does TLS exist?

Without TLS:

- Someone between you and the server (your ISP, a hacker on the same WiFi) can read all data
- They can also **modify** data — inject ads, change passwords, redirect logins
- You can't verify the server is who it claims to be

With TLS:

- Data is **encrypted** — unreadable to observers
- Data has **integrity** — cannot be modified without detection
- Server is **authenticated** — you know you're talking to the real server, not an impersonator

## Certificates and the chain of trust

A TLS certificate is like a **passport**. It contains:

- The server's domain name (e.g., `example.com`)
- The server's public key
- Who issued the certificate (the CA — Certificate Authority)
- Expiry date
- A digital signature from the CA

Your browser/OS comes pre-loaded with a list of **trusted CAs** (like DigiCert, Let's Encrypt, Sectigo). When a server presents its certificate, your browser checks:

1. Is this certificate signed by a trusted CA?
2. Does the domain name match?
3. Is it expired?

If all checks pass — connection is trusted.

## Certificate types

| Type                           | What it covers               | Example                          |
| ------------------------------ | ---------------------------- | -------------------------------- |
| Single domain                  | One exact domain             | `example.com`                    |
| Wildcard                       | All subdomains               | `*.example.com`                  |
| SAN (Subject Alternative Name) | Multiple domains             | `example.com`, `api.example.com` |
| Self-signed                    | Signed by yourself, not a CA | Dev/internal use only            |

## The openssl command — your TLS Swiss Army knife

```bash
# View a certificate from a live server:
openssl s_client -connect example.com:443 -showcerts

# View certificate details from a file:
openssl x509 -in cert.pem -text -noout

# Check expiry date only:
openssl x509 -in cert.pem -noout -dates

# Check a live server's certificate expiry:
echo | openssl s_client -connect example.com:443 2>/dev/null | openssl x509 -noout -dates

# Generate a private key:
openssl genrsa -out private.key 4096

# Generate a Certificate Signing Request (CSR):
openssl req -new -key private.key -out request.csr
# You'll be asked to fill in country, organization, domain, etc.

# Generate a self-signed certificate (for testing):
openssl req -x509 -newkey rsa:4096 -keyout key.pem -out cert.pem -days 365 -nodes
# -nodes = no passphrase on the key (useful for servers)

# Verify that a key matches a certificate:
openssl x509 -noout -modulus -in cert.pem | md5sum
openssl rsa -noout -modulus -in private.key | md5sum
# Both md5sums must match — if they do, the key and cert belong together
```

## Let's Encrypt — free automated TLS

Let's Encrypt is a free, automated Certificate Authority. Use `certbot` to get certificates:

```bash
sudo apt install certbot python3-certbot-nginx

# Get a certificate for your domain (nginx example):
sudo certbot --nginx -d example.com -d www.example.com

# Get certificate only (no auto-config):
sudo certbot certonly --standalone -d example.com

# Auto-renewal (certbot sets this up automatically via cron/systemd):
sudo certbot renew --dry-run

# List certificates:
sudo certbot certificates
```

Certificates are stored in `/etc/letsencrypt/live/example.com/`:

- `fullchain.pem` — your certificate + intermediate chain
- `privkey.pem` — your private key

## TLS in common tools

```bash
# curl — verify a server's certificate (default behavior):
curl https://example.com

# Skip verification (ONLY for testing — insecure!):
curl -k https://example.com

# Specify a CA bundle:
curl --cacert /path/to/ca-bundle.pem https://internal.example.com

# wget:
wget --no-check-certificate https://example.com  # insecure, testing only
```

## Common TLS errors and what they mean

| Error                          | Meaning                                            | Fix                                 |
| ------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------- |
| `SSL_ERROR_RX_RECORD_TOO_LONG` | Server not speaking TLS (plain HTTP on HTTPS port) | Check server config                 |
| `certificate verify failed`    | Certificate not trusted (self-signed or expired)   | Add CA to trust store or renew cert |
| `hostname mismatch`            | Cert doesn't cover this domain                     | Get correct cert                    |
| `certificate expired`          | Past the `notAfter` date                           | Renew certificate                   |

---

# TOPIC 23: nftables — THE MODERN FIREWALL

## What is nftables?

`nftables` is the modern replacement for `iptables`. It has been the default firewall framework in:

- RHEL/CentOS/Rocky Linux 8+
- Debian 10+
- Ubuntu 20.04+ (uses nftables backend even for `ufw`)

The core concepts are the same as iptables (chains, rules, matches, actions), but the syntax is cleaner and more consistent.

## nftables vs iptables — key differences

| Feature                          | iptables                 | nftables                 |
| -------------------------------- | ------------------------ | ------------------------ |
| Separate tools for IPv4/IPv6     | `iptables` / `ip6tables` | One tool: `nft`          |
| Separate tools for ARP filtering | `arptables`              | One tool: `nft`          |
| Rule syntax                      | Verbose, inconsistent    | Clean, consistent        |
| Atomic rule updates              | No (flush + reload)      | Yes                      |
| Performance                      | OK                       | Better (JIT compilation) |

## Basic nftables concepts

- **Table**: Top-level container. Specifies the address family (ip, ip6, inet, arp, bridge)
- **Chain**: List of rules. Has a type (filter, nat, route), hook (input, output, forward), and priority
- **Rule**: The actual match + action (accept, drop, reject, log, etc.)

## Essential nft commands

```bash
# List all current rules:
nft list ruleset

# List a specific table:
nft list table inet filter

# Flush (delete) everything in a table:
nft flush ruleset

# Save rules to file:
nft list ruleset > /etc/nftables.conf

# Load rules from file:
nft -f /etc/nftables.conf
```

## Creating a basic firewall with nftables

```bash
# The nftables config file:
sudo nano /etc/nftables.conf
```

A complete basic firewall:

```
#!/usr/sbin/nft -f
flush ruleset

table inet filter {
    chain input {
        type filter hook input priority 0; policy drop;

        # Allow established/related connections:
        ct state established,related accept

        # Allow loopback interface:
        iif "lo" accept

        # Allow ICMP (ping):
        ip protocol icmp accept
        ip6 nexthdr icmpv6 accept

        # Allow SSH:
        tcp dport 22 accept

        # Allow HTTP and HTTPS:
        tcp dport { 80, 443 } accept

        # Log and drop everything else:
        log prefix "nftables-dropped: " drop
    }

    chain forward {
        type filter hook forward priority 0; policy drop;
    }

    chain output {
        type filter hook output priority 0; policy accept;
    }
}
```

Apply it:

```bash
sudo nft -f /etc/nftables.conf
sudo systemctl enable nftables
sudo systemctl start nftables
```

## NAT with nftables

```bash
table ip nat {
    chain prerouting {
        type nat hook prerouting priority -100;

        # Port forwarding: redirect incoming port 8080 to internal 192.168.1.10:80
        tcp dport 8080 dnat to 192.168.1.10:80
    }

    chain postrouting {
        type nat hook postrouting priority 100;

        # Masquerade (SNAT) for outgoing traffic:
        oifname "eth0" masquerade
    }
}
```

## Adding/deleting rules dynamically

```bash
# Add a rule to block an IP:
nft add rule inet filter input ip saddr 1.2.3.4 drop

# Add a rule to a specific position (before rule handle 5):
nft insert rule inet filter input position 5 tcp dport 8080 accept

# Get rule handles (needed to delete specific rules):
nft -a list ruleset

# Delete a rule by handle number:
nft delete rule inet filter input handle 8

# Rate limiting — allow max 10 SSH connections per minute:
nft add rule inet filter input tcp dport 22 ct state new limit rate 10/minute accept
```

## UFW (Uncomplicated Firewall) — friendlier interface

On Ubuntu/Debian, `ufw` provides a simpler interface:

```bash
sudo ufw enable
sudo ufw status verbose

sudo ufw allow ssh
sudo ufw allow 80/tcp
sudo ufw allow 443/tcp
sudo ufw deny 23/tcp          # block telnet

sudo ufw delete allow 80/tcp
sudo ufw reset                # reset to defaults
```

---

# TOPIC 24: NETWORK FILE SYSTEMS (NFS & SMB)

## What is a Network File System?

A Network File System lets you **mount a directory from another computer** as if it were a local folder. Applications don't need to know the files are remote.

Two main protocols:

- **NFS** (Network File System) — native Linux/Unix. Fast, simple, used in Linux clusters and Kubernetes
- **SMB/CIFS** (Server Message Block) — Windows-originated. Used to access Windows shares from Linux

## NFS — setup and usage

### NFS Server (the machine sharing files)

```bash
# Install NFS server:
sudo apt install nfs-kernel-server

# Create a directory to share:
sudo mkdir -p /srv/nfs/shared
sudo chown nobody:nogroup /srv/nfs/shared

# Edit the exports file — define what to share:
sudo nano /etc/exports
```

Example `/etc/exports`:

```
# Format: directory  client(options)
/srv/nfs/shared    192.168.1.0/24(rw,sync,no_subtree_check)
/srv/nfs/readonly  *(ro,sync,no_subtree_check)
```

Common export options:
| Option | Meaning |
|--------|---------|
| `rw` | Read and write |
| `ro` | Read only |
| `sync` | Write to disk before replying (safe) |
| `async` | Reply before writing (faster but risky) |
| `no_subtree_check` | Skip subtree checking (recommended) |
| `no_root_squash` | Allow remote root to be root locally (risky) |
| `root_squash` | Map remote root to `nobody` (default, safer) |

```bash
# Apply exports without restarting:
sudo exportfs -ra

# Show current exports:
sudo exportfs -v

# Start and enable NFS:
sudo systemctl start nfs-kernel-server
sudo systemctl enable nfs-kernel-server
```

### NFS Client (the machine mounting files)

```bash
# Install NFS client tools:
sudo apt install nfs-common

# Show what a server is sharing:
showmount -e 192.168.1.100

# Mount temporarily:
sudo mount -t nfs 192.168.1.100:/srv/nfs/shared /mnt/remote

# Verify mount:
df -h /mnt/remote
ls /mnt/remote

# Unmount:
sudo umount /mnt/remote
```

### Permanent NFS mount via /etc/fstab

```bash
sudo nano /etc/fstab
```

Add this line:

```
192.168.1.100:/srv/nfs/shared  /mnt/remote  nfs  defaults,_netdev  0  0
```

`_netdev` tells the system to wait for the network before mounting.

```bash
# Test the fstab entry:
sudo mount -a
```

## SMB/CIFS — mounting Windows shares

```bash
# Install CIFS utilities:
sudo apt install cifs-utils

# Mount a Windows share:
sudo mount -t cifs //192.168.1.50/SharedFolder /mnt/windows \
  -o username=Administrator,password=secret,domain=WORKGROUP

# Better — store credentials in a file (don't put passwords on command line!):
sudo nano /etc/samba/credentials
```

Credentials file content:

```
username=Administrator
password=secret
domain=WORKGROUP
```

```bash
sudo chmod 600 /etc/samba/credentials

# Mount using credentials file:
sudo mount -t cifs //192.168.1.50/SharedFolder /mnt/windows \
  -o credentials=/etc/samba/credentials

# Permanent mount in /etc/fstab:
//192.168.1.50/SharedFolder  /mnt/windows  cifs  credentials=/etc/samba/credentials,_netdev  0  0
```

## Samba — sharing Linux files to Windows

```bash
# Install Samba:
sudo apt install samba

# Create a share directory:
sudo mkdir -p /srv/samba/public
sudo chmod 0777 /srv/samba/public

# Configure Samba:
sudo nano /etc/samba/smb.conf
```

Add to the end of `smb.conf`:

```ini
[public]
   comment = Public Samba Share
   path = /srv/samba/public
   browseable = yes
   writable = yes
   guest ok = yes
   create mask = 0664
   directory mask = 0775
```

```bash
# Test config syntax:
testparm

# Add a Samba user (must be an existing Linux user):
sudo smbpasswd -a douglas

# Restart Samba:
sudo systemctl restart smbd nmbd
sudo systemctl enable smbd
```

# TOPIC 25: IPv6

## Why IPv6?

IPv4 has ~4.3 billion addresses. The world ran out. IPv6 was created to replace it with a practically unlimited address space: **340 undecillion addresses** (3.4 × 10³⁸).

We are in a **dual-stack** transition period — most systems use both IPv4 and IPv6 simultaneously.

## IPv6 address format

IPv6 addresses are **128 bits** long, written as 8 groups of 4 hex digits separated by colons:

```
2001:0db8:85a3:0000:0000:8a2e:0370:7334
```

## IPv6 shortening rules

1. **Leading zeros** in each group can be removed: `0db8` → `db8`, `0000` → `0`
2. **One group of consecutive all-zero groups** can be replaced with `::` (once only)

```
2001:0db8:85a3:0000:0000:8a2e:0370:7334
→ 2001:db8:85a3:0:0:8a2e:370:7334        # remove leading zeros
→ 2001:db8:85a3::8a2e:370:7334           # compress the two zero groups
```

## Special IPv6 addresses

| Address     | Meaning                                  | IPv4 equivalent                |
| ----------- | ---------------------------------------- | ------------------------------ |
| `::1`       | Loopback (this machine)                  | `127.0.0.1`                    |
| `::`        | Unspecified                              | `0.0.0.0`                      |
| `fe80::/10` | Link-local (auto-configured, not routed) | `169.254.0.0/16`               |
| `fc00::/7`  | Unique local (private network)           | `10.0.0.0/8`, `192.168.0.0/16` |
| `2000::/3`  | Global unicast (public internet)         | Public IPs                     |
| `ff00::/8`  | Multicast                                | `224.0.0.0/4`                  |

## IPv6 prefix lengths

IPv6 uses CIDR notation like IPv4:

- `/128` — single host (like `/32` in IPv4)
- `/64` — standard subnet (half the bits for the network, half for hosts)
- `/48` — typical allocation to an organization
- `/32` — ISP prefix

A `/64` subnet has `2^64` = 18.4 quintillion host addresses. You'll never run out.

## Linux commands for IPv6

```bash
# Show IPv6 addresses on all interfaces:
ip -6 addr show

# Show IPv6 routes:
ip -6 route show

# Ping IPv6 (use ping6 or ping -6):
ping6 ::1
ping -6 2001:db8::1

# SSH to an IPv6 host (wrap address in brackets):
ssh user@[2001:db8::1]
ssh -6 user@ipv6host.example.com

# curl with IPv6:
curl -6 https://example.com

# traceroute for IPv6:
traceroute6 2001:4860:4860::8888    # Google's IPv6 DNS
```

## Neighbor Discovery Protocol (NDP)

NDP is IPv6's replacement for ARP. Uses ICMPv6.

```bash
# Show the IPv6 neighbor cache (like ARP cache for IPv4):
ip -6 neigh show

# Flush the neighbor cache:
sudo ip -6 neigh flush all
```

## IPv6 address auto-configuration (SLAAC)

With SLAAC (Stateless Address Autoconfiguration), a host can **automatically generate its own IPv6 address** without DHCP:

1. Host generates a link-local address (`fe80::`) using its MAC address
2. Router sends a **Router Advertisement** with the network prefix
3. Host combines the prefix + its interface identifier = global IPv6 address

```bash
# Check if SLAAC addresses were assigned:
ip -6 addr show | grep "scope global"
# Look for addresses with "dynamic" or "mngtmpaddr" flags
```

## Configuring static IPv6 with netplan (Ubuntu)

```yaml
# /etc/netplan/01-netcfg.yaml
network:
  version: 2
  ethernets:
    eth0:
      addresses:
        - 192.168.1.10/24 # IPv4
        - 2001:db8::10/64 # IPv6 static
      routes:
        - to: default
          via: 192.168.1.1
        - to: ::/0
          via: 2001:db8::1 # IPv6 default gateway
      nameservers:
        addresses:
          - 8.8.8.8
          - 2001:4860:4860::8888 # Google IPv6 DNS
```

```bash
sudo netplan apply
```

## Firewall for IPv6 — ip6tables / nftables

```bash
# ip6tables (separate from iptables):
sudo ip6tables -L -n -v

# Allow SSH over IPv6:
sudo ip6tables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 22 -j ACCEPT

# With nftables, IPv4 and IPv6 share one ruleset using "inet" family (see Topic 23)
```

# TOPIC 26: LOAD BALANCING WITH HAPROXY & NGINX

## What is load balancing?

When many users hit your service, one server can't handle the load. A **load balancer** sits in front of multiple backend servers and distributes incoming requests among them.

Real-world analogy: A hotel with 10 reception desks. The doorman (load balancer) tells each arriving guest which desk to go to so no single desk is overwhelmed.

## Layer 4 vs Layer 7 load balancing

|            | Layer 4 (Transport) | Layer 7 (Application)       |
| ---------- | ------------------- | --------------------------- |
| Sees       | IP + port only      | HTTP headers, URL, cookies  |
| Speed      | Very fast           | Slightly slower (more work) |
| Routing by | IP/port             | URL path, hostname, cookies |
| Example    | TCP proxy           | Nginx, HAProxy (HTTP mode)  |

## HAProxy — high-performance load balancer

```bash
sudo apt install haproxy
```

### Basic HAProxy config

```bash
sudo nano /etc/haproxy/haproxy.cfg
```

```
global
    log /dev/log local0
    maxconn 4096

defaults
    log     global
    mode    http
    option  httplog
    option  dontlognull
    timeout connect 5000ms
    timeout client  50000ms
    timeout server  50000ms

# Frontend: what HAProxy listens on
frontend web_frontend
    bind *:80
    bind *:443 ssl crt /etc/ssl/certs/example.pem
    redirect scheme https if !{ ssl_fc }
    default_backend web_servers

# Backend: the real servers
backend web_servers
    balance roundrobin
    option httpchk GET /health
    server server1 192.168.1.101:8080 check
    server server2 192.168.1.102:8080 check
    server server3 192.168.1.103:8080 check backup
```

```bash
# Test config syntax:
haproxy -c -f /etc/haproxy/haproxy.cfg

sudo systemctl restart haproxy
sudo systemctl enable haproxy
```

### HAProxy load balancing algorithms

| Algorithm    | Description                                   | Best for                          |
| ------------ | --------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------- |
| `roundrobin` | Each server in turn                           | Equal servers                     |
| `leastconn`  | Server with fewest active connections         | Long-lived connections (DB, LDAP) |
| `source`     | Hash of client IP (same client → same server) | Session persistence               |
| `random`     | Random selection                              | Simple distribution               |
| `uri`        | Hash of URI                                   | Cache servers                     |

### HAProxy stats page

```
frontend stats
    bind *:8404
    stats enable
    stats uri /stats
    stats refresh 10s
    stats auth admin:password
```

Visit `http://yourserver:8404/stats` to see real-time stats.

## Nginx as a load balancer

```bash
sudo apt install nginx
```

```nginx
# /etc/nginx/conf.d/loadbalancer.conf

upstream backend_servers {
    least_conn;                        # load balancing algorithm
    server 192.168.1.101:8080;
    server 192.168.1.102:8080;
    server 192.168.1.103:8080 backup;  # only used if others fail
    keepalive 32;                      # keep connections open
}

server {
    listen 80;
    server_name example.com;

    location / {
        proxy_pass http://backend_servers;
        proxy_set_header Host $host;
        proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
    }

    # Health check endpoint:
    location /health {
        access_log off;
        return 200 "OK\n";
    }
}
```

```bash
sudo nginx -t      # test config
sudo systemctl reload nginx
```

### Nginx algorithms

```nginx
upstream backend {
    # round-robin (default — no keyword needed)
    server 192.168.1.101;
    server 192.168.1.102;
}

upstream backend_leastconn {
    least_conn;
    server 192.168.1.101;
    server 192.168.1.102;
}

upstream backend_iphash {
    ip_hash;      # same client IP always goes to same server
    server 192.168.1.101;
    server 192.168.1.102;
}
```

# TOPIC 27: PACKET CAPTURE WITH tcpdump & tshark

## Why capture packets?

Packet capture lets you **watch raw network traffic** like an X-ray for your network. Use it when:

- A service isn't connecting but you don't know why
- You want to verify encryption is working
- You're debugging DNS, TLS handshakes, or connection resets
- You need to see what a program is actually sending

## tcpdump — the essential packet capture tool

```bash
# Install:
sudo apt install tcpdump

# Basic — capture on interface eth0, print to screen:
sudo tcpdump -i eth0

# Capture on any interface:
sudo tcpdump -i any

# Show IP addresses (not hostnames) and port numbers (not service names):
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -nn

# Capture and save to a file (pcap format — can open in Wireshark):
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -w capture.pcap

# Read from a saved file:
sudo tcpdump -r capture.pcap

# Limit number of packets captured:
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -c 100
```

## tcpdump filters — the most important part

Filters let you capture ONLY the traffic you care about.

```bash
# Filter by host:
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 host 192.168.1.100

# Filter by source or destination:
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 src 192.168.1.100
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 dst 192.168.1.100

# Filter by port:
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 port 443
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 port 80 or port 443

# Filter by protocol:
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 icmp
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 tcp
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 udp

# Filter by network (CIDR):
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 net 192.168.1.0/24

# Combine filters with and/or/not:
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 host 192.168.1.100 and port 443
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 not port 22   # everything except SSH

# See HTTP GET requests:
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -nn -A 'tcp port 80 and (((ip[2:2] - ((ip[0]&0xf)<<2)) - ((tcp[12]&0xf0)>>2)) != 0)'

# Practical: capture DNS queries:
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -nn port 53

# Practical: capture TLS handshakes:
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -nn 'tcp port 443 and (tcp[((tcp[12]&0xf0)>>2)] = 0x16)'
```

## Reading tcpdump output

```
14:23:45.123456 IP 192.168.1.5.52341 > 8.8.8.8.53: UDP, length 32
│              │  │ source IP.port   │ dest IP.port  │ proto   │ size
│              └── timestamp
└── packet number
```

For TCP, you'll also see flags:

- `S` = SYN (starting connection)
- `A` = ACK (acknowledging)
- `F` = FIN (closing connection)
- `R` = RST (reset — connection abruptly terminated)
- `P` = PSH (push data now)

A normal TCP 3-way handshake looks like:

```
client > server: S (SYN)
server > client: S. (SYN-ACK)
client > server: . (ACK)
```

## tshark — Wireshark on the command line

`tshark` is the CLI version of Wireshark. More powerful filtering than tcpdump.

```bash
sudo apt install tshark

# Capture and display — similar to tcpdump:
sudo tshark -i eth0

# Capture to file:
sudo tshark -i eth0 -w capture.pcap

# Read with display filter (Wireshark filter syntax):
tshark -r capture.pcap -Y "http.request.method == GET"
tshark -r capture.pcap -Y "dns"
tshark -r capture.pcap -Y "tcp.flags.reset == 1"    # find resets
tshark -r capture.pcap -Y "tls.handshake.type == 1" # TLS ClientHellos

# Extract specific fields:
tshark -r capture.pcap -T fields -e ip.src -e ip.dst -e tcp.dstport -Y "tcp.flags.syn == 1"

# Show HTTP requests and status codes:
tshark -r capture.pcap -T fields -e http.request.method -e http.request.uri -e http.response.code

# Follow a TCP stream (reassemble the conversation):
tshark -r capture.pcap -q -z follow,tcp,ascii,0
```

## Practical debugging examples

```bash
# Is my DNS working? Watch DNS queries and responses:
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -nn port 53 -A

# Is the server sending RSTs? (connection refused or firewall drop):
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -nn 'tcp[13] & 4 != 0'

# Capture a failed TLS handshake:
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -nn port 443 -w tls_debug.pcap
# Then open in Wireshark for detailed analysis

# Monitor HTTP traffic to a specific server:
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -nn -A host 192.168.1.100 and port 80
```

# TOPIC 28: VPN WITH WIREGUARD

## What is a VPN?

VPN stands for **Virtual Private Network**. It creates an **encrypted tunnel** between two computers (or networks) over the internet, as if they were directly connected on the same local network.

Real-world analogy: Imagine digging a private underground tunnel between two buildings in different cities. People inside the tunnel can communicate privately, even though the cities are far apart.

Use cases:

- Securely connect to a remote office network
- Access resources behind a firewall from anywhere
- Connect Kubernetes clusters or cloud VMs to your local machine
- Privacy on untrusted networks (public WiFi)

## Why WireGuard?

WireGuard is the **modern VPN protocol**. Compared to OpenVPN and IPsec:

- Much **simpler** to configure (a few lines vs hundreds)
- Much **faster** (designed from scratch for performance)
- Much **smaller** codebase (~4000 lines vs 600,000 for OpenVPN)
- Built into the **Linux kernel** since 5.6 (March 2020)

## Installing WireGuard

```bash
# Ubuntu/Debian:
sudo apt install wireguard

# RHEL/CentOS/Fedora:
sudo dnf install wireguard-tools

# Verify:
wg --version
```

## WireGuard concepts

- **Peer**: Any WireGuard endpoint (not client/server — every peer is equal)
- **Interface**: A virtual network interface (e.g., `wg0`)
- **Private key**: Secret key for your peer. Never share.
- **Public key**: Derived from the private key. Share with others.
- **Endpoint**: The peer's public IP:port (how to reach them)
- **AllowedIPs**: Which IP ranges to route through the tunnel

## Setting up WireGuard — server side

```bash
# Step 1: Generate key pair on the server:
wg genkey | sudo tee /etc/wireguard/server_private.key | \
  wg pubkey | sudo tee /etc/wireguard/server_public.key
sudo chmod 600 /etc/wireguard/server_private.key

# Step 2: Create the server config:
sudo nano /etc/wireguard/wg0.conf
```

Server config (`/etc/wireguard/wg0.conf`):

```ini
[Interface]
Address = 10.0.0.1/24
ListenPort = 51820
PrivateKey = <paste contents of server_private.key>

# Enable IP forwarding and NAT (for clients to access internet):
PostUp = iptables -A FORWARD -i %i -j ACCEPT; iptables -t nat -A POSTROUTING -o eth0 -j MASQUERADE
PostDown = iptables -D FORWARD -i %i -j ACCEPT; iptables -t nat -D POSTROUTING -o eth0 -j MASQUERADE

[Peer]
# Client 1 — Douglas's laptop
PublicKey = <client_public_key>
AllowedIPs = 10.0.0.2/32   # only this specific IP for this peer
```

```bash
# Step 3: Enable IP forwarding on the server:
echo "net.ipv4.ip_forward = 1" | sudo tee -a /etc/sysctl.conf
sudo sysctl -p

# Step 4: Start WireGuard:
sudo systemctl enable wg-quick@wg0
sudo systemctl start wg-quick@wg0

# Check status:
sudo wg show
```

## Setting up WireGuard — client side

```bash
# Step 1: Generate key pair on the client:
wg genkey | sudo tee /etc/wireguard/client_private.key | \
  wg pubkey | sudo tee /etc/wireguard/client_public.key

# Step 2: Create client config:
sudo nano /etc/wireguard/wg0.conf
```

Client config:

```ini
[Interface]
Address = 10.0.0.2/24
PrivateKey = <paste contents of client_private.key>
DNS = 1.1.1.1

[Peer]
# The server
PublicKey = <server_public_key>
Endpoint = <server_public_ip>:51820
AllowedIPs = 0.0.0.0/0, ::/0   # route ALL traffic through the VPN
                                 # Use 10.0.0.0/24 to split-tunnel (VPN only for private IPs)
PersistentKeepalive = 25        # send keepalive every 25s (needed if behind NAT)
```

```bash
# Connect:
sudo wg-quick up wg0

# Disconnect:
sudo wg-quick down wg0

# Enable auto-start:
sudo systemctl enable wg-quick@wg0

# Check connection status:
sudo wg show
```

## WireGuard monitoring commands

```bash
# Show all peers and their last handshake:
sudo wg show

# Show data transferred per peer:
sudo wg show wg0 transfer

# Show interface details:
ip addr show wg0

# Watch live:
watch -n 1 sudo wg show
```

## Split tunneling

Split tunneling means: only send some traffic through the VPN, not everything.

```ini
# In client [Peer] section:
AllowedIPs = 10.0.0.0/24     # only route the VPN subnet — local internet goes direct
```

vs:

```ini
AllowedIPs = 0.0.0.0/0       # route everything through VPN
```

---

# TOPIC 29: NETWORK TROUBLESHOOTING METHODOLOGY

## The golden rule

**Never guess. Diagnose systematically.** Work through the OSI model from Layer 1 up (or from the symptom down), ruling out each layer before moving to the next.

## The 7-step methodology

```
Step 1: Define the problem precisely
Step 2: Layer 1 — Physical
Step 3: Layer 2 — Data Link (ARP, MAC)
Step 4: Layer 3 — Network (IP, routing)
Step 5: Layer 4 — Transport (ports, firewall)
Step 6: Layer 5-7 — Application (DNS, TLS, app config)
Step 7: Document the fix
```

## Step 1 — Define the problem

Before touching any command, answer:

- **What** exactly is failing? (error message, symptom)
- **When** did it start? (after a change? after a reboot?)
- **Who** is affected? (one user, one server, everyone?)
- **What changed** recently? (deployment, config change, update?)

## Step 2 — Layer 1: Physical

```bash
# Is the network interface up?
ip link show
# Look for "state UP" vs "state DOWN"

# Bring an interface up:
sudo ip link set eth0 up

# Check for errors (collisions, drops, errors):
ip -s link show eth0
# Look at RX errors, TX errors, dropped packets

# Check cable/hardware (on physical machines):
ethtool eth0
# Look for: "Link detected: yes" and speed/duplex
```

## Step 3 — Layer 2: Data Link

```bash
# Can you see other devices on the local network?
arp -n
ip neigh show

# Is your IP assigned?
ip addr show

# Is there an IP conflict? (two devices with same IP)
sudo arping -I eth0 192.168.1.100
# If you see replies from DIFFERENT MAC addresses — IP conflict!
```

## Step 4 — Layer 3: Network

```bash
# Can you ping your own IP?
ping -c 3 $(ip addr show eth0 | awk '/inet / {print $2}' | cut -d/ -f1)

# Can you ping the gateway?
ip route show default      # find your gateway
ping -c 3 192.168.1.1      # ping it

# Can you ping a public IP (bypasses DNS)?
ping -c 3 8.8.8.8

# Trace the route:
traceroute 8.8.8.8
# Look for where it stops — that's where the problem is

# Check routing table:
ip route show
```

Decision tree:

```
Can ping self?
  NO  → IP not assigned (check: ip addr, dhclient)
  YES → Can ping gateway?
          NO  → Local network issue (ARP, cable, VLAN, firewall on router)
          YES → Can ping 8.8.8.8?
                  NO  → Routing issue (check: ip route, NAT, ISP)
                  YES → Can ping google.com?
                          NO  → DNS issue (Topic 7)
                          YES → Application-level problem
```

## Step 5 — Layer 4: Transport

```bash
# Is the port open on the server?
ss -tlnp | grep :80          # is port 80 listening?
ss -tlnp | grep :443

# Can you reach the port from the client?
nc -zv 192.168.1.100 80      # -z = just test, -v = verbose
# "Connection succeeded" = port is reachable
# "Connection refused" = port not listening
# Timeout = firewall blocking

# Is the firewall blocking it?
# On the server:
sudo nft list ruleset | grep -A5 "chain input"
sudo iptables -L -n -v

# Test from client with tcpdump watching server:
# Terminal 1 (server):
sudo tcpdump -i eth0 -nn port 80
# Terminal 2 (client):
nc -zv server_ip 80
# If server sees SYN but no packets arrive from client → firewall on client
# If server sends SYN-ACK but client gets nothing → firewall on server or in-between
```

## Step 6 — Layer 7: Application

```bash
# Is DNS resolving correctly?
dig example.com
nslookup example.com
# Check both the answer AND which server responded

# Is TLS working?
openssl s_client -connect example.com:443 -servername example.com
# Look for: "Verify return code: 0 (ok)"

# Is the application listening and responding?
curl -v http://192.168.1.100/    # -v shows headers and connection details
wget -O- http://192.168.1.100/

# Check application logs:
sudo journalctl -u nginx --since "10 minutes ago"
sudo tail -f /var/log/nginx/error.log
sudo tail -f /var/log/syslog

# Is something using too many connections?
ss -s                            # socket statistics summary
lsof -i :80                      # what process owns port 80
```

## Quick reference — the right tool for the problem

| Symptom                   | First command                        | Second command                    |
| ------------------------- | ------------------------------------ | --------------------------------- |
| No network at all         | `ip link show`                       | `ip addr show`                    |
| Can't reach gateway       | `arp -n`                             | `ping <gateway>`                  |
| Can ping IP, not hostname | `dig hostname`                       | `cat /etc/resolv.conf`            |
| Connection refused        | `ss -tlnp \| grep <port>`            | `nft list ruleset`                |
| Connection times out      | `sudo tcpdump -i any port <N>`       | `traceroute <host>`               |
| Slow network              | `iperf3 -c server`                   | `ip -s link show`                 |
| High packet loss          | `ping -c 100 <host>`                 | `mtr <host>`                      |
| SSL error                 | `openssl s_client -connect host:443` | `openssl x509 -in cert.pem -text` |

## Tools quick reference

```bash
# mtr — combines ping + traceroute, updates live:
mtr 8.8.8.8

# iperf3 — measure bandwidth:
# Server: iperf3 -s
# Client: iperf3 -c server_ip

# nmap — port scan (use only on systems you own or have permission):
nmap -sV -p 1-1000 192.168.1.100    # scan first 1000 ports
nmap -sn 192.168.1.0/24             # ping sweep (find live hosts)

# strace — see system calls including network calls:
strace -e trace=network curl http://example.com

# lsof — list open files/connections by a process:
lsof -i                   # all network connections
lsof -i :80               # who is using port 80
lsof -i @192.168.1.100    # connections to a specific IP

# netstat (older alternative to ss):
netstat -tlnp             # listening TCP ports
netstat -an | grep ESTABLISHED   # active connections
```
